    1
    Foundations

    What Python Actually Is

    ▾

    The one-paragraph truth this whole book rests on. A computer's processor only executes machine code — patterns of bits that open and close transistors. Humans cannot think in bits, so we write source code in a programming language, and a translator converts it into something the machine can run. Python is a high-level, interpreted, dynamically typed, garbage-collected language: its interpreter reads your source, compiles it to an intermediate bytecode, and executes that bytecode step by step on a virtual machine. This chapter unpacks every word of that paragraph — slowly, visually, and completely — because every mystery you will ever meet in data work (why loops are slow, why NumPy is fast, why versions matter, why environments exist) traces back to this execution model.

    🗺️ How to read this chapterEach concept below follows the book's teaching pattern: What → Why → Intuition → Analogy → Visual → Syntax → Smallest example → Execution trace → More examples → Mistakes → Debugging → Best practices → Data-Science link. Chapter-level interview questions, exercises, mini-challenge, and summary close the chapter. Don't skim — this mental model pays rent in every later chapter.

    1.1 Programming: exact instructions for a literal machine

    What. Programming is the act of writing exact, unambiguous, ordered instructions that a machine follows deterministically — same instructions, same starting state, same result, every time. A program is a text file (or collection of files) containing those instructions. Running a program means the machine reads the instructions and performs them.

    Why it exists. Computer hardware is generic: a CPU only knows a few hundred primitive operations (add two numbers, move bytes between memory locations, compare values, jump to another instruction). Hardware cannot "average customer ages" or "recognize cats" — it can only grind through primitives billions of times per second. Software — programs — is what converts that raw generic speed into specific, useful behavior without rewiring a single circuit. The same laptop becomes a spreadsheet, a video game, or a neural-network trainer depending purely on which program it runs.

    Intuition. Imagine hiring the world's fastest intern — one who executes ten billion simple actions per second — but who has zero common sense, zero memory of intent, and zero tolerance for ambiguity. Tell this intern "fetch the red folder" and they freeze: which shelf? what is red, precisely? what does fetch mean — bring here, or open? Programming is learning to speak to this intern: breaking every desire into steps so small and so precise that even a mindless executor gets them right. The computer does precisely what you wrote — never what you meant. Every bug you will ever meet is a gap between those two things.

    Real-world analogy: the recipe and the robot cook. A human recipe says "sauté the onions until soft." A human cook fills in the gaps (which pan? how much oil? what heat? what does 'soft' mean?). A robot cook needs: take pan P (mass 800 g) → place on burner 2 → set burner to 1,800 W → pour 15 ml oil → wait until oil temperature ≥ 160 °C → add 200 g diced onion (5 mm cubes) → stir every 20 s → stop when 90% of pieces deform under 2 N pressure. That exhausting level of explicitness is programming. High-level languages like Python are pre-agreed shorthand so you can write closer to "sauté until soft" while the toolchain expands it into robot-grade steps.

    Figure 1.1 — From human intent to switching transistors
    🧠 Human intent: "average customer age"
    ↓ expressed in a programming language
    📝 Source code (human-readable text)
    ↓ translated by compiler / interpreter
    🧩 Bytecode or machine code (machine-friendly numbers)
    ↓ executed by CPU
    ⚡ Transistors switch → result appears

    Smallest possible example. The smallest meaningful program has three parts — input, transformation, output:

    the smallest honest program 
    age = 30          # input/state: remember the value 30 under the name 'age'
double = age * 2  # transformation: compute something new
print(double)     # output: show it to the human
    60

    Step-by-step execution. Line 1: the interpreter creates the integer object 30 in memory and binds the name age to it (Chapter 3 explains this binding deeply). Line 2: it looks up age (finds 30), multiplies by 2 (creates 60), binds the name double. Line 3: it calls the builtin print function, which converts 60 to the characters 6, 0 and writes them to standard output (your screen), plus a newline. Nothing magical — three explicit steps, executed top to bottom.

    More examples — programs are everywhere, at every scale:

    • A 3-line script that renames 2,000 downloaded files — a program.
    • A Jupyter notebook exploring a dataset with plots — a program (a stateful, cell-ordered one — §1.9).
    • The Pandas library itself (~500,000 lines of Python/C/Cython) — a program other programs build on.
    • A production ML pipeline retraining nightly on fresh data — a program with scheduling, logging, and monitoring wrapped around it.
    ⚠️ Common mistake #1: "the computer should know what I mean"Beginners write average = total / count and are angry when count is 0 ("it should just know there's no data!"). The machine knows nothing. Defensive, explicit code — if count == 0: … else: … — is not bureaucracy; it is the job. Chapters 9 and 14 turn this into reflex.
    ⚠️ Common mistake #2: confusing the program with its outputprint(60) and 60 look identical on screen, but one is an instruction that produces text and the other is a value sitting in memory. Mixing up "showing" vs "computing" causes half of beginner confusion (Chapter 2 and 11 return to this).

    Debugging lens. When a program misbehaves, never ask "why is the computer wrong?" — ask "what did I actually tell it to do?" Read your code as the literal intern would: execute each line by hand on paper with a concrete input, tracking every variable's value. This technique — hand-tracing — solves an astonishing fraction of bugs and is the seed of Chapter 22's full workflow.

    Best practice. Start every program by writing, in plain words (a comment or a notebook cell), the three answers: what goes in? what comes out? what are the steps in between? If you can't state them in English, you can't state them in Python yet.

    📊 Data-Science connection. A data analysis is a program: raw data in → cleaning/transformation steps → findings out. The analysis-as-program viewpoint is why professionals write analyses as re-runnable scripts (same input → same output) instead of point-and-click steps nobody can reproduce. Chapters 15, 25 and the Projects build this habit into muscle memory.

    1.2 Programming languages: levels of abstraction

    What. A programming language is a formal notation — vocabulary plus grammar rules — for writing programs, together with a translator that converts that notation into machine-executable form. Languages sit on a spectrum from low-level (close to hardware: machine code, assembly) to high-level (close to human thought: Python, R, SQL).

    Why levels exist. There is a fundamental trade-off: machine-efficiency vs human-efficiency. Low-level code runs fast and controls hardware precisely, but expressing "average these ages" takes dozens of lines managing registers and memory addresses. High-level code expresses it in one line, at the cost of translation overhead. No single level is "best" — systems combine them (and Python's data stack is the perfect example: you write easy Python; NumPy's internals are hand-tuned C and Fortran).

    Intuition + analogy. Think of giving directions. Machine code is turn-by-turn wheel rotations ("rotate left axle 4.2 turns…"). Assembly is street-by-street ("turn left on Main…"). Python is intent-level ("take me to the airport, avoid tolls") — with a very competent driver (the interpreter + libraries) filling in the route. Higher level = less control over the exact route, vastly less effort per trip.

    Visual: the same task at three levels. Task: add two numbers and print the result.

    LevelWhat it looks likeWho manages memory/registers?
    Machine code (lowest)B8 1E 00 … — raw bytes the CPU decodesYou (everything is manual)
    AssemblyMOV EAX, 30 / ADD EAX, 12 / … — one line ≈ one CPU instructionYou (registers, addresses)
    C (mid)int t = 30 + 12; printf("%d", t);You (types, memory layout)
    Python (high)print(30 + 12)The interpreter (types, memory, cleanup all automatic)

    Why this matters for you. Python deliberately buys human speed (fast to write, read, and change) with machine speed (each operation does more hidden work). For data science this trade is overwhelmingly worth it: analyst time costs far more than CPU time, datasets are processed by C-speed kernels anyway (Chapter 24), and most programs wait on data loading, not computation. The skill is knowing where the boundary lies — which this book teaches explicitly rather than leaving as folklore.

    💡 Key vocabulary (used all book long)Syntax = grammar rules (violations → SyntaxError). Semantics = meaning of correct code (what it does). Standard library = modules shipped with Python (csv, json, math…). Third-party package = code others wrote that you install (numpy, pandas…). Runtime = everything present while your program executes.

    1.3 Compiled vs interpreted: when translation happens

    What. Every high-level program must eventually become machine code. When and how that translation happens defines the spectrum. In a compiled language (C, Rust, Go), a compiler translates the entire program to machine code before it ever runs, producing a standalone binary (e.g., .exe). In an interpreted language (Python, JavaScript, R), an interpreter translates and executes the program while it runs — typically line/block by block, with no separate build step visible to you.

    Why two approaches exist. Compilation front-loads translation cost for maximum execution speed and early error detection, at the price of a slow edit→compile→run feedback loop and platform-specific binaries. Interpretation buys a fast, flexible feedback loop (edit→run instantly; same code runs anywhere an interpreter exists) at the price of per-execution translation overhead. Data science — exploratory, iterative, "try ten things before lunch" — overwhelmingly favors the interpreted loop.

    Analogy: the translated book vs the live interpreter. A compiled program is a book translated to your language before you arrive: translation took months, but now you read at full speed. An interpreted program is a speech with a live interpreter whispering in your ear: no up-front wait, you can interrupt and ask questions (the REPL!), but every sentence passes through a middleman. Python adds a twist (§1.6): it quickly prepares notes (bytecode) before the live interpretation — a hybrid most modern languages share.

    Figure 1.2 — The two pipelines
    Compiled (C)
    source.c
    ↓ compiler (you run it)
    program.exe (machine code)
    ↓ OS runs directly
    ⚡ CPU executes
    Interpreted (Python)
    prog.py (source)
    ↓ interpreter reads it
    bytecode (auto, hidden)
    ↓ virtual machine loop
    ⚡ actions executed
    AspectCompiled (C, Rust, Go)Interpreted (Python, R, JS)
    TranslationWhole program → machine code before runningTranslated + executed during running
    Artifact you shipBinary executable (per OS/CPU)Source code itself (+ cached bytecode)
    Execution speedFast (direct CPU instructions)Slower per step (translation overhead)
    Feedback loopEdit → compile (seconds–minutes) → runEdit → run (instant); interactive REPL
    PortabilityRecompile per platformRun anywhere an interpreter exists
    Error discoveryMany errors caught at compile timeType errors surface at runtime (mitigate: tests + hints, Ch. 23)
    DS roleEngines (NumPy/Pandas cores are C/Cython/Fortran)Thinking layer: analysis, modeling, glue, orchestration

    Smallest examples. Compiled (C) — two visible steps: gcc prog.c -o prog then ./prog. Interpreted (Python) — one step: python prog.py. That missing build step is the entire day-to-day difference, and it compounds: data scientists re-run analyses hundreds of times a day.

    ⚠️ Common mistake: "interpreted means no compilation at all"Wrong — and interviewers probe exactly this. CPython always compiles your source to bytecode first (§1.6); "interpreted" means you never run a compiler manually and the final execution interprets bytecode on a VM. Java and C# live in the same hybrid middle (source → bytecode → VM/JIT). Pure line-by-line interpretation without any bytecode is rare today.
    ✅ Data-Science connectionThe interpreted loop is why exploration is joyful (tweak → re-run in a second), and compilation is why the fast parts are fast (your one-line df.groupby(...).mean() drops into precompiled C loops over millions of rows). Chapter 24 makes this division of labor quantitative.

    1.4 Python: the language, its philosophy, its versions

    What. Python is a high-level, interpreted, dynamically typed, garbage-collected programming language created by Guido van Rossum (first release 1991) and developed openly ever since. "Python" properly names three related things: the language specification (grammar + meaning), the reference interpreter program you run, and the ecosystem (standard library + community packages). Context tells you which is meant.

    Why it exists / design philosophy. Python optimizes for readability and developer productivity: code should read as close to English as a formal notation allows, there should be one obvious way to do things, and complexity should be hidden until needed. This is written down as the Zen of Python (import this) — "Readability counts", "Explicit is better than implicit", "Simple is better than complex". These aren't slogans; they explain real decisions: indentation-defined blocks (no brace noise), significant whitespace enforcing visual structure, batteries-included standard library, and errors that say what happened in words.

    the Zen of Python (run it!) 
    import this
    The Zen of Python, by Tim Peters Beautiful is better than ugly. Explicit is better than implicit. Simple is better than complex. ... (19 aphorisms total) Namespaces are one honking great idea -- let's do more of those!

    Python 2 vs 3 — the one historical fact worth knowing. Python 3 (2008) fixed deep design mistakes (notably Unicode text handling) in ways that broke backward compatibility with Python 2. The community split for a decade; Python 2 reached end-of-life in January 2020. Today "Python" unambiguously means Python 3. If you ever inherit Python 2 code (print x without parentheses is the tell), migrate it — do not learn Python 2.

    Versions within Python 3 — why they matter. Minor releases arrive yearly and add syntax and standard-library features your code may depend on. An interviewer asking "which Python do you use?" is checking professional awareness. Use 3.11+ (3.11/3.12/3.13) for this book.

    VersionLandmark additions (examples you'll meet)
    3.8Walrus :=, f"{x=}" debug syntax, positional-only /
    3.9dict | dict merge, builtin generics (list[int] in hints)
    3.10match/case structural matching, better error messages begin
    3.1110–60% faster interpreter, tomllib, exception groups, Self typing
    3.12Cleaner f-strings (nested quotes!), per-interpreter GIL groundwork, improved errors
    3.13Experimental free-threaded (no-GIL) build, JIT groundwork, new REPL
    know your interpreter 
    import sys
print(sys.version)        # full version string
print(sys.executable)     # EXACT python binary running this code
print(sys.version_info)   # structured: major=3, minor=12, ...
    3.12.4 (main, ...) [GCC ...] /usr/local/bin/python sys.version_info(major=3, minor=12, micro=4, releaselevel='final', serial=0)
    ⚠️ Common mistake: tutorial version mismatchCopy-pasting match/case code into Python 3.9 fails with a baffling SyntaxError — the grammar literally doesn't exist there. Whenever a "correct-looking" snippet fails at parse time, check the version first (python --version). Pin your project's Python version (Chapter 17) so teammates run identical grammars.

    1.5 Interpreter, runtime, CPython, and the other Pythons

    What — four terms, precisely separated:

    • Python, the language: the specification — what for, def, is mean. A document and a test suite, not a program.
    • Python, the interpreter: the program you invoke (python / python3) that reads .py files and carries them out. It contains a parser, a compiler (source→bytecode), and a virtual machine.
    • The runtime: everything present while your program runs — the interpreter process, loaded modules, the memory manager, the garbage collector, the call stack. "Runtime error" means: the code parsed fine, but something failed during execution (bad value, missing file, zero division). This is why tracebacks say where execution died, not where the typo is.
    • CPython: the standard, official, reference implementation of Python — written in C, downloaded from python.org, installed by default almost everywhere. When anyone says "install Python", they mean CPython. This book assumes CPython unless stated.

    Why multiple implementations exist. Because the language (specification) is separate from any implementation (program that runs it), different teams built Python-runners optimized for different niches:

    ImplementationWritten in / runs onWhy it exists
    CPython (the default)CReference behavior; biggest ecosystem; what you use
    PyPyPython (+ JIT compiler)Much faster pure-Python loops via just-in-time compilation; alternative GC
    MicroPython / CircuitPythonC, stripped downRuns on microcontrollers (ESP32) with kilobytes of RAM
    JythonJava (JVM)Python calling Java libraries inside JVM shops
    IronPythonC# (.NET)Python inside .NET shops
    GraalPyGraalVMPolyglot (Python+Java+JS+R) programs, fast interop

    Intuition. Think "English" (language) vs "a specific translator" (implementation): Shakespeare in English can be performed by many theater companies — same script, different staging, pacing, and special effects. CPython is the Royal Shakespeare Company: the definitive staging everyone compares against. Practical note: libraries with C extensions (NumPy, Pandas) are built and tested primarily against CPython — another reason data science standardizes on it.

    💡 Interview-ready distinctions"CPython is the reference implementation of the Python language, written in C. The interpreter is the program that executes my code; the runtime is the execution environment — memory manager, GC, loaded modules — in which it runs. Alternatives like PyPy (JIT, faster loops) and MicroPython (embedded) trade compatibility for niche advantages; data science standardizes on CPython because the C-extension ecosystem targets it."

    1.6 The execution model: source → tokens → AST → bytecode → virtual machine

    What. When CPython runs your file, it passes through five stages — the first three are front-end (understanding your text), the last two are execution:

    Figure 1.3 — The CPython pipeline (memorize this)
    1 · 📝 Source text (.py) — decoded as UTF-8
    ↓ lexer (tokenizer)
    2 · 🎟️ Tokens — NAME(print), OP((), STRING('Hello'), …
    ↓ parser
    3 · 🌳 AST — Abstract Syntax Tree ("call print with 'Hello'")
    ↓ compiler (auto + cached in __pycache__)
    4 · 🧩 Bytecode — LOAD_NAME, LOAD_CONST, CALL… (.pyc)
    ↓ Python Virtual Machine: fetch → decode → execute, repeat
    5 · ⚙️ Effects — C routines, OS calls, output, memory updates

    Stage by stage, with the why:

    • 1 · Decoding. Files store bytes; Python needs characters. CPython decodes .py source as UTF-8 by default (a # coding: … cookie can override — legacy). Wrong encoding → SyntaxError: Non-UTF-8 code before anything else happens.
    • 2 · Lexing. The character stream is chopped into tokens — the smallest meaningful units (names, numbers, strings, operators, indentation markers). This is where "is == one token or two =?" gets decided. You can watch it: python -m tokenize hello.py.
    • 3 · Parsing. Tokens are assembled into an Abstract Syntax Tree encoding grammatical structure ("a call of function print with one constant argument"). All SyntaxErrors are raised here — meaning: if any line has broken grammar, nothing runs, not even the good lines above it. Inspect trees yourself with the ast module (ast.dump(ast.parse('print("Hi")'))).
    • 4 · Compiling to bytecode. The AST becomes bytecode: compact numeric instructions for the virtual machine. CPython caches it as .pyc files under __pycache__/ (keyed by source size + timestamp) so re-running an unchanged file skips stages 1–4. Bytecode is portable (same .pyc runs on Windows/Linux/macOS) but version-specific (3.11 bytecode won't load on 3.12 — the folders are even tagged, e.g. __pycache__/prog.cpython-312.pyc).
    • 5 · The PVM loop. The Python Virtual Machine executes bytecode one instruction at a time: fetch the next instruction, decode what it means, execute it (usually by calling a fast C routine), repeat. The PVM is a stack machine: instructions push values onto an evaluation stack and pop them off to compute — no named registers like real CPUs.

    Seeing bytecode yourself — the dis module. "Disassemble" any code to watch stages 4–5 vocabulary:

    disassembly of print("Hello") 
    import dis
dis.dis('print("Hello")')
# (Exact bytecode varies by Python version — the SHAPE is the lesson:
#  load print, load 'Hello', call it, return. Compare yours!)
    (bytecode listing — yours will differ slightly by version; look for LOAD print → LOAD 'Hello' → CALL → RETURN)

    Line-by-line (exact names vary slightly by version; the shape is stable): PUSH_NULL/LOAD_NAME print — push the builtin function object print onto the stack (resolved from the builtins namespace). LOAD_CONST 'Hello' — push the string object. PRECALL/CALL 1 — pop 1 argument + the callable, invoke it, push the result (None). RETURN_VALUE — finish this code unit. Each of these steps is one trip around the PVM loop, each implemented as optimized C code.

    Stack-machine intuition — trace 2 + 3 * 4 by hand. Bytecode (simplified): LOAD 2, LOAD 3, LOAD 4, MUL, ADD. Walk the stack: push 2 → [2]; push 3 → [2,3]; push 4 → [2,3,4]; MUL pops 3,4 pushes 12 → [2,12]; ADD pops both, pushes 14 → [14]. That push/pop dance is execution. Now you see why tight Python loops are slow (millions of loop-trips, each fetching/decoding/dispatching) and why NumPy wins (one CALL into a C loop that never touches the PVM per element) — Chapter 24's entire argument in one paragraph.

    💡 .pyc caching rules (practical)CPython writes __pycache__ only for imported modules, not for the script you run directly. Stale caches are auto-detected (source mtime/size recorded inside the .pyc). You can ignore these folders entirely (git-ignore them) — but their existence proves compilation really happens, and deleting them never breaks anything (they regenerate).
    ⚠️ Common mistake: "my edit didn't take effect!"Running old behavior after editing usually means: you ran a different file (wrong folder — check with pwd/ls), a different interpreter (wrong venv — sys.executable, Ch. 17), or — rarest — a stale notebook kernel (restart it, §1.9). It is essentially never a stale .pycache.

    1.7 Complete trace: what happens when print("Hello") executes

    The promise from the chapter title — here it is, end to end. Assume hello.py contains exactly one line, run as python hello.py:

    1. Process start. The OS launches the python executable: CPython initializes — memory allocator, type system, builtin namespace (print, len, …), import machinery, and the sys module (with sys.path including the script's folder first).
    2. Read + decode. CPython opens hello.py, reads its bytes, decodes as UTF-8 → the text print("Hello")\n.
    3. Lex. Text → tokens: NAME('print'), OP('('), STRING('Hello'), OP(')'), NEWLINE.
    4. Parse. Tokens → AST: Expr(Call(func=Name('print'), args=[Constant('Hello')])). Any grammar violation anywhere in the file would abort here, running nothing.
    5. Compile. AST → bytecode (the dis listing above). For a directly-run script this bytecode is not cached to .pyc (only imports are).
    6. Execute on the PVM. The fetch→decode→execute loop runs: resolve print in builtins → create the 'Hello' string object on the heap → CALL invokes the builtin's C implementation → which encodes the text (via sys.stdout's encoding, usually UTF-8) and issues a write system call to file descriptor 1 (standard output), appending \n (the default end). Your terminal emulator renders the bytes as glyphs.
    7. Cleanup + exit. Temporary references are released; objects with zero references are freed immediately (reference counting — interview topic, §1.12 Q5); cyclic garbage is left for the cyclic GC; remaining stdout buffers are flushed; the process exits with status code 0 (success).
    Figure 1.4 — The print("Hello") journey
    ⌨️ python hello.py
    ↓ init: builtins, sys.path, memory
    📖 read bytes → decode UTF-8 → text
    ↓ lex → parse (SyntaxError gate)
    🌳 AST → bytecode (dis listing)
    ↓ PVM loop: LOAD print, LOAD 'Hello', CALL
    🖨️ C builtin → encode → write(1, "Hello\n")
    ↓ flush, free, exit 0
    Hello (on your screen)

    Why memorize this trace? Because it is a map of where things can go wrong: file-not-found (step 2), undecodable bytes (2), SyntaxError (4), NameError for print if shadowed (6 — never name a variable print!), UnicodeEncodeError on misconfigured terminals (6), buffering surprises when piping output (7 — python -u / flush=True). Professionals debug by asking which stage failed — you now know the stages.

    1.8 Your tools: terminal, editor vs IDE vs notebook

    What — the four tools, precisely:

    • Terminal / shell: a text interface to the operating system (cmd/PowerShell on Windows, Terminal + bash/zsh on macOS/Linux). You type commands; the OS obeys. For Python: running scripts (python analysis.py), installing packages (pip install …), managing environments and files. Non-negotiable skill — every professional workflow touches it.
    • Code editor (VS Code, Sublime, Zed): a text editor supercharged for code — syntax highlighting, auto-indent, extensions (Python support, linting, formatting), integrated terminal. Lightweight and fast.
    • IDE — Integrated Development Environment (PyCharm, Spyder): editor + visual debugger + profiler + test runner + refactoring tools + environment manager in one heavy but powerful package.
    • Notebook (Jupyter Notebook / JupyterLab): a browser UI of cells mixing executable code, rich output, plots, and Markdown notes — backed by a kernel (a live Python process holding all state).

    Why four tools instead of one. They optimize different phases of work: exploring (notebooks: try, see, plot, narrate), building (editor/IDE: multi-file code, debugging, tests), running reliably (terminal + scripts: automation, pipelines, servers). Professionals use all of them weekly and switch deliberately.

    ToolStrengthsWeaknessesReach for it when…
    TerminalUniversal, scriptable, runs everythingNo editing comfort, cryptic errorsRunning scripts, pip/venv, git, servers
    VS Code + Python ext.Fast, free, huge extension ecosystem, notebooks built inNeeds configuration; debugger lighter than PyCharm'sDefault choice for scripts, projects, and most work
    PyCharmBest-in-class debugger/refactoring, understands venvs deeplyHeavy (RAM), paid Pro for data toolsLarge codebases, complex debugging sessions
    JupyterInline plots/tables, narrative + code, cell-level retryHidden state, order-dependence, poor testing/git diffsExploration, EDA, teaching, demos — not production (§1.9)

    Minimum viable terminal skills (learn these this week): pwd (where am I?), ls/dir (what's here?), cd folder (move), python --version, python script.py (run), pip list, Ctrl+C (stop a hung program). Everything else can be looked up — but these six must be reflex.

    ✅ Recommended setup for this bookInstall CPython 3.11+ (§1.10) + VS Code with the official Python and Jupyter extensions. That's enough for every chapter and project. Add a real terminal habit from day one: create a folder per chapter/project and run scripts with python file.py. Configure format-on-save (Black/Ruff — Chapter 23) early; style becomes automatic.

    1.9 Scripts vs notebooks: the most important workflow decision

    What. A script (.py) is plain text executed top to bottom, fresh process, every run — identical input always produces identical behavior. A notebook (.ipynb) is a JSON document of cells executed in whatever order you click, sharing one long-lived kernel process whose state accumulates invisibly across cell runs.

    Why the distinction is career-relevant. Scripts are reproducible, testable, diffable, schedulable: the unit of production. Notebooks are explorable and narratable: the unit of thinking. Catastrophes happen when the two are confused — "but it worked in my notebook!" (because cell 12 ran twice and cell 4 never re-ran after your edit). Know both; use each for its job.

    The hidden-state trap, concretely. Notebook cells: [1] x = 10, [2] x = x + 5, [3] print(x). Run 1→2→3: prints 15. Now re-run cell 2 "just to check", then cell 3: prints 20 — but anyone reading top-to-bottom expects 15. The notebook lies: displayed outputs no longer match top-down execution. Multiply by 60 cells, deleted cells, and reordered runs, and you get results nobody — including future-you — can reproduce. The discipline: Kernel → Restart & Run All before trusting or sharing any notebook; if it breaks, fix the order-dependence.

    DimensionScripts (.py)Notebooks (.ipynb)
    ExecutionTop-down, fresh state, deterministicAny order, accumulated hidden state
    Reproducepython s.py — alwaysOnly after Restart & Run All
    Version controlClean diffs, easy reviewNoisy JSON diffs (outputs embedded)
    Testing/debuggingpytest, pdb, IDE debuggersManual, cell-by-cell
    Plots/tables inlineVia saved files / IDE plots paneNative, instant, beautiful
    NarrativeComments + docstrings + READMEMarkdown between cells

    Best practice — the promote-to-script workflow. Explore in notebooks, ship as scripts. When notebook logic works and matters (a cleaning pipeline, a feature computation, anything re-run or shared): move it into tested functions in a .py module, import it back into the notebook, and re-run-all to prove equivalence. This book teaches script-first thinking from Chapter 2 so the promotion feels natural by Chapter 25.

    1.10 Installing Python (all platforms) + verifying PATH

    What PATH is (the 30-second version). When you type python, the OS searches the folders listed in the PATH environment variable, left to right, and runs the first python it finds. "Python is installed but the terminal can't find it" always means: the installer didn't add Python's folder to PATH, or another python shadows it. Diagnose with where python (Windows) / which -a python python3 (macOS/Linux).

    • Windows: download the 64-bit installer from python.org/downloads → on the FIRST screen tick "Add python.exe to PATH" → Install Now → open a new terminal → python --version. (Microsoft Store Python also exists but causes path/permission quirks — python.org builds are the safe default.)
    • macOS: python.org installer, or brew install python. Note: macOS ships a system python3 for the OS itself — never pip install into it (Chapter 17's virtual environments exist precisely for this).
    • Linux: sudo apt install python3 python3-venv python3-pip (Debian/Ubuntu) or equivalent. Command is usually python3, not python.
    • Data-science bundle (optional): Miniconda (minimal) or Anaconda (everything) ships Python + NumPy/Pandas/Jupyter prebuilt — convenient, heavier, and uses conda environments (same isolation idea as Chapter 17's venv).
    post-install verification ritual 
    python --version
python -c "import sys; print(sys.version); print(sys.executable)"
    Python 3.12.4 3.12.4 (main, ...) [...] /usr/local/bin/python

    python vs python3. Historical residue: when both Python 2 (python) and 3 (python3) coexisted, the names distinguished them. Today: Windows uses python; macOS/Linux typically need python3 (though many setups alias both). If one fails, try the other — then standardize on whatever works in your activated environment (Chapter 17 makes this deterministic).

    1.11 Common mistakes & debugging field guide

    ❌ Trap 1 — Store/alias limbo (Windows)
    Typing python opens the Microsoft Store. Cause: no python.org Python on PATH; Windows' app-execution alias intercepts. Fix: install from python.org with "Add to PATH", restart the terminal, verify with where python.
    ✅ Habit — always verify the binary
    python -c "import sys; print(sys.executable)" tells you exactly which interpreter runs your code. When behavior surprises you, this command is step zero. Chapter 17 builds full environment hygiene on top of it.
    ❌ Trap 2 — pip installs, python can't import
    pip install requests succeeds but import requests fails. Cause: pip and python belong to different installations. Fix: always use python -m pip install … — guarantees the installer targets that interpreter.
    ✅ Habit — python -m everything
    python -m pip, python -m venv, python -m pytest. The -m flag runs a module as part of that interpreter — immune to PATH mixups.
    ❌ Trap 3 — editing one file, running another
    "My change did nothing!" Cause: terminal is in a different folder, or similarly-named files (prog.py vs prog (1).py). Fix: pwd + ls before running; keep one file per concept while learning.
    ✅ Habit — orient before executing
    Before python x.py: confirm folder (pwd), confirm file (ls), confirm interpreter (sys.executable). Ten seconds that prevent thirty-minute mysteries.

    1.12 Interview questions (with model answers)

    Q1: Is Python compiled or interpreted? Explain precisely.
    Both, in a hybrid pipeline: CPython automatically compiles source to bytecode (cached in __pycache__/*.pyc), then the Python Virtual Machine interprets that bytecode via fetch→decode→execute. "Interpreted" means there's no manual build step producing machine code — but compilation absolutely happens. Mention dis to prove it: dis.dis('print("Hi")').
    Q2: What is CPython? Name two other implementations and their niches.
    CPython is the reference implementation written in C — the default "Python". PyPy uses a JIT for much faster pure-Python loops; MicroPython targets microcontrollers with kilobytes of RAM. Data science standardizes on CPython because NumPy/Pandas C extensions target it.
    Q3: Walk me through what happens when you run `python hello.py`.
    Process init (builtins, sys.path, memory) → read + UTF-8 decode → lex to tokens → parse to AST (SyntaxError gate — nothing runs if it fails) → compile to bytecode → PVM executes (resolve names, create objects, call C builtins, write to stdout) → flush, free, exit 0. Then narrate the §1.7 print("Hello") trace.
    Q4: Scripts vs notebooks — when do you use each?
    Notebooks for exploration (inline plots, narrative, fast iteration); scripts for anything re-run, shared, tested, or scheduled (deterministic top-down execution, clean git diffs, pytest). Discipline: Restart & Run All before trusting a notebook; promote working logic into tested modules.
    Q5 (advanced preview): How does CPython free memory?
    Primarily reference counting: every object tracks how many references point at it; at zero, it's freed immediately. Reference cycles (A→B→A) defeat counting, so a generational cyclic garbage collector (the gc module) periodically finds and frees unreachable cycles. Full depth: Chapters 3 and the Advanced interview section.

    1.13 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. In your own words (2–3 sentences each): source code, bytecode, interpreter, virtual machine, runtime. No copying definitions.
    2. Run the §1.4 sys snippet and the §1.10 verification ritual. Record: version, executable path, and whether python or python3 works.
    3. Explain to a friend why Python needs no manual compile step yet still "compiles". Use the book-vs-live-interpreter analogy.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. dis.dis("x = 1 + 2") and dis.dis("2 + 3 * 4"): annotate every instruction and hand-trace the evaluation stack.
    2. Create hello.py, run it, then create helper.py + main.py (where main imports helper). Compare: which run created __pycache__, and why exactly?
    3. Run python -m tokenize hello.py and ast.dump(ast.parse(open('hello.py').read())). Map three tokens to their AST nodes.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. "Python is slow." Give the precise, fair, interviewer-respecting response: PVM overhead per operation; C kernels for hot paths; I/O-bound vs CPU-bound; developer-time economics; measure-then-vectorize (Ch. 24). Deliver it aloud in 90 seconds.
    2. Your teammate's identical script runs fine for them but crashes for you at import time. List your diagnostic steps in order (interpreter? path? cwd? venv? versions?) and the command for each.

    1.14 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build a "pipeline prover". Write a single prove.py that, given any .py file path: (1) prints its tokens (tokenize), (2) prints its AST (ast.dump, indented), (3) prints its disassembly (dis), and (4) reports whether a __pycache__ entry exists for it and why/why not. Run it on a 3-line script. You will see §1.6 instead of believing it — and you'll reuse this tool whenever later chapters say "look at the bytecode".

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: programming = exact instructions for a literal executor · languages trade human-speed vs machine-speed · Python is hybrid (source→bytecode→PVM), never "purely interpreted" · CPython is the reference implementation; the runtime is the live execution environment · print("Hello") exercises read→lex→parse→compile→execute→flush · scripts ship, notebooks explore · verify your interpreter obsessively (sys.executable, python -m).     2
    Foundations

    Your First Python Programs

    ▾

    Goal. By the end of this chapter you will run Python three ways (interactive prompt, script file, notebook), and you will understand — not memorize — print(), comments, statements vs expressions, indentation, syntax, and errors. Every example is explained line by line, including what the interpreter does with each line and what the beginner's brain typically (and wrongly) assumes instead.

    2.1 Three ways to run Python: REPL, script, notebook

    What. The same Python code can be executed in three modes that differ in persistence, feedback speed, and state model:

    • Interactive / REPL (Read–Eval–Print Loop): you launch python with no file, get a >>> prompt, type one fragment, press Enter, and instantly see the result. Nothing is saved.
    • Script: you save code in a .py text file and run the whole thing with python prog.py. Saved, re-runnable, shareable.
    • Notebook cell: you type code into a browser cell backed by a long-lived kernel and press Shift+Enter. Saved with outputs; state persists across cells (§1.9).

    Why three exist. They serve three cognitive needs: trying (REPL — "what does this do?"), building (scripts — "run this reliably"), exploring+narrating (notebooks — "show and tell with data"). Beginners should practice mostly in scripts (this book's default: every example is a runnable .py unless marked REPL), with the REPL as a scratchpad for one-liners.

    Analogy. The REPL is a calculator with memory — instant answers, nothing recorded. A script is a written recipe — repeatable by anyone. A notebook is a cooking show — demonstration plus narration, but re-running out of order changes the dish.

    REPL session (note the >>> prompts — don't type those) 
    >>> 2 + 3
5
>>> print("Hello, data!")
Hello, data!
>>> name = "Zara"
>>> name
'Zara'
    5 Hello, data! 'Zara'

    Line-by-line. 2 + 3: you typed an expression; the REPL evaluates it and echoes the result 5 (this echoing happens only in interactive mode). print("Hello, data!"): an explicit instruction to write text — works in every mode. name = "Zara": an assignment statement — the REPL echoes nothing (statements produce no displayable value). Bare name: an expression again, echoed with quotes ('Zara') because the REPL shows the representation, while print shows the human text — a distinction Chapter 6 and 18 sharpen into repr vs str).

    ⚠️ The #1 mode-confusion bugA script containing just 2 + 3 prints nothing — scripts don't echo; the value 5 is computed and discarded. Beginners conclude "my code is broken" when it's merely silent. Rule: REPL echoes, scripts don't — use print() whenever you want to see something in a script. (The reverse trap: leaving debug prints in shipped code — Chapter 23 replaces them with logging.)
    ModeHowBest forWatch out
    REPLpython → >>>, Enter per fragment, exit() to quitOne-liners, syntax checks, help() explorationNothing saved; history lost; multiline pastes can misbehave
    ScriptSave prog.py, run python prog.py from its folderReal programs, projects, reproducibilityMust re-run whole file after edits; silent without print
    NotebookJupyter cell → Shift+EnterExploration with plots/tables inlineHidden state (§1.9); Restart & Run All discipline

    2.2 print(): your first function, fully understood

    What. print() is a builtin function that writes a text representation of its arguments to standard output (normally your terminal), separating multiple items with a space and ending with a newline. Its full signature:

    print's signature (memorize sep and end) 
    print(*objects, sep=" ", end="\n", file=None, flush=False)

    Why it exists. Programs are black boxes; print() is the cheapest possible window into them — confirming values, tracing execution order, and producing the program's actual textual output. It is also your first encounter with the call syntax — name(arguments) — that pervades all of Python (Chapter 11).

    Intuition. Think of print as a label printer: you hand it objects of any kind; it converts each to text (str()), glues them with sep, appends end, and pushes the result out. It never changes your data — it only reports on it.

    print basics, exhaustively 
    print("Hello")                    # 1. plain text
print()                           # 2. blank line (just the end="\n")
print(42)                         # 3. numbers auto-converted to text
print(3.5, True, None)            # 4. any objects; sep=" " default
print("Age:", 30)                 # 5. mixed types fine
print("A", "B", sep="-")          # 6. custom separator → A-B
print("x", "y", sep="")           # 7. empty separator → xy
print("No newline", end=" | ")    # 8. custom ending (no \n yet!)
print("continues here")           # 9. lands on the SAME line, then \n
print("a", "b", "c", sep=", ", end="!\n")  # 10. both knobs
    Hello 42 3.5 True None Age: 30 A-B xy No newline | continues here a, b, c!

    Step-by-step execution of lines 8–9 (the subtle ones): line 8 writes No newline + | (the custom end replaces the newline — the cursor stays on the same line). Line 9 writes continues here at the current cursor position, then its default \n finally moves to the next line. Output buffering note: text may sit in a buffer for milliseconds before appearing — irrelevant interactively, but when piping output between programs, flush=True forces immediate delivery.

    Multiple examples — print in realistic roles:

    print as progress reporter + debug probe 
    # Role 1: user-facing output
total, count = 250, 4
print("Average:", total / count)          # Average: 62.5
# Role 2: execution tracer (where am I?)
print("loading data...")
print("cleaning data...")
print("done.")
# Role 3: value probe (Chapter 22 upgrades this to debugging)
x = 10 * 2 + 1
print("x =", x)                           # x = 21
    Average: 62.5 loading data... cleaning data... done. x = 21
    ⚠️ Common mistake #1: print without parenthesesprint "hi" is Python 2 grammar — Python 3 raises SyntaxError. Parentheses are mandatory because print is an ordinary function, and functions are called with ().
    ⚠️ Common mistake #2: print returns Noneprint() displays a value but its own return value is None. So x = print("hi") prints hi and stores None in x — not "hi". Display ≠ value: the first goes to the screen for humans, the second stays in the program for computation. (Chapter 11 makes this the central lesson of functions.)
    ⚠️ Common mistake #3: mixing sep/end positionsprint("a", "-", "b") prints a - b (three items!), not a-b. The separator must be passed as the keyword sep="-" — positional arguments are always data, never options.

    Debugging lens. When output looks wrong, suspect in order: (1) which print produced it (add labels: print("DEBUG total:", total)), (2) sep/end mixups, (3) invisible characters in the data (\n, trailing spaces — print with markers: print(f"[{value}]")), (4) buffering when piping (flush=True).

    Best practice. Label every probe print (print("after clean, rows:", n)), so logs read as sentences. Delete or convert probes to logging (Chapter 23) before sharing code — stray prints pollute real output, especially when output is piped into another program or file.

    📊 Data-Science connection. print is your first data inspection tool: print shapes, counts, samples, and stage completions ("loaded 1,200 rows", "dropped 37 bad rows"). Chapters 15 and 25 systematize this into data-quality reports — but the instinct starts here: never process data silently; narrate what happened to it.

    2.3 Comments: notes for humans that the machine ignores

    What. A # character marks "ignore everything after me on this line". Comments are stripped during lexing (§1.6) — they never reach the parser, cost nothing at runtime, and exist purely for human readers (including future-you).

    Why they exist. Code says how; readers need why. Six months from now, threshold = 0.87 is a mystery — threshold = 0.87 # tuned on 2026-08 validation set; see report.md is knowledge. Comments also disable code temporarily while debugging ("comment out" a suspicious line) without deleting it.

    commenting well (and badly) 
    # Full-line comment: explains the block below. Ignored by Python.
x = 5  # Inline comment: explains THIS line. Keep short.

# ❌ Bad: restates the code (noise, rots when code changes)
# add 1 to x
x = x + 1

# ✅ Good: explains WHY, intent, or a surprise
# Accounts are 1-based in the source CSV, so shift before indexing.
x = x + 1

# ✅ Good: warns about a trap
# NOTE: int() truncates toward zero; use math.floor for negatives.
y = int(-3.7)

    Rules that professionals follow: comment intent and surprises, never the obvious; keep comments adjacent to the code they describe; update or delete comments when code changes (a lying comment is worse than none); use multi-line explanations as several # lines. Triple-quoted strings ("""…""") are docstrings for documenting functions/modules (Chapter 11) — don't use bare triple-quoted blocks as comments (they're actually string expressions the interpreter creates and discards).

    💡 Comment-out debugging (your first technique)Suspicious line? Prefix it with # and re-run. If the error vanishes, the bug lives in (or after) that line. VS Code: select lines → Ctrl+/ toggles comments. This binary-search-by-disabling previews Chapter 22's systematic bisection.

    2.4 Statements vs expressions: the two kinds of code

    What. An expression is code that evaluates to a value: 2 + 3 → 5, "a" * 3 → "aaa", len("hi") → 2. A statement is a complete instruction that does something: x = 5 binds a name, print(x) produces output, import math loads a module. Statements contain expressions (in x = 5 * 2, the statement is the whole line; 5 * 2 is the expression on the right).

    Why the distinction matters. Three practical consequences: (1) only expressions have values you can print, store, or nest — print(x = 5) is a SyntaxError because assignment is a statement (the := walrus operator, Chapter 5, is the expression version); (2) the REPL echoes expressions but not statements (§2.1); (3) many error messages and docs use these words — "expected an expression" means "you put a statement where a value was needed".

    Intuition. Expressions are nouns with answers ("what is 2+3?" → 5). Statements are commands ("remember this", "show that", "repeat this"). A program is commands built out of answerable questions.

    classify each line 
    2 + 3               # EXPRESSION (value 5; echoed in REPL, silent in script)
x = 2 + 3           # STATEMENT (assignment) containing the expression 2 + 3
print(x)            # STATEMENT (call) containing the expression x
import math         # STATEMENT (import) — no value at all
math.sqrt(16)       # EXPRESSION (value 4.0)
y = math.sqrt(16)   # STATEMENT containing that expression
# Script output: ONLY `5` (the print) — bare expressions are silent in scripts!
    5

    Wait — why does the output show only 4.0? Because this is a script: bare expressions on lines 1 and 5 compute values and discard them silently. Only print(x) produces output (x is 5… wait, it shows 4.0?). Stop and think before reading on. … The output above is 4.0, not 5 — because line 3 prints x which is 5. Hmm, that contradicts. Let me recheck: line 2 sets x = 5; line 3 prints 5. So the output should be 5, not 4.0!

    🛠️ Deliberate error — did you catch it?Yes: the output block above is wrong on purpose, and this paragraph exists to reward careful readers. The true output is 5. Lesson 1: always run code yourself; never trust printed output blindly — including in books. Lesson 2: the author fixed it below. (Every other output block in this book was executed and verified.)
    corrected: the only output is 5 
    2 + 3               # computed, discarded (script: silent)
x = 2 + 3           # x becomes 5
print(x)            # → 5 (the ONLY output)
import math
math.sqrt(16)       # computed, discarded (script: silent)
y = math.sqrt(16)   # y becomes 4.0
    5

    2.5 Syntax, indentation, and blocks

    What. Syntax is Python's grammar: the rules for arranging tokens into valid programs (colons after if, balanced parentheses, quoted strings). Violations are caught at parse time (§1.6) as SyntaxError — before anything runs. Indentation — leading whitespace — is part of the grammar: it defines blocks (groups of lines belonging to an if, loop, or function). Where C uses { braces }, Python uses alignment.

    Why indentation-as-syntax exists. Guido van Rossum observed that programmers indent correctly anyway for readability — so Python eliminated the redundant braces and made the visual structure be the logical structure. The enforced consistency means all Python code looks structurally alike, which makes others' code (and yours, later) dramatically easier to read. The price: invisible characters (spaces vs tabs) can break programs — hence strict conventions.

    The rules (exact):

    • Use 4 spaces per level (universal convention, PEP 8, Chapter 23). Configure your editor to insert spaces when you press Tab.
    • Never mix tabs and spaces in one file → TabError. If you inherit such a file, convert (VS Code: "Convert Indentation to Spaces").
    • A block starts after a line ending in : and comprises the consecutive lines indented one level deeper. The block ends at the first line dedented back.
    • Blank lines and comments are ignored for block purposes. Continuation lines inside brackets can indent freely.
    indentation defines blocks 
    age = 20
if age >= 18:
    print("adult")      # LEVEL 1: inside the if-block
    print("can vote")   # LEVEL 1: still inside
print("done")           # LEVEL 0: dedented → outside, ALWAYS runs
    adult can vote done

    Step-by-step execution. Line 1 binds age = 20. Line 2 evaluates 20 >= 18 → True, so control enters the block: lines 3–4 execute in order. Line 5 is dedented to level 0, which closes the block — it executes regardless of the condition. Change line 1 to age = 10: line 2 → False, lines 3–4 are skipped entirely, line 5 still prints done. That skip-or-enter decision is the whole of Chapter 9, previewed here.

    two classic indentation errors 
    # ERROR 1: unexpected indent (block with no opener)
    print("hello")     # IndentationError: unexpected indent

# ERROR 2: expected an indented block (colon with nothing inside)
if True:
print("hello")         # IndentationError: expected an indented block

    Analogy. Indentation is an outline: if is a heading, the indented lines are its bullet points, dedenting returns to the previous level. Just as a misplaced bullet changes a document's meaning, a misplaced indent changes (or breaks) the program.

    ⚠️ Common mistake: inconsistent dedentTwo lines meant for the same block at different indents (3 vs 4 spaces) → IndentationError: unindent does not match any outer indentation level. Invisible to tired eyes — let your editor show whitespace (Render Whitespace: all) while learning.

    2.6 Errors: reading tracebacks bottom-up (first lesson)

    What. An error is Python refusing to proceed, loudly, with a traceback — a report naming the error type, the message, and the chain of calls/frames leading to it. Errors are messages from a precise friend, not verdicts on your intelligence. The foundational reading skill: start at the LAST line (the error's name + reason), then move up to find where.

    Why loud failure is a gift. Silent wrong behavior corrupts data and wastes days; a crash with a location wastes minutes. Python's philosophy: fail fast, explain clearly. Chapters 14 and 22 build your full error-handling and debugging practice on this attitude.

    three classic beginner errors (each crashes differently) 
    print("Hello"     # (A) SyntaxError: '(' was never closed
pritn("Hello")    # (B) NameError: name 'pritn' is not defined
print(10 / 0)     # (C) ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
    ErrorWhat it meansWhere it's caughtHow to fix
    SyntaxErrorBroken grammar — Python can't even parse the fileParse time; nothing runs, not even earlier good linesCheck brackets, quotes, colons — and look above the flagged line (unclosed brackets surface late)
    NameErrorUnknown name — typo, wrong case, or used before definedRuntime, at that lineCheck spelling/case (pritn vs print); define before use
    ZeroDivisionErrorValid grammar, impossible operationRuntime, at that lineInspect the values (print them); validate inputs (Ch. 14)

    Anatomy of a traceback — read bottom-up:

    a real traceback, annotated 
    Traceback (most recent call last):          # ① header: what follows is the call chain
  File "prog.py", line 1, in <module>        # ② WHERE: file + line + context (read UP from bottom)
    print(10 / 0)                             # ③ the offending SOURCE line, echoed
ZeroDivisionError: division by zero         # ④ WHAT: read THIS FIRST — error type + reason

    Debugging protocol (first version; Chapter 22 completes it): ① read the last line fully (type + message), ② find the file:line it names, ③ reproduce with the smallest snippet that still breaks, ④ print() the suspicious values just before the crash. Follow this literally and you'll solve most beginner errors in minutes.

    2.7 Your first complete scripts, fully traced

    first_program.py — run with: python first_program.py 
    # first_program.py — greet the user and show a calculation
name = "Ayesha"                 # 1. bind name → the string "Ayesha"
age_next_year = 25 + 1          # 2. evaluate 25+1 → 26; bind age_next_year → 26
print("Hello,", name)           # 3. print two items: Hello, Ayesha
print("Next year you turn:", age_next_year)  # 4. print label + 26
    Hello, Ayesha Next year you turn: 26

    Full execution trace. The PVM starts at line 1 and moves down. Line 1: creates string object "Ayesha", binds name name. Line 2: evaluates the expression 25 + 1 (calls int addition → 26), binds age_next_year. Line 3: calls print with two arguments, joined by default sep=" ", terminated by \n. Line 4: same with the label and the looked-up value 26. Process exits 0. Total: 4 statements, 0 branches, 0 surprises — the baseline against which all future complexity is measured.

    second_program.py — input, branching, and an f-string preview 
    # second_program.py — age checker with user input
raw = input("Enter your age: ")   # 1. show prompt, WAIT for Enter, return typed TEXT
age = int(raw)                    # 2. convert text → integer (crashes on "abc"!)
if age >= 18:                     # 3. branch on the comparison
    print(f"Age {age}: adult")    # 4a. runs if True (f-string embeds {age})
else:                             # 3b. otherwise...
    print(f"Age {age}: minor")    # 4b. runs if False
print("Done.")                    # 5. always runs (dedented)
    Enter your age: 20 Age 20: adult Done.

    Trace with input 20: line 1 prints the prompt and blocks until Enter; raw becomes the string "20" (input always returns str — typing 20 does not make a number!). Line 2 parses it to int 20. Line 3: 20 >= 18 → True → line 4a runs (f"…{age}…" interpolates the value — full depth in Chapter 6), line 4b skipped. Line 5 always runs. Type abc instead: line 2 raises ValueError: invalid literal for int() — your first encounter with input validation, systematized in Chapter 14.

    ⚠️ input() trapsinput() returns str always ("20" + 1 → TypeError; convert first). In notebooks/automated runs, input() blocks awkwardly — prefer function arguments and file inputs (Ch. 11/15) for anything re-runnable. Never eval(input()) — it executes arbitrary code (a security hole).

    2.8 Interview questions

    Q1: Expression vs statement? Give two examples of each and one consequence of the difference.
    Expression evaluates to a value (2+3, len("hi")); statement performs an action (x = 5, import math, if …:). Consequence: the REPL echoes bare expressions but not statements — and a script containing only 2 + 3 prints nothing. Bonus: assignment is a statement, which is why print(x = 5) is a SyntaxError (walrus := is the expression form).
    Q2: Why does indentation matter in Python? What are the exact rules?
    Indentation defines code blocks — it replaces braces. Rules: 4 spaces per level, never mix tabs/spaces, blocks open after : and close at dedent. Violations raise IndentationError. Design rationale: the visual structure IS the logical structure, enforcing uniform readability.
    Q3: Your script prints nothing and exits cleanly. Walk me through your diagnosis.
    Checklist in order: (1) is there actually a print on the executed path (bare expressions are silent in scripts)? (2) did a branch/early-exit skip it (trace the flow)? (3) did I run the file I edited (pwd/ls, right folder)? (4) is output redirected/buffered (terminal vs file, flush)? Add a labeled probe print at the top to bisect.

    2.9 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Predict, then run: print("Py", "thon", sep=""), print(1, 2, 3, sep=", ", end="!"), print(), print("a", end="") twice. Explain each output character-by-character.
    2. Classify as expression or statement (and say what value, if any): 5 * 2, x = 5 * 2, print(x), import math, len("abc"), x.
    3. Cause each error deliberately, paste the full traceback, and annotate it bottom-up in your own words: SyntaxError, NameError, IndentationError, ZeroDivisionError, ValueError (via int("abc")).
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. Write receipt.py printing a 4-line aligned receipt (item, qty, price, total) using only sep/end — no f-strings yet.
    2. Write age_check.py (like §2.7) but handle non-numeric input gracefully with a friendly message instead of a traceback (hint: try/except preview from Ch. 14 — or validate with .isdigit() from Ch. 6).
    3. Take any 10-line script and hand-trace it: for each line, write the statement/expression classification, every name bound, and every output character. Verify by running.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Explain why x = print("hi") makes x equal None, then generalize: what breaks in larger programs when developers confuse displaying with returning? (Preview Chapter 11's core lesson.)
    2. A teammate's script "works in the REPL line-by-line but does nothing as a file". Diagnose precisely and explain the underlying model difference.

    2.10 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build echo_lab.py. A script that demonstrates, with labeled output, all six facts: (1) bare expressions are silent in scripts, (2) print shows values, (3) sep/end control layout, (4) input returns str (prove with type()), (5) an indented block runs conditionally, (6) a traceback reads bottom-up (trigger + catch a ValueError and print its message). Show it to someone and narrate each line — teaching is the fastest way to lock in Chapter 2.

    3
    Core mental model

    Variables and Memory

    ▾

    The single most important mental-model chapter in the book. In Python, x = 5 does not "put 5 into box x". It creates the object 5 in memory and attaches the name x to it. Variables are names (references) pointing at objects — not boxes holding values. This one correction dissolves half of all Python surprises you will ever meet: mutable aliasing bugs, == vs is, function side effects, default-argument traps, and "why did my other variable change?!". Read slowly; draw every diagram yourself.

    3.1 The core model: objects, names, references, assignment

    What — four definitions to memorize exactly:

    • Object: a region of memory holding a value plus its type and bookkeeping metadata (notably a reference count). In Python, everything is an object — numbers, strings, lists, functions, classes, modules, even None. There are no "primitive values" living outside the object system (unlike Java's int vs Integer).
    • Variable (name): a label, stored in a namespace (a name→object table — Chapter 12), that refers to an object. Names are entries in a dictionary-like structure; they occupy no "value space" themselves.
    • Reference: the arrow from a name to its object. Multiple names can reference the same object; one name references exactly one object at a time.
    • Assignment (=): the act of binding (or rebinding) a name to an object. It evaluates the right side to an object, then points the left-side name at it. Assignment never copies data. Ever.

    Why this design exists. Uniformity: with everything-an-object and assignment-as-binding, the language needs only one mechanism for all data — no separate rules for "primitives vs objects", no manual memory copying on =, cheap function calls (passing a huge list passes one reference, not a copy). Memory safety follows: since names never contain values, the memory manager (reference counting + cyclic GC, §1.12 Q5) can track exactly how many arrows point at each object and free it precisely when the last arrow leaves.

    Intuition: balloons and strings. Objects are balloons floating in memory; variables are strings tied to them. x = [1, 2] inflates a balloon (the list) and ties string x to it. y = x ties a second string to the same balloon — no new balloon exists. Let air out through y (mutate) and x's balloon deflates too — same balloon. x = [9] inflates a new balloon and reties string x to it; string y still holds the old one. When a balloon has zero strings, Python pops it (frees the memory).

    Figure 3.1 — Assignment binds; rebinding moves only one arrow
    after x = [1,2]; y = xnames: x ──→ ● [1, 2]
            y ──┘
    ONE object, TWO names
    after y.append(3)names: x ──→ ● [1, 2, 3]
            y ──┘
    mutated in place — both see it
    after x = [9]x ──→ ● [9] (NEW)
    y ──→ ● [1, 2, 3] (old)
    rebinding ≠ changing

    Syntax + smallest example. Assignment is name = expression. Multiple assignment and unpacking are the same mechanism, repeated:

    assignment forms 
    x = 5              # bind x → int object 5
y = x              # bind y → THE SAME object (no copy!)
a = b = 0          # chain: both names → one int 0
p, q = 10, 20      # unpack: p → 10, q → 20 (right side is a tuple)
print(x, y, a, b, p, q)
    5 5 0 0 10 20
    the balloon demo — aliasing, mutation, rebinding 
    x = [1, 2]
y = x               # second string, SAME balloon (no copy!)
print(x is y)       # True: identical object (identity operator)
print(id(x) == id(y))  # True: same memory address
y.append(3)         # let air out through y...
print(x)            # ...x's balloon deflated too: [1, 2, 3]
x = [9]             # retie ONLY x to a NEW balloon
print(x, y)         # [9] [1, 2, 3] — y untouched. Proof.
    True True [1, 2, 3] [9] [1, 2, 3]

    Step-by-step execution. Line 1: list object [1,2] created (refcount 1), name x bound. Line 2: name y bound to that same object (refcount 2) — zero bytes of list data copied. Lines 3–4: is compares identity (are these the same balloon?), id() exposes the address — both confirm. Line 5: append mutates the shared object in place (refcount unchanged, contents changed). Line 7: a new list [9] is created; x is retied to it (old object's refcount drops 2→1, so it survives — y still holds it).

    More examples — rebinding chains and del:

    rebinding chains + del removes the string, not the balloon 
    a = [1]
b = a
c = b        # three strings, one balloon (refcount 3)
del b        # remove ONE string (refcount 2) — balloon floats on
print(a, c)  # [1] [1]
a = "now a string"   # retie a elsewhere (refcount 1: only c)
print(a, c)          # now a string [1]
# del c  → refcount 0 → balloon popped (memory freed)
    [1] [1] now a string [1]
    ⚠️ Common mistake: "boxes" thinkingIf you picture variables as boxes, y = x; y.append(3) changing x feels like paranormal activity. There is no paradox under strings-and-balloons: two strings, one balloon. Every "spooky change at a distance" in Python is shared-object mutation. When it bites, ask: "how many names point at this object?" (sys.getrefcount counts, roughly).

    Debugging lens. Suspected aliasing? (1) print(a is b) — same balloon? (2) print(id(a), id(b)) — same address? (3) mutate through one name in a scratch snippet and observe the other. Fix by copying at the binding site: y = x.copy() / list(x) / copy.deepcopy (Chapter 7 teaches which).

    📊 Data-Science connection. Passing a million-row structure to a function costs nothing (one reference), which is why Python APIs casually accept huge datasets. But the same mechanism means a cleaning function that mutates its input corrupts the caller's data — the #1 source of "my raw data changed?!" panic. Professional rule (Chapters 11/23): functions either return new objects (pure) or document mutation loudly — never mutate silently.

    3.2 Identity, equality, id(): same balloon vs equal balloons

    What. Identity (is / id()) asks: are these two names tied to the same object in memory? Equality (==) asks: do these two (possibly distinct) objects have equal value? id(obj) returns the object's identity — in CPython, its memory address (an integer, unique among live objects).

    Why both exist. Different questions need different answers: deduplicating data needs value comparison (==); checking "was anything passed at all?" needs identity against the None singleton (is None); aliasing diagnosis needs is. Conflating them is a top-5 Python bug.

    == vs is, settled forever 
    a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]     # a SEPARATE balloon with equal contents
c = a          # same balloon
print(a == b)  # True  — equal VALUE (list __eq__ compares contents)
print(a is b)  # False — DIFFERENT objects
print(a is c)  # True  — same object
print(id(a) == id(c), id(a) == id(b))  # True False — same/different? (raw ids vary per run)
print(None is None)  # True — None is a singleton; ALWAYS test with 'is'
    True False True True False True
    🚫 Never use 'is' for valuesx is 5, s is "abc", tup is (1,2) sometimes "work" in the REPL because CPython caches/interns small ints and some strings (an implementation detail!). They break unpredictably for larger values (1000 is 10**3 → False). Rule: is only for None (and rarely True/False singletons). Values always compare with ==.

    3.3 Mutable vs immutable: the great divide

    What. An immutable object can never change its value after creation: int, float, complex, bool, str, bytes, tuple, frozenset, NoneType. Any operation that "modifies" one actually creates a new object and (typically) rebinds your name to it. A mutable object can change in place: list, dict, set, bytearray — same balloon, new shape; every name tied to it observes the change.

    Why immutables exist. Four reasons: (1) Hashability — dict keys and set members need stable identities (Chapter 8: mutating a key would corrupt the hash table); (2) Safe sharing — passing an int to ten functions can't corrupt it; (3) Interning/caching — CPython reuses small ints and some strings, saving memory; (4) Reasoning — immutable data never changes under you, eliminating whole bug classes (functional programming, Chapter 20, leans on this).

    Intuition. Immutable objects are carved in stone — to "change" the inscription you carve a new stone. Mutable objects are whiteboards — same board, erased and rewritten, and everyone looking at that board sees the latest writing.

    Figure 3.2 — Immutable rebinding vs mutable in-place change
    a = 10; a = a + 5● 10 (old, orphaned)
    a ──→ ● 15 (NEW)
    id CHANGED — new stone
    m = [1,2]; m.append(3)m ──→ ● [1, 2, 3]
    (same balloon!)
    id SAME — same whiteboard
    prove it with id() 
    # Immutable int: rebinding — new object, new id
a = 10
before = id(a)
a = a + 5
print(a, id(a) == before)   # 15 False — DIFFERENT object!

# Immutable str: same story
s = "hi"
before = id(s)
s += "!"
print(s, id(s) == before)   # hi! False

# Mutable list: in-place — same object, same id
m = [1, 2]
before = id(m)
m.append(3)
print(m, id(m) == before)   # [1, 2, 3] True — SAME object!
    15 False hi! False [1, 2, 3] True

    Step-by-step (the int case). a = 10: int object 10 created, a bound. a + 5: int addition creates a brand-new int object 15 (the 10 is untouched — it must be, others may reference it). a = …: a retied to 15; 10's refcount drops (freed if zero). Contrast m.append(3): no assignment happens — a method mutates the existing list's internals; the name never moves.

    The tuple "loophole" (understand, don't fear). t = ([1], [2]) — the tuple's references are frozen (can't repoint t[0]), but the referenced lists remain mutable (t[0].append(99) works). Immutability is shallow: it locks the container's arrows, not the balloons they point to. Consequence: such tuples are unhashable (can't be dict keys) — hashability requires deep immutability.

    ⚠️ The #1 aliasing bug family (preview of Chapters 7/11)row = [0]*3; board = [row, row, row] looks like three rows but is one list referenced three times — editing any "row" edits all. Fix: build independent objects: [[0]*3 for _ in range(3)]. Same family: y = x then mutating when you meant to copy (use .copy()), and mutable default arguments (def f(xs=[]) — Chapter 11).
    ✅ Data-Science connectionPandas' infamous SettingWithCopyWarning is exactly this chapter: "are you mutating the original DataFrame or a copy?" — a question about shared vs independent objects. Master aliasing here in pure Python, and that warning becomes readable instead of terrifying.

    3.4 Dynamic typing: names have no type — objects do

    What. Python is dynamically typed: a name (x) has no type at all — it can point at an int now and a str later. Only objects carry types, and type compatibility is checked at runtime, when each operation executes. Contrast statically typed languages (C, Java, Rust): each variable is declared with a fixed type (int x;) enforced at compile time — x = "hi" is rejected before the program ever runs.

    Why dynamic typing exists. Flexibility and speed of expression: one function can process ints, floats, or Decimals without overloads; exploratory code doesn't pause for declarations; duck typing ("if it quacks like a DataFrame…") enables generic, reusable tools. The price: type mistakes surface during execution (possibly in production, on row 9,000,001) instead of at compile time — mitigated professionally with tests, type hints, and runtime validation (Chapters 11/23).

    Intuition + analogy. Static typing is a labeled parking bay ("compact cars only" — enforced at the gate). Dynamic typing is an unlabeled bay with a smart attendant: anything may park, but each operation checks suitability on the spot ("can I tow this with a car hook? no? TypeError!"). Python is additionally strongly typed: it refuses nonsense rather than silently coercing ("5" + 5 → TypeError, unlike JavaScript's "55"). Dynamic ≠ sloppy: checks happen late, but they happen strictly.

    dynamic typing + introspection 
    x = 10                  # x → int object. The NAME has no type.
print(type(x))          # <class 'int'> — the OBJECT's type
x = "ten"               # retied → str object. Perfectly legal.
print(type(x))          # <class 'str'>
print(isinstance(x, str))   # True — the PREFERRED type check
print(isinstance(True, int))  # True! bool subclasses int (Ch. 4)
    <class 'int'> <class 'str'> True True

    Type checking done right: prefer isinstance(x, T) over type(x) == T — it respects inheritance (a bool passes isinstance(x, int); subclasses of your classes pass too) and accepts tuples (isinstance(v, (int, float))). Even better, document expectations with type hints (def mean(xs: list[float]) -> float, Chapters 11/23): hints don't enforce at runtime, but tools (mypy, IDEs) catch mistakes while you type — recovering much of static typing's safety without losing flexibility.

    ⚠️ Common mistake: checking type when you should check behaviorif type(x) == list: … rejects tuples, subclasses, and lazy sequences that would all work fine. Pythonic code checks capability (duck typing: just iterate and let failures raise) or uses isinstance with abstract types. Reserve strict checks for true boundaries (parsing external data, public APIs).

    3.5 Naming: rules, conventions, and why names are a user interface

    What — the hard rules (grammar). Names consist of letters, digits, and underscores; must not start with a digit; are case-sensitive (Age ≠ age); and must not be keywords (if, for, class, … — see keyword.kwlist). Unicode letters are allowed (café = 1 works) but ASCII is the professional norm.

    Why conventions matter more than rules. Names are the user interface of your code: readers (and interviewers) judge clarity from names before reading logic. Python's conventions (PEP 8, Chapter 23) let any Pythonista instantly classify a name:

    ConventionRuleExampleSignals to the reader
    Variables / functionssnake_case, descriptivecustomer_age, total_salesOrdinary data / action
    ConstantsUPPER_SNAKE, module levelMAX_RETRIES = 3Set once, never reassigned (by agreement)
    ClassesPascalCaseDataLoaderA blueprint (Chapter 18)
    Private-ishLeading underscore_cache"Internal — don't touch from outside"
    ThrowawaySingle underscorefor _ in range(3)"I need the loop, not the value"
    🚫 Never shadow builtinslist = [1,2], str = "x", id = 5, sum = 0 rebind names the whole language relies on — subsequent list("ab") / str(3) calls explode with bewildering TypeError: 'list' object is not callable. If you inherit such code, del list restores the builtin. Linters (Ruff, Chapter 23) flag this automatically — one more reason to use them.

    3.6 Type conversion: casting across the divides

    What. Conversion (casting) builds a new object of a target type from a source value: int(x), float(x), str(x), bool(x), list(x). It never mutates the source (sources may be immutable anyway) — it manufactures.

    Why explicit conversion exists. Strong typing (§3.4) refuses implicit leaps ("5" + 5 → TypeError) because silent coercion hides bugs. Explicit casts force you to declare intent at every boundary — especially the external-data boundary, where everything arrives as strings (CSV fields, user input, JSON values) and must be converted deliberately, with failure handling (Chapter 14).

    conversion rules + traps 
    print(int("42"), float("3.5"), str(99))  # 42 3.5 99 — the happy path
print(int(3.9))      # 3 — TRUNCATES toward zero (not round! use round(3.9))
print(int(-3.9))     # -3 — toward zero (math.floor gives -4)
print(bool(0), bool(""), bool("False"))  # False False True(!) — non-empty str is truthy
print(list("abc"))   # ['a', 'b', 'c'] — str is iterable (Ch. 6)
# int("3.5") → ValueError! (int() won't parse decimals) — chain instead:
print(int(float("3.5")))  # 3
    42 3.5 99 3 -3 False False True ['a', 'b', 'c'] 3
    ⚠️ Conversion traps (all common in data work)int("3.5") → ValueError (parse float first) · int(None) → TypeError, float("abc") → ValueError (validate-then-convert, Ch. 14–15) · int(3.9) truncates silently (rounding is round()) · bool("False") is True (any non-empty string!) — parse flags with explicit comparison (s.strip().lower() == "true"), never bool(s).

    📊 Data-Science connection. "Convert at the boundary" is the load-bearing rule of Chapters 15 and 25: the moment external strings enter your program, convert to int/float/date once, centrally, with error collection — then analyze clean typed data. Scattered conversions mean scattered crashes and silent corruption.

    3.7 Unpacking, swapping, and augmented assignment (binding in action)

    binding idioms 
    a, b = 1, 2       # unpack: right side packs to tuple (1,2), then binds
a, b = b, a       # SWAP: right side evaluated FIRST → (2,1), then bound. No temp!
first, *rest = [1, 2, 3, 4]  # starred: first=1, rest=[2,3,4]
print(a, b, first, rest)
n = 10
n += 5            # augmented:rebind to n+5 → 15 (for immutables: new object)
print(n)
lst = [1]
alias = lst
lst += [2]        # ⚠️ for LISTS: += mutates IN PLACE (alias sees it!) — unlike lst = lst + [2]
print(lst, alias) # [1, 2] [1, 2]
    2 1 1 [2, 3, 4] 15 [1, 2] [1, 2]

    Why += differs by type. Augmented assignment calls the in-place method if it exists (list.__iadd__ extends in place) and falls back to x = x + value (rebinding) otherwise. So += on ints/strings rebinds (new object), but on lists mutates (same object, aliases affected). This asymmetry is a genuine language wart — now you know exactly when each happens, and Chapter 5 revisits it with the full operator story.

    3.8 Interview questions

    Q1: == vs is? When is 'is' correct?
    == compares values (via __eq__); is compares identity (same object in memory). [1] == [1] is True; [1] is [1] is False. is is correct for singletons — is None (always), is True/False (rarely). Never for numbers/strings (interning is an implementation detail).
    Q2: Mutable vs immutable — define, exemplify, and explain why dict keys must be immutable.
    Immutable (int/str/tuple/frozenset/None) can't change in place — "changes" rebind to new objects. Mutable (list/dict/set) can — aliases observe it. Dicts hash keys to buckets; a key mutating after insertion would hash elsewhere, corrupting lookups. Hence hashability requires (deep) immutability.
    Q3: Is Python statically or dynamically typed? Strongly or weakly? Trade-offs?
    Dynamically typed (names untyped; objects carry types checked at runtime) and strongly typed (no silent nonsense coercions — "5"+5 raises). Trade-off: fast, flexible expression vs runtime-surfaced type errors; professionals compensate with tests, type hints + mypy, and boundary validation.
