ds.update(["X", "Y"])                 # add many
print(sorted(ds))
# Dedup that PRESERVES order (sets don't!) — the pro idiom:
items = ["b", "a", "b", "c", "a"]
print(list(dict.fromkeys(items)))  # ['b', 'a', 'c']
    ['Ali', 'Daniyal', 'Hina', 'Omar', 'Zara'] ['Omar', 'Zara'] ['Ali'] ['Ali', 'Daniyal', 'Hina'] True True True ['Ali', 'Hina', 'Omar', 'X', 'Y', 'Zara'] ['b', 'a', 'c']
    Figure 8.1 — Venn intuition (A={1,2,3}, B={3,4})
    A − B1, 2
    only left
    A ∩ B3
    both
    B − A4
    only right
    A △ B1, 2, 4
    exactly one
    A∪B = {1,2,3,4} · operators: | & - ^ · methods: union/intersection/difference/symmetric_difference (methods accept ANY iterable; operators need sets)

    Step-by-step (why dict.fromkeys dedups in order). dict.fromkeys(items) builds {"b": None, "a": None, "c": None} — dicts ignore duplicate keys (keeping first-insertion position) and preserve insertion order (guaranteed 3.7+). list(…) takes the keys → ['b','a','c']. O(n), order-stable — strictly better than list(set(items)) whenever order matters (which is usually).

    sets in data jobs: overlap, coverage, invalid detection 
    required = {"name", "age", "email"}
row = {"name": "Ali", "age": 30}
missing = required - row.keys()      # keys-view supports set ops!
print("missing:", missing)           # {'email'}
valid_cities = {"Lahore", "Karachi", "Islamabad"}
seen = {"Lahore", "Paris", "Karachi"}
print("invalid:", seen - valid_cities)  # {'Paris'} — instant data validation
print("coverage:", sorted(seen & valid_cities))  # sorted: set ORDER varies per run!
# frozenset: the IMMUTABLE (hashable!) set — for set-valued dict keys / constants:
fs = frozenset([1, 2, 2])
print(fs, hash(fs) is not None)  # frozenset({1, 2}) True
    missing: {'email'} invalid: {'Paris'} coverage: ['Karachi', 'Lahore'] frozenset({1, 2}) True
    ⚠️ Set trapsIteration order is arbitrary (never index/assume — sort first for stable output) · {} is a dict; set() is empty set · elements must be hashable ({[1]} → TypeError) · remove(x) raises if absent (use discard for "ensure absent") · mutating methods return None (s = s.add(x) → None disaster, same as sort).

    8.3 Dictionaries: the king of data structures

    What. A dict maps hashable keys to arbitrary values: {"name": "Ali", "age": 30}. Mutable, insertion-ordered (guaranteed 3.7+), average O(1) get/set/delete via hashing. Keys: almost always str/int/tuple (hashable immutables). Values: anything, including nested dicts/lists.

    Why dicts exist (five jobs). (1) Records — user["name"] beats user[0] (labels beat positions). (2) Lookups/mappings — id→name, code→price. (3) Counters/histograms — word→count (or Counter). (4) Caches/memo tables — args→result (lru_cache automates this, Ch. 20). (5) JSON-shaped data — APIs, configs, documents (Chapter 15: JSON ↔ dict is nearly 1:1).

    Intuition: hashing in 60 seconds. hash("name") scrambles the key into a huge integer; modulo table-size picks a bucket; the entry lives there. Lookup re-hashes and jumps straight to the bucket — no scanning. That's the O(1): compute where it must be, instead of searching. Collisions (two keys, one bucket) are resolved internally (open addressing in CPython) — you never see them, except as the reason worst-case is O(n) but average-case O(1). And now the key rule is obvious: if a key mutated after insertion, its hash would point to the wrong bucket — hence keys must be immutable.

    dict essentials: access, defaults, merge, remove, views 
    user = {"name": "Ali", "age": 30}
print(user["name"])               # direct — KeyError if missing (loud = good sometimes)
print(user.get("city"))           # None — safe probe (never raises)
print(user.get("city", "Lahore")) # default when missing (NOT stored)
user["city"] = "Lahore"           # add / overwrite (same syntax!)
user.setdefault("tags", []).append("vip")  # get-or-CREATE, then use — atomic idiom
print(user)
user.update({"age": 31, "vip": True})  # bulk merge (overwrites age, adds vip)
print(user.pop("vip"))            # remove AND return → True
print(user.pop("ghost", None))    # safe pop with default (no KeyError)
del user["city"]                  # remove (KeyError if missing)
# VIEWS: live windows, not copies — reflect later changes, support set ops on keys:
print(user.keys(), user.values(), user.items())
user["new"] = 1
print(user.keys())  # dict_keys([...,'new']) — the view UPDATED (it's live!)
    Ali None Lahore {'name': 'Ali', 'age': 30, 'city': 'Lahore', 'tags': ['vip']} True None dict_keys(['name', 'age', 'tags']) dict_values(['Ali', 31, ['vip']]) dict_items([('name', 'Ali'), ('age', 31), ('tags', ['vip'])]) dict_keys(['name', 'age', 'tags', 'new'])

    Step-by-step (setdefault). user.setdefault("tags", []): key missing → store the default [] under "tags" and return it; the .append("vip") then mutates that stored list. One lookup, atomic, no if "tags" not in user dance. Contrast .get(k, []) + append: appends to a temporary that's discarded — the classic "my append vanished" bug. (For grouping/counters at scale: collections.defaultdict / Counter below.)

    iteration patterns + nested dicts + comprehensions 
    prices = {"pen": 2.5, "book": 12.0, "bag": 30.0}
for k in prices:                  # keys (default)
    print(k, end=" ")
print()
for k, v in prices.items():       # key+value — THE standard loop
    print(f"{k} → {v}")
for v in prices.values():         # values only
    print(v, end=" ")
print()
# Nested: drill with chained [k], guard with .get chains:
students = {"s01": {"name": "Ali", "scores": [88, 92]},
            "s02": {"name": "Zara", "scores": [95, 90]}}
print(students["s01"]["scores"][0])              # 88 — drill down
print(students.get("s99", {}).get("name", "?"))  # ? — safe drill (no KeyError)
avg = {sid: sum(r["scores"])/len(r["scores"]) for sid, r in students.items()}
print(avg)  # {'s01': 90.0, 's02': 92.5} — dict comprehension (Ch. 13)
inv = {v: k for k, v in {"a": 1, "b": 2}.items()}  # invert → {1:'a', 2:'b'}
print(inv)
    pen book bag pen → 2.5 book → 12.0 bag → 30.0 2.5 12.0 30.0 88 ? {'s01': 90.0, 's02': 92.5} {1: 'a', 2: 'b'}
    counting + grouping: manual, then the stdlib upgrades 
    votes = ["a", "b", "a", "c", "a"]
counts = {}                                   # manual counter (know the pattern!)
for v in votes:
    counts[v] = counts.get(v, 0) + 1
print(counts)  # {'a': 3, 'b': 1, 'c': 1}
from collections import Counter, defaultdict
print(Counter(votes))           # Counter({'a': 3, 'b': 1, 'c': 1}) — + most_common!
print(Counter(votes).most_common(2))  # [('a', 3), ('b', 1)]
groups = defaultdict(list)      # missing key → auto-creates list()!
for w in ["apple", "ant", "bear", "bee"]:
    groups[w[0]].append(w)      # no setdefault needed
print(dict(groups))  # {'a': ['apple', 'ant'], 'b': ['bear', 'bee']}
# Merge (3.9+): | creates NEW, |= updates in place:
print({"a": 1} | {"b": 2})      # {'a': 1, 'b': 2}
    {'a': 3, 'b': 1, 'c': 1} Counter({'a': 3, 'b': 1, 'c': 1}) [('a', 3), ('b', 1)] {'a': ['apple', 'ant'], 'b': ['bear', 'bee']} {'a': 1, 'b': 2}
    🚫 Never resize a dict while iterating itfor k in d: del d[k] → RuntimeError: dictionary changed size during iteration. (Changing values of existing keys is fine; adding/removing keys is not.) Fixes: iterate list(d.items()) snapshot, collect keys-then-delete, or build a new dict: d = {k: v for k, v in d.items() if keep(k, v)}.
    ⚠️ More dict trapsd[k] on missing → KeyError (choose deliberately: loud [] vs safe .get vs creating .setdefault) · .get(k, []).append(x) appends to garbage (use setdefault/defaultdict) · views are live (snapshot with list() before sorting/storing) · update returns None (same mutator rule) · inversion loses data on duplicate values ({"a":1,"b":1} → {1:"b"} — last wins silently!).

    8.4 Hashing, equality contracts, and performance

    The contract (interview-grade). (1) Objects that compare == must have equal hashes (hash(1) == hash(1.0) == hash(True) — and indeed {1, 1.0, True} collapses to {1}!). (2) Hashes must be stable for the object's lifetime as a key — hence immutability. (3) Unequal objects should usually differ in hash (collisions only cost speed, never correctness).

    Operationlisttupleset / dict
    Index/key accessO(1)O(1)O(1) average (hash)
    Search (in)O(n) scanO(n) scanO(1) average (hash)
    Append/add/insertO(1)* amortized— (immutable)O(1) average
    Delete by value/keyO(n)—O(1) average
    Memory per itemLow (dense array)LowestHigher (sparse table + hashes)

    Debugging lens. "My key is right there but lookup fails!" → the key's hash or equality differs from what you think: hidden whitespace ("Lahore " vs "Lahore"), type mismatch (1 vs "1" — note 1 == "1" is False so hashes differ), float dust, or (custom classes) broken __hash__/__eq__ (Chapter 18). Diagnose: print(repr(key)) for both sides, compare type()s.

    8.5 The definitive chooser (+ when to graduate beyond builtins)

    NeedUseWhyGraduate to…
    Labels/keys, records, JSON, counters, cachesdictO(1) lookup by keydataclass (fixed-schema records, Ch. 18) · Pandas (Part 2)
    Uniqueness, fast membership, group mathsetO(1) membership; | & - ^Counter (multisets) · DB UNIQUE/indexes
    Fixed record, safety, dict key, multi-returntupleImmutable = hashable + shape-guaranteednamedtuple/dataclass (named fields)
    Ordered + growing/shrinking/reorderinglistGeneral sequence, rich methodsdeque (queues) · NumPy arrays (numeric scale)
    ✅ Data-Science connectionJSON APIs deserialize to dicts; CSV rows become dicts (csv.DictReader, Ch. 15); group-by aggregates are dicts (region → total); feature vocabularies are dicts (word → index); label sets are sets; coordinates/shapes are tuples. Pandas Series/DataFrame are, conceptually, dicts + arrays with superpowers — every method there (value_counts ≈ Counter, groupby ≈ defaultdict) echoes this chapter.

    8.6 Interview questions

    Q1: Why must dict keys be immutable/hashable? What breaks otherwise?
    Dicts hash keys to storage buckets. A key mutating after insertion would hash to a different bucket — lookups would miss an entry that's "right there", and the table would corrupt. Hence: immutable keys (str/int/tuple-of-immutables) with stable hashes. Demo: try {[1]: "x"} → TypeError: unhashable.
    Q2: d[k] vs .get() vs .setdefault() vs defaultdict — when each?
    d[k]: missing is a bug — crash loudly (fail fast). .get(k, default): missing is routine — probe without storing. .setdefault: get-or-create atomically (grouping into plain dicts). defaultdict(factory): repeated get-or-create with a factory (cleaner grouping/counters at scale).
    Q3: How do dicts achieve O(1) lookup? What's the worst case?
    Hash the key → bucket index → jump directly (open addressing in CPython). Average O(1); worst case O(n) under pathological hash collisions (all keys one bucket) — vanishingly rare with real hashes; CPython also randomizes string hashing (PYTHONHASHSEED) against collision attacks.
    Q4: set vs list for membership testing — complexity and the crossover?
    in on list: O(n) scan. On set: O(1) hash. For repeated lookups, convert once (set(big), O(n) build) then enjoy O(1) each — §5.6 measured ~2500× at n=200k. One-off single lookup on small data: list is fine (no build cost).

    8.7 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Convert [("a",1),("b",2)] to a dict two ways (dict(), comprehension); convert back to a sorted key list. Explain each step.
    2. On paper, Venn-sketch | & - ^ for A={1,2,3,4}, B={3,4,5}; verify in code (sort outputs for stable comparison).
    3. Show three setdefault uses (grouping, counting-init, config-default) and rewrite one with defaultdict.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. Group words by first letter with setdefault; then with defaultdict; then as a one-pass dict comprehension over sorted(set(...)). Compare readability.
    2. Invert {"a":1,"b":2,"c":1} two ways: lossy ({1:?...} — which key survives and why?) and lossless ({1: ["a","c"], 2: ["b"]}).
    3. Validate CSV-header coverage: required - set(header) for missing, set(header) - required - optional for unexpected. Report both.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. First non-repeating character in O(n) (Counter + order pass), then all duplicates in O(n), then top-k frequent in O(n log k) (heapq) — three solutions, three complexity arguments.
    2. Implement a tiny hash table (list of buckets + chaining) supporting __setitem__/__getitem__; demonstrate a collision and how chaining resolves it. (Demystifies dicts permanently.)

    9
    Control flow

    Conditional Logic

    ▾

    What/why. Conditionals let programs decide: execute different code for different data. if/elif/else evaluates conditions top-down and runs the first True branch — at most one branch ever runs. Combined with truthiness (every object's inherent True/False nature, §4.4) and short-circuiting (§5.5), conditions stay expressive without verbosity. This chapter also teaches the design of branching: guard clauses that flatten nesting, the ternary for value-choices, and match for structural dispatch.

    9.1 if / elif / else: first-True-wins (with execution traces)

    What. if COND: opens a chain; zero or more elif COND: alternatives follow; an optional else: catches "nothing matched". Semantics: test each condition in order; execute the first True branch's block; skip everything else in the chain — including later conditions (which never even evaluate).

    grading chain, traced 
    score = 82
if score >= 90:        # 82>=90? No → skip block AND move on
    grade = "A"
elif score >= 80:      # 82>=80? YES → run block, EXIT whole chain
    grade = "B"
elif score >= 70:      # NEVER TESTED (chain already exited!)
    grade = "C"
else:                   # skipped (a branch ran)
    grade = "F"
print(grade)  # B
    B

    Step-by-step. Condition 1 False → its block skipped, chain continues. Condition 2 True → block runs (grade = "B"), chain exits immediately — condition 3 is never evaluated (prove it: put 1/0 in an elif after a True branch — no crash!). else runs only when no condition was True. Consequences: (1) order matters — put the most specific/highest-threshold first (testing >= 80 before >= 90 would swallow all A-students into B!); (2) overlapping conditions make later branches dead code — a linter-flagged smell.

    order matters: the swallowed-A bug 
    def grade_bad(s):
    if s >= 80:      # ❌ catches 95 too — everything ≥80 is "B"!
        return "B"
    elif s >= 90:    # DEAD CODE — unreachable (any ≥90 already returned)
        return "A"
    return "F"

def grade_good(s):
    if s >= 90:      # ✅ specific first, general later
        return "A"
    elif s >= 80:
        return "B"
    return "F"

print(grade_bad(95), grade_good(95))  # B A — same conditions, different ORDER
    B A

    Nesting (and its limits). if blocks can contain further ifs for two-dimensional decisions — but each level multiplies the reader's mental state (2 levels = 4 paths, 3 = 8…). Rule: 2 levels occasionally, 3 levels = refactor (guard clauses §9.4, lookup tables, or extracted functions).

    nested: outer gate, inner detail 
    age, member = 25, True
if age >= 18:            # outer: which price TABLE?
    if member:            # inner: which ROW?
        price = 8
    else:
        price = 12
else:
    price = 5
print(price)  # 8
    8

    9.2 Truthiness mastery: the complete rulebook

    What. In boolean context (if, while, and/or/not, ternary, filter), Python calls bool(obj): False for the falsy list, True otherwise. Falsy — the complete, exact list: False, None, numeric zeros (0, 0.0, 0j, Decimal(0), Fraction(0)), empties ("", b"", [], {}, set(), frozenset(), range(0), ()), and class instances whose __bool__ returns False or __len__ returns 0. Everything else — including "False", "0", [0], {"a": 0}, generators, and all custom objects by default — is truthy.

    idiomatic conditions vs verbose traps 
    items, name, age, resp = [], "Ali", 0, "y"
if items: print("has items")       # ✅ idiomatic — NOT len(items) > 0
else: print("empty cart")
if name: print("named")            # ✅ — NOT name != ""
if age is not None:                # ✅ 0 is VALID data! NEVER `if age:` here
    print("age known:", age)
if resp in ("y", "yes"):           # ✅ — NOT == "y" or "yes" (always-True bug!)
    print("confirmed")
if (n := len(items)) == 0:         # walrus when you need the value too
    print(f"cart has {n} items")
    empty cart named age known: 0 confirmed cart has 0 items
    ⚠️ The five truthiness traps (memorize)① if x == True: → if x: (and == True fails for truthy non-bools like 1… actually 1 == True is True via int-equality — but "a" == True is False while if "a": passes! — inconsistent, hence banned). ② if age/count/qty: when zero is valid → is not None. ③ if s == "a" or "b": always True → in ("a","b"). ④ bool(user_string) for flags (§3.6 — "False" is truthy!). ⑤ Generators are always truthy (even empty ones — truthiness doesn't consume!) — test emptiness with next(gen, sentinel), never if gen:.

    9.3 Combining conditions: and/or/not + De Morgan + readability

    compound conditions, done readably 
    age, member, banned = 25, True, False
# ✅ Positive, grouped, short-circuited (cheap tests first!):
if age >= 18 and member and not banned:
    print("welcome, member")
# De Morgan's laws — push NOT inward by flipping and↔or:
#   not (A and B) == (not A) or (not B)
#   not (A or B)  == (not A) and (not B)
x = 5
print(not (x > 0 and x < 10))     # False
print(not (x > 0) or not (x < 10))  # False — same (De Morgan)
# Readability: name complex conditions!
is_working_age = 18 <= age < 65   # chained comparison (§5.4)
is_eligible = is_working_age and member and not banned
if is_eligible:
    print("eligible")
    welcome, member False False eligible

    Best practices. Order and-operands cheap-first, likely-to-fail-first (short-circuit skips the expensive rest: if cheap(x) and costly(x):). Prefer positive conditions (if valid: over if not invalid: — negation taxes every reader). Name any condition needing a comment — the name is the comment. Parenthesize mixed and/or (precedence §5.3).

    9.4 Guard clauses: flatten the pyramid of doom

    What. A guard clause handles an edge case with an early return/continue/raise at the top, leaving the main logic flat. It inverts nesting: instead of "if valid: [20 lines] else: error", write "if invalid: error-and-exit; [20 flat lines]".

    ❌ Pyramid of doom (3 levels, happy path buried)
    def process(rows):
      if rows:
        if valid(rows):
          if len(rows) < MAX:
            return analyze(rows)
          else: return "too many"
        else: return "invalid"
      else: return "empty"
    ✅ Guards (flat, happy path last and obvious)
    def process(rows):
      if not rows: return "empty"
      if not valid(rows): return "invalid"
      if len(rows) >= MAX: return "too many"
      return analyze(rows)
    Each line eliminates one case; survivors reach the goal.

    Why guards win. Readers process one eliminated case per line instead of holding nested context; each error sits beside its condition (no scrolling to match elses); adding a new case is one line, not re-indentation surgery. Apply the same shape to loops (if bad: continue) and validation (if bad: raise … — Chapter 14).

    9.5 Ternary + dispatch tables + match

    Ternary (A if COND else B) chooses between two values (not blocks):

    ternary: value-choice, not control flow 
    score = 78
status = "pass" if score >= 60 else "fail"   # ✅ one value-choice
print(status)
label = "even" if score % 2 == 0 else "odd"
print(f"{score} is {label}")
# Nested ternary — legal but SMELLY (use if/elif for 3+ outcomes):
grade = "A" if score >= 90 else ("B" if score >= 80 else "C")
print(grade)  # C
# Dispatch TABLE beats long elif-chains for value mapping:
fmt_handlers = {"csv": "parse commas", "json": "parse braces", "xml": "parse tags"}
print(fmt_handlers.get("json", "unknown format"))  # parse braces — no chain at all!
    pass 78 is even C parse braces

    Structural matching (match/case, 3.10+). For shape-based dispatch — different types/structures/patterns, with variable binding:

    match: dispatch + destructuring 
    def describe(value):
    match value:
        case None:
            return "missing"
        case True | False:          # OR-pattern (bool BEFORE int — §4.4!)
            return "flag"
        case int() | float():       # TYPE pattern
            return "number"
        case {"name": str(n)}:      # MAPPING pattern + bind n
            return f"user {n}"
        case [first, *rest]:        # SEQUENCE pattern + bind
            return f"list starting {first} (+{len(rest)} more)"
        case _:
            return "something else"

for v in [None, True, 3.5, {"name": "Ali"}, [1, 2, 3], "hi"]:
    print(repr(v), "→", describe(v))
    None → missing True → flag 3.5 → number {'name': 'Ali'} → user Ali [1, 2, 3] → list starting 1 (+2 more) 'hi' → something else

    When each. Ternary: one condition, two values, fits on a line. Dict-dispatch: many fixed keys → values/functions (data-driven, extensible without code changes). match: shapes/types/structures with binding (parsers, ASTs, API responses, command routers). if/elif: general boolean logic. Nested ternaries and match for two plain booleans are both over-engineering — match the tool to the shape.

    9.6 DS-flavored branching + debugging conditions

    realistic data checks 
    def bucket_age(age):
    if age is None:                 # missing FIRST (None breaks comparisons!)
        return "unknown"
    if not isinstance(age, (int, float)) or isinstance(age, bool):
        return "invalid"            # bool excluded: True is 1 ( §4.4)!
    if age < 0 or age > 130:
        return "implausible"
    if age < 13:
        return "child"
    if age < 20:
        return "teen"
    return "adult"                  # flat guards — no elif needed with returns!

print([bucket_age(a) for a in [None, True, -5, 8, 16, 30, "x"]])
    ['unknown', 'invalid', 'implausible', 'child', 'teen', 'adult', 'invalid']
    🛠️ Debugging conditionsWrong branch taken? (1) Print the condition values, not your assumptions: print(f"{age=!r}, {type(age)=}") (!r shows hidden spaces/quotes). (2) Test each sub-condition separately. (3) Suspect types: "25" >= 18 → TypeError in Py3 (no str↔int comparison — convert first!). (4) Suspect None (None > 0 → TypeError — guard None first, as above).

    9.7 Interview questions

    Q1: How does if/elif/else execute? Why does order matter? What never evaluates?
    Top-down, first-True-branch-wins, at most one runs. Order matters because earlier branches swallow overlapping cases (§9.1's swallowed-A demo — dead code below). Conditions after a True branch never evaluate (side effects skipped — exploitable for guards, dangerous if you relied on them).
    Q2: List all falsy values. Why is `if items:` better than `if len(items) > 0:`? What's the generator exception?
    False, None, numeric zeros, empty str/bytes/containers, range(0), __bool__/__len__-falsy objects. if items: is shorter, faster (no call), and generic. Exception: generators are always truthy regardless of emptiness — probe with next(g, sentinel).
    Q3: What are guard clauses and why do professionals prefer them?
    Early-exit handling of edge cases (return/continue/raise) that flattens nesting. Benefits: one case per line, errors beside conditions, happy path prominent at the end, new cases = one line. The alternative pyramid buries logic and complicates diffs.
    Q4: Ternary vs if/else vs dict-dispatch vs match — when each?
    Ternary: single value-choice on one line. if/else: general logic/blocks. Dict-dispatch: fixed key→value/function mapping (data-driven, no chain). match: structural patterns with binding (types, shapes, sequences, mappings). Nested ternaries and match-for-booleans are smells.

    9.8 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Fix if resp == "y" or "yes": and prove the original is always-True with resp = "no". Explain the parse.
    2. Write correct checks for: non-empty list, provided-but-zero age, non-blank string (strip first!), non-None config. Explain each choice.
    3. Trace the §9.1 chain with scores 95/85/75/65, listing every evaluated condition and skipped block.
    Level 2 · Apply

    2. Rewrite a 20-line nested validator as guard clauses; count indent levels before/after.
    3. Build a match router for API payloads: {"op": "add", "args": [...]}, {"op": "get", "key": …}, malformed → error dict. Bind and use the variables.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Design bucket_age (§9.6) for a hostile reviewer: defend every branch's order (None? bool? type? range?) and name what breaks if two branches swap.
    2. Refactor a 5-branch elif value-mapper into dict-dispatch with a default; argue when you'd switch back to elif (hint: non-hashable keys, range conditions, side effects).

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: first-True-wins (order specific→general; later conditions never evaluated) · truthiness complete-list + 5 traps · and/or cheap-first, name complex conditions, De Morgan to simplify · guards flatten pyramids · ternary for values, dict-dispatch for maps, match for shapes · guard None before comparing. Next: Chapter 10 — loops at full depth: for/while mastery, break/continue/else, nested loops, and knowing when not to loop at all.
    10
    Control flow

    Loops

    ▾

    What/why. Loops repeat work: for iterates over a known collection ("for each row…"), while repeats until a condition changes ("while retries remain…"). Python's for is a for-each over iterables (Chapter 19's protocol) — there is no C-style index loop, because enumerate/range cover those cases readably. The senior skill has two halves: choosing the right iteration tool (range/enumerate/zip/dict-views/generators) and recognizing when a vectorized operation or builtin should replace the loop entirely (the Ch. 24 ladder).

    10.1 for: the for-each philosophy + range, enumerate, zip

    What. for VAR in ITERABLE: binds VAR to each item in turn and runs the block. The loop variable leaks (it survives after the loop, holding the last item — §10.5). Loop over values directly; reach for indices only when mutation-by-position or parallel structure demands it.

    iteration toolkit, completely 
    for i in range(3):            # range(stop): 0,1,2 — LAZY (O(1) memory!)
    print(i, end=" ")
print()
for i in range(2, 11, 3):     # range(start, stop-EXCLUSIVE, step)
    print(i, end=" ")
print()
for i in range(5, 0, -1):     # countdown (negative step)
    print(i, end=" ")
print()
names = ["Ali", "Zara"]
for i, n in enumerate(names, start=1):  # index + value (BEST general loop)
    print(f"{i}. {n}")
for n, s in zip(names, [88, 95]):       # PARALLEL — STOPS AT SHORTEST!
    print(n, s)
for n, s in zip(names, [88]):           # Zara silently DROPPED (length mismatch!)
    print("short:", n, s)
for k, v in {"a": 1, "b": 2}.items():   # dicts via .items() (never index a dict!)
    print(k, v)
    0 1 2 2 5 8 5 4 3 2 1 1. Ali 2. Zara Ali 88 Zara 95 short: Ali 88 a 1 b 2

    Step-by-step (range laziness). range(10**9) stores three integers (start/stop/step, ~48 bytes) and computes each value on demand — it never materializes a billion-item list. list(range(10**9)) would attempt exactly that (~8 GB → MemoryError/death). Rule: range is for counting/looping; only list() it when tiny and genuinely needed as data.

    zip` strictness (3.10+). Silent truncation hides length-mismatch bugs (dropped students, misaligned columns!). zip(a, b, strict=True) raises ValueError on unequal lengths — use it whenever inputs must align (parallel columns, paired tests). For intentionally-uneven pairing, itertools.zip_longest fills gaps with a fillvalue` (Chapter 20).

    ⚠️ Loop anti-patterns (unlearn these)for i in range(len(xs)): x = xs[i] → for x in xs: (direct!) or enumerate (need index). i = 0; for x in xs: …; i += 1 → enumerate. while i < len(xs): … i += 1 → for. Manual indexing is slower, longer, and off-by-one-prone — the for-each exists to delete it.

    10.2 while: condition-driven repetition + guaranteed progress

    What. while COND: tests before every round (including the first — possibly zero rounds) and repeats while True. Correctness demands guaranteed progress: something in the body must move the state toward falsifying the condition, on every possible path (including continue-skipped ones — §10.3).

    while patterns: countdown, retry-bounded, sentinel, Newton 
    # 1. Countdown (progress: attempts decreases every round)
attempts = 3
while attempts > 0:
    print("try, left:", attempts)
    attempts -= 1
# 2. Bounded retry (progress: tries increases; CAP prevents infinity)
tries, ok = 0, False
while not ok and tries < 5:
    tries += 1
    ok = (tries == 3)      # pretend flaky work succeeds on try 3
print("succeeded after", tries)
# 3. Sentinel processing (progress: items consumed)
items = [3, 1, 0, 9]
while items:
    x = items.pop(0)       # shrinks every round → terminates (O(n²) though — deque!)
    if x == 0:
        print("hit zero, stop"); break
    print("saw", x)
# 4. Converge (progress: guess improves; tolerance + cap BOTH required!)
guess, target, iters = 10.0, 2.0, 0
while abs(guess*guess - target) > 1e-9 and iters < 100:  # float==never! tolerance!
    guess = (guess + target/guess) / 2   # Newton step for sqrt
    iters += 1
print("sqrt(2)≈", guess, "in", iters, "iters")
    try, left: 3 try, left: 2 try, left: 1 succeeded after 3 saw 3 saw 1 hit zero, stop sqrt(2)≈ 1.4142135623730954 in 7 iters

    Why while True + break is legitimate (with discipline). For "loop until something inside decides" (event loops, input validation, servers), testing at the top is awkward — while True: with clearly-visible breaks reads better. Discipline: every while True needs an obviously reachable break (preferably first-screen visible) — and for anything that could spin forever, a trip-count/iteration cap or timeout.

    🛠️ Infinite-loop rescue + preventionHung? Ctrl+C (KeyboardInterrupt) — in notebooks, ⬛ Stop/Interrupt kernel. Then audit: ① does the condition variable change on every path (or does continue skip the update)? ② float equality that never hits (while x != 0.3: — use tolerance!)? ③ break actually reachable? Prevention: prefer bounded for (range(max_tries)) whenever a cap exists — bounded loops can't hang.

    10.3 break / continue / pass / else — the full control panel

    Figure 10.1 — Loop execution map
    test condition / fetch next item
    ↓ enter round
    continue → abandon round, jump to next test/fetch  ·  break → exit loop NOW  ·  pass → no-op placeholder
    ↓ loop finishes
    else runs ONLY if the loop ended WITHOUT break (natural exhaustion)
    control statements, each proven 
    # continue: skip noise, keep signal
for x in [1, -2, 3, -4, 5]:
    if x < 0:
        continue          # abandon THIS round (negatives skipped)
    print(x, end=" ")
print()
# break + for-else: search WITHOUT flag variables
nums = [5, 7, 9]
for n in nums:
    if n % 2 == 0:
        print("found even:", n)
        break             # exit NOW — else SKIPPED
else:                     # runs: loop exhausted WITHOUT break
    print("no even number")
# while-else: same rule (else skipped on break)
count = 0
while count < 3:
    count += 1
else:
    print("counted to", count)  # counted to 3
# pass: syntactic placeholder (empty block would be IndentationError)
for x in []:
    pass  # TODO: handle empty case later
    1 3 5 no even number counted to 3

    Step-by-step (for-else). Each round: even? No (5, 7, 9 all odd) → no break → fetch next. Iterator exhausts → loop ends naturally → else runs. Had any round hit break, control would jump past the else. Mnemonic: else = "no-break" (think nobreak). Uses: search-miss handling, prime testing (no divisor found), validation (no violation found) — all without a found = False flag.

    ⚠️ break/continue scope: ONE level onlyIn nested loops, break exits only the innermost loop. To exit multiple levels: extract a function and return (best), set a flag + break twice (meh), or raise a private exception (rare). If you need labeled-break, you need a function.

    10.4 Nested loops: pairs, grids, and the O(n²) price tag

    nested loops: pairs, table, early exit via function 
    # All pairs (i, j) with i != j — O(n²) rounds
vals = [1, 2, 3]
pairs = []
for a in vals:            # outer: n rounds
    for b in vals:        # inner: n rounds PER outer → n² total
        if a != b:
            pairs.append((a, b))
print(pairs)  # 6 pairs from n=3
# Multiplication table (nested + str.join formatting):
for r in range(1, 4):
    print(" ".join(f"{r*c:2d}" for c in range(1, 4)))
# Early multi-level exit: FUNCTION + return (the clean pattern)
def find_first_pair_summing_to(xs, target):
    for i, a in enumerate(xs):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(xs)):
            if a + xs[j] == target:
                return (i, j)   # exits BOTH loops instantly
    return None
print(find_first_pair_summing_to([2, 7, 11, 15], 9))  # (0, 1)
    [(1, 2), (1, 3), (2, 1), (2, 3), (3, 1), (3, 2)] 1 2 3 2 4 6 3 6 9 (0, 1)

    Cost intuition. n=100 → 10,000 inner rounds; n=10,000 → 100,000,000 (minutes). Nested loops over the same large data are the #1 accidental-quadratic (Chapter 24) — always ask: "can a set/dict (Ch. 8) replace the inner loop?" (§10.6's two-sum is the canonical rescue: O(n²) → O(n)).

    10.5 Loop variables leak + the iteration protocol (preview)

    leak demo + what for really does 
    for i in range(3):
    pass
print("leaked i =", i)   # 2 — for-targets bind in the ENCLOSING scope (no block scope!)
# Comprehension variables do NOT leak (3+): [j for j in range(3)] → j undefined after
# What `for x in xs:` ACTUALLY does (Chapter 19's protocol, previewed):
xs = [10, 20]
it = iter(xs)            # 1. ask for an iterator (cursor)
while True:              # 2. fetch until exhausted
    try:
        x = next(it)     # 3. next item (raises StopIteration at end)
    except StopIteration:
        break            # 4. for-loops catch this SILENTLY and exit
    print("item:", x)
    leaked i = 2 item: 10 item: 20

    Why this matters now. The leak explains "mystery variable" bugs (a stale i reused later — use fresh names or functions to contain loops). The protocol explains everything iterable: strings, files, generators, dicts, ranges, zips — all work in for because all answer iter()/next(). Chapter 19 turns this preview into full mastery (including writing your own iterables).

    10.6 When NOT to loop: the replacement ladder

    What. Explicit loops are the clearest tool for complex per-item logic — and the slowest for simple bulk transforms. Climb this ladder deliberately:

    RungFormWhen
    ① Explicit loopfor x in xs: …Complex logic, side effects, early exit, per-item errors
    ② Comprehension[f(x) for x in xs if p(x)]Transform + filter → new collection (Ch. 13)
    ③ Builtinsum / min / max / any / all / joinReductions — C-speed, zero bookkeeping
    ④ Stdlib algorithmCounter / groupby / heapq / bisectCounting, grouping, top-N, searching sorted data
    ⑤ Vectorized (Part 2)NumPy/Pandas opsNumeric bulk work at scale (10–100×, Ch. 24)
    climbing the ladder on one task (sum of even squares) 
    xs = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
total = 0                       # ① loop: clearest, slowest
for x in xs:
    if x % 2 == 0:
        total += x * x
print(total)                    # 20
print(sum(x*x for x in xs if x % 2 == 0))  # ②+③: generator + builtin — best pure-Python
# ⑤ vectorized (Part 2): import numpy as np; a = np.array(xs); print(((a[a%2==0])**2).sum())
    20 20
    ✅ Rule of thumbUnder ~10k items, write whatever is clearest (usually ①/②). Over ~100k in hot paths, climb to ③/④/⑤ — after profiling (Chapter 24). Premature vectorization of cold code wastes readability for zero user-visible gain.

    10.7 Interview questions

    Q1: What does for-else / while-else do? Give a use case that avoids a flag.
    else runs iff the loop exhausted without break (mnemonic: "no-break"). Use: search patterns — break on found, else = not-found handling. Example: prime test (break on divisor, else prime), validation (break on violation, else valid).
    Q2: range vs list(range) — memory, and the rule?
    range stores start/stop/step (~48B) and computes on demand — O(1) memory for any size. list(range(n)) materializes n objects — O(n) memory (billions = death). Rule: loop over range directly; list() only tiny ranges genuinely needed as data.
    Q3: How do you exit nested loops cleanly? Why not flag-chains?
    Extract a function and return (exits all levels instantly, names the search, testable). Flag + double-break works but litters logic and breaks down at 3 levels. (Python has no labeled break by design — functions are the answer.)
    Q4: Solve two-sum in O(n) and explain why nested loops lose.
    Single pass with a seen-dict: for each x, check if target - x was seen (O(1) hash); else store x. O(n) time, O(n) space. Nested loops re-scan the tail per element — O(n²) time (10k items: 100M ops vs 10k). General lesson: inner-loop scans → hash lookups.

    10.8 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Hand-trace list(range(1, 10, 3)), list(range(5, 0, -2)), list(range(0)); verify. Explain why range(3, 3) is empty.
    2. Rewrite a while countdown as for-over-range. Which is safer against infinity and why?
    3. Demonstrate the loop-variable leak; then show a comprehension variable NOT leaking. Explain (function scope vs comprehension scope — Ch. 12 preview).
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. FizzBuzz 1–30 as a loop, then as a comprehension building a list of strings. Which reads better? (Defend your answer.)
    2. find_first_even(nums) with for-else (no flags); all_positive(nums) with while-else. Test empty inputs — what SHOULD they return?
    3. zip(a, b, strict=True): show it catching a misalignment your silent zip hid. Then solve the same task with itertools.zip_longest.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Two-sum O(n) with dict (§10.7 Q4) + prove the complexity; then extend to return ALL pairs. What changes?
    2. Benchmark loop-sum vs generator+sum vs NumPy-sum at n=10⁶; place each on the §10.6 ladder with measured ratios.

    10.9 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build loop_lab.py. Implement FizzBuzz FIVE ways (loop, comprehension, generator+join, function-mapped, while) + a prime-finder with for-else + a bounded-retry input prompter (3 tries, then give up gracefully). Time all five FizzBuzzes at n=100k with time.perf_counter. The point isn't speed — it's proving you can reach any rung of the ladder on demand.

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: for = for-each (values direct; enumerate/zip/items; strict=True for alignment) · range lazy O(1) · while needs guaranteed progress + caps (tolerance for floats!) · break/continue one level; else = no-break · nested = O(n²) (hash the inner loop away) · loop vars leak · climb the replacement ladder deliberately. Next: Chapter 11 — functions, the deepest chapter: abstraction, arguments, returns, recursion, closures, and decorators-from-first-principles.
    11
    Abstraction · deepest chapter

    Functions

    ▾

    What/why. A function is a named, reusable, testable unit of logic: inputs (parameters) go in, work happens in an isolated scope, an output (return value) comes out. Functions exist to kill duplication (DRY — Don't Repeat Yourself), name ideas (a well-named call reads as English), isolate complexity (test/debug one thing at a time), and compose programs from trustworthy parts. Data science without functions is copy-pasted notebook chaos where a fixed bug resurrects in cell 34; with functions it becomes a reusable analysis library (Chapter 25). This is the deepest chapter because functions are the hinge of everything after it: scope (12), recursion/closures/decorators (here + 21), classes as function-bundles (18), testing (23), and every project.

    Figure 11.1 — Anatomy of a definition and a call
    def mean(xs): … ← DEFINITION: creates a function object, binds the name. Body runs ZERO times.
    ↓ call: mean([10, 20])
    arguments bound to parameters → fresh LOCAL frame pushed on the call stack
    ↓ body executes (names resolve LEGB — Ch. 12)
    return value → frame destroyed → value lands at the call site, execution resumes

    11.1 Define, call, return — and the None contract

    What. def NAME(params): + indented body + return EXPR. Calling (NAME(args)) runs the body with parameters bound to argument objects (references — §3.1, no copying). return exits immediately with a value; falling off the end (or bare return) returns None.

    define + call + return, with the object lesson 
    def mean(xs):
    """Return the arithmetic mean of a non-empty list."""
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)

print(mean([10, 20, 30]))  # 20.0 — the VALUE lands here
print(mean.__name__, callable(mean))  # mean True — no parens = the OBJECT itself (address varies!)
print(mean([10, 20, 30]) * 2)  # 40.0 — calls are EXPRESSIONS (nestable!)
result = print("hi")       # print DISPLAYS 'hi' but RETURNS None
print(result)              # None — display ≠ return ( §2.2's law, generalized)
def silent(xs):
    total = sum(xs)        # computes... and drops it (no return!)
print(silent([1, 2]))      # None — falling off the end returns None
    20.0 mean True 40.0 hi None None

    Step-by-step (mean([10, 20, 30])). ① The argument expression [10, 20, 30] evaluates to a list object. ② A new frame is pushed; parameter xs binds to that object (one reference — the list is not copied). ③ Body: sum(xs) → 60, len(xs) → 3, 60/3 → 20.0. ④ return 20.0: frame destroyed, 20.0 substituted at the call site. ⑤ Outer print displays it. Total: definition ran 0 times until called; each call gets a fresh, isolated frame (local variables never leak between calls).

    The return contract (professional law). Return values; print at the edges. A function that only prints gives the program nothing to compute with, test, or compose — its result evaporates into pixels. Structure: pure core functions return data; one thin outer layer (CLI main(), notebook cell) prints/displays. Every project in this book follows this shape — and Chapter 23's testability argument depends on it (you can't assert on printed pixels).

    ⚠️ Return trapsCode after return is dead (unreachable — linters flag it) · return inside a loop exits the whole function (not just the loop — use it for "found it!" (§10.4), fear it when you meant break) · inconsistent returns (return x on one path, implicit None on another) force every caller to None-check — return one type-shape from all paths or raise (Ch. 14).

    11.2 Parameters vs arguments: positional, keyword, defaults

    What. Parameters live in the definition (placeholders: def train(model, epochs)). Arguments live in the call (actual objects: train("forest", 50)). Binding is positional-or-keyword by default: pass by order (positional) or by name (keyword, any order). Defaults (epochs=10) make parameters optional — evaluated once at def time (this single fact causes §11.3's famous trap).

    argument styles, completely 
    def train(model="linear", epochs=10, verbose=True):
    return f"{model}: {epochs} epochs, verbose={verbose}"

print(train())                        # all defaults
print(train("forest"))                # positional (by order)
print(train(epochs=50, model="mlp"))  # keyword (any order, self-documenting)
print(train("mlp", verbose=False))    # mixed: positionals FIRST, then keywords
# print(train(model="a", "b"))        # SyntaxError: positional after keyword!
# print(train("a", "b", "c", "d"))    # TypeError: too many positional args
# print(train(speed=5))               # TypeError: unexpected keyword 'speed'
    linear: 10 epochs, verbose=True forest: 10 epochs, verbose=True mlp: 50 epochs, verbose=True mlp: 10 epochs, verbose=False

    Rules (exact). In calls: all positionals before all keywords; each parameter bound at most once (f(1, x=2) with def f(x) → "multiple values for argument"). In definitions: non-default parameters before defaulted ones (def f(a=1, b): → SyntaxError). Keywords at call sites are documentation that executes: dropna(inplace=True) vs dropna(True) — the first is self-explaining; the second sends readers hunting.

    🐍 The mutable-default trap (top-10 interview question)def f(xs=[]): xs.append(1); return xs — the default list is created once, at def time, and shared across all calls that omit it: f() → [1], then f() → [1, 1]. The function "remembers" — spooky, stateful, bug-breeding. Fix (memorize): def f(xs=None): xs = [] if xs is None else xs — fresh list per call. Generalize: defaults must be immutable (None, 0, "", (), frozenset()).
    mutable default: the bug + the fix, side by side 
    def buggy(item, bag=[]):      # ❌ ONE list shared by all default-calls
    bag.append(item)
    return bag
print(buggy("a"))  # ['a']
print(buggy("b"))  # ['a', 'b'] — LEAKED across calls!

def fixed(item, bag=None):    # ✅ fresh list per call
    if bag is None:
        bag = []
    bag.append(item)
    return bag
print(fixed("a"))  # ['a']
print(fixed("b"))  # ['b'] — independent ✓
print(buggy.__defaults__)  # (['a', 'b'],) — the shared object, red-handed!
    ['a'] ['a', 'b'] ['a'] ['b'] (['a', 'b'],)

    11.3 *args and **kwargs: flexible signatures + call-site unpacking

    What. In definitions: *args collects extra positional arguments into a tuple; **kwargs collects extra keyword arguments into a dict. Parameters after *args become keyword-only (must be passed by name). In calls, */** do the reverse: unpack a sequence/dict into separate arguments.

    flexible signatures, completely 
    def report(title, *args, scale=1, **kwargs):
    # title: required (positional-or-keyword)
    # *args: extra positionals → TUPLE (possibly empty)
    # scale: KEYWORD-ONLY (sits after *args — must pass scale=...)
    # **kwargs: extra keywords → DICT
    return {"title": title, "args": args, "scale": scale, "meta": kwargs}

print(report("sales", 10, 20, scale=2, region="PK", q="Q1"))
# Unpacking at CALL time:
vals = [1, 2, 3]
print(max(*vals))                 # max(1, 2, 3) → 3
print(*vals, sep="-")            # 1-2-3 — print each element!
cfg = {"sep": "-", "end": "!\n"}
print("a", "b", **cfg)           # a-b! — dict unpacks to keywords
merged = {**{"a": 1}, **{"b": 2}}  # {a:1, b:2} — dict merge via unpacking
print(merged, [*vals, 4])        # [1,2,3,4] — list building via unpacking
    {'title': 'sales', 'args': (10, 20), 'scale': 2, 'meta': {'region': 'PK', 'q': 'Q1'}} 3 1-2-3 a-b! {'a': 1, 'b': 2} [1, 2, 3, 4]

    When to use each. *args/**kwargs: wrappers and decorators (def wrapper(*a, **k): …; return fn(*a, **k) — forwards any signature, Chapter 21), extensible APIs (accept-and-forward options to a backend), truly variadic functions (max(), print()). Keyword-only (after */*args): config/boolean flags that must be named (sort(reverse=True)-style clarity enforced). Bare * forces keyword-only with no variadics (def f(a, *, b)). Positional-only (/, 3.8+): library APIs whose parameter names aren't contractual (def f(a, b, /, c) — callers can't write a=).

    keyword-only, bare *, positional-only 
    def strict(a, b, /, c, *, d):
    # a, b: POSITIONAL-ONLY (before /) — f(a=1,...) → TypeError
    # c: normal (either way)
    # d: KEYWORD-ONLY (after *) — f(..., 4) positionally → TypeError
    return (a, b, c, d)
print(strict(1, 2, 3, d=4))     # ✓
print(strict(1, 2, c=3, d=4))   # ✓
# print(strict(a=1, b=2, c=3, d=4))  # TypeError: positional-only!
def save(path, *, overwrite=False, encoding="utf-8"):  # bare * — flags must be named
    return (path, overwrite, encoding)
print(save("f.csv", overwrite=True))
    (1, 2, 3, 4) (1, 2, 3, 4) ('f.csv', True, 'utf-8')

    11.4 Scope essentials, docstrings, and type hints

    Scope in one paragraph (full story: Chapter 12). Parameters and body-variables are local: born at call, dead at return, invisible outside. Reading a name searches Local → Enclosing → Global → Builtin (LEGB); assigning always binds locally unless declared global/nonlocal. Consequence: a function can't accidentally clobber your variables — and you can't "reach in" to grab its locals. Design through the signature: data enters via parameters, exits via return — nothing else crosses the boundary.

    Docstrings (mandatory for non-trivial functions). The triple-quoted string as the body's first statement becomes fn.__doc__ — shown by help(fn), IDEs, and doc generators. Format: one-line summary, then Args/Returns/Raises as needed (Google/NumPy style — pick one per project).

    Type hints (cheap, high-value documentation). def pct(part: float, whole: float) -> float: — hints are ignored at runtime (zero cost, zero enforcement) but read by humans, IDEs (autocomplete, inline errors), and mypy (a static checker that finds type bugs before running). Hint all public signatures; hint locals only where unclear.

    documented + hinted function (the professional shape) 
    def pct(part: float, whole: float) -> float:
    """Return part as a percentage of whole.

    Args:
        part: The portion (must be within [0, whole]).
        whole: The total (must be non-zero).

    Returns:
        The percentage, e.g. pct(3, 4) == 75.0.

    Raises:
        ZeroDivisionError: if whole is 0.
    """
    return 100 * part / whole

print(pct(3, 4))
print(pct.__doc__.splitlines()[0])  # first line — tools show this
help(pct)  # full beauty — try it in the REPL!
from typing import get_type_hints
print(get_type_hints(pct))  # {'part': float, 'whole': float, 'return': float}
    75.0 Return part as a percentage of whole. Help on function pct in module __main__: pct(part: float, whole: float) -> float Return part as a percentage of whole. ... {'part': <class 'float'>, 'whole': <class 'float'>, 'return': <class 'float'>}

    11.5 lambda: anonymous one-expression functions (and their limits)

    What. lambda PARAMS: EXPRESSION creates a function object with no name, whose body is a single expression (no statements, no annotations, auto-returned). Use: throwaway callables passed to key=, map/filter/sorted, callbacks, tiny dispatch.

    lambda: right uses + the late-binding trap 
    users = [("Ali", 30), ("Zara", 25)]
users.sort(key=lambda u: u[1])   # ✅ throwaway key — lambda's home turf
print(users)
print(sorted(["bb", "a", "ccc"], key=lambda s: (len(s), s)))  # tuple keys!
# ✅ dispatch table of tiny ops:
ops = {"add": lambda a, b: a + b, "mul": lambda a, b: a * b}
print(ops["mul"](3, 4))  # 12
# ❌ ANTI-PATTERNS:
# square = lambda x: x**2   → use `def square(x): return x**2` (PEP 8: name it! no annotations, worse tracebacks)
# THE late-binding trap: lambdas capture VARIABLES, not values!
funcs = [lambda: i for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in funcs])  # [2, 2, 2] — NOT [0,1,2]! all share final i=2
fixed = [lambda i=i: i for i in range(3)]  # default binds CURRENT value
print([f() for f in fixed])  # [0, 1, 2] ✓
    [('Zara', 25), ('Ali', 30)] ['a', 'bb', 'ccc'] 12 [2, 2, 2] [0, 1, 2]

    Step-by-step (late binding). Each lambda: i stores a reference to the variable i (the loop's single binding — §10.5's leak!), not a snapshot. After the loop, i == 2; calling any lambda reads i now → 2, three times. The i=i default evaluates at definition time (per iteration), freezing each value. Same trap bites def closures in loops (§11.7) — one mechanism, both constructs.

    11.6 Recursion: self-similar problems, base cases, and limits

    What. Recursion solves a problem by having the function call itself on a smaller subproblem, until reaching a base case solvable directly. Every recursion needs: (1) base case(s) that stop, (2) a recursive step that shrinks toward them, (3) combination of sub-results. Missing any → infinite recursion → RecursionError (CPython caps ~1000 frames — sys.getrecursionlimit()).

    Intuition. Russian dolls with instructions: "to open doll N: open doll N−1, then handle your layer" — plus "doll 0: just hand over the prize" (base case). The call stack is the pile of half-opened dolls: each frame waits while deeper frames finish, then resumes. Trace any recursion as down (calls, shrinking) then up (returns, combining).

    factorial: trace down, then up 
    def factorial(n):
    print("  " * (5-n) + f"call fact({n})")
    if n <= 1:                       # BASE CASE — the stopper (without: RecursionError)
        print("  " * (5-n) + "→ base: return 1")
        return 1
    sub = factorial(n - 1)            # SHRINK toward base
    result = n * sub                  # COMBINE on the way up
    print("  " * (5-n) + f"→ return {n}*{sub}={result}")
    return result

print("answer:", factorial(5))
    call fact(5) call fact(4) call fact(3) call fact(2) call fact(1) → base: return 1 → return 2*1=2 → return 3*2=6 → return 4*6=24 → return 5*24=120 answer: 120

    Multiple examples — where recursion shines vs flops:

    recursion: trees (great) vs naive fibonacci (terrible) 
    # GREAT: nested structures (depth unknown, shape self-similar)
def deep_sum(obj):
    if isinstance(obj, (int, float)) and not isinstance(obj, bool):
        return obj                      # base: a number
    if isinstance(obj, (list, tuple, set)):
        return sum(deep_sum(x) for x in obj)  # shrink: each element
    if isinstance(obj, dict):
        return sum(deep_sum(v) for v in obj.values())
    return 0                            # base: anything else contributes 0
print(deep_sum({"a": [1, 2, {"b": 3}], "c": (4,)}))  # 10

# TERRIBLE: naive fibonacci — exponential recomputation!
def fib(n):
    return n if n < 2 else fib(n-1) + fib(n-2)  # fib(30): ~2.7M calls!
import functools
@functools.lru_cache(maxsize=None)   # memoize: each n computed ONCE → O(n)!
def fib_fast(n):
    return n if n < 2 else fib_fast(n-1) + fib_fast(n-2)
print(fib_fast(100))  # instant (naive fib(100): longer than the universe)
    10 354224848179261915075

    Recursion vs iteration (professional judgment). Use recursion when the data is recursive (trees, nested JSON, filesystems, grammars) — the code mirrors the shape. Use iteration for linear repetition (counting, scanning, accumulating) — no stack risk, less overhead, no limit. Python has no tail-call optimization (deliberately — tracebacks stay complete), so deep linear recursion always risks RecursionError; convert with an explicit stack list when depth is unbounded.

    11.7 Closures: functions that remember their birthplace

    What. A closure is a function object bundled with references to the enclosing scope's variables it uses — even after the enclosing function has returned. Mechanism: inner def reads outer variables (LEGB's E); Python stores them in fn.__closure__ cells. Writing to them needs nonlocal (else assignment makes a local — Chapter 12).

    counter closure, dissected 
    def make_counter(step=1):
    count = 0                       # enclosing variable — the closure's memory
    def inc():
        nonlocal count              # WITHOUT this: UnboundLocalError (assigns local!)
        count += step               # write to the CAPTURED cell
        return count
    return inc                      # return the inner function (closure attached!)

c1 = make_counter()                 # own cell: count=0
c2 = make_counter(step=10)          # SEPARATE cell: independent memory!
print(c1(), c1())                   # 1 2
print(c2())                         # 10 — independent ✓
print(c1.__closure__[0].cell_contents)  # 2 — peek at the captured cell!
# Factory pattern: one template, many configured functions:
def make_adder(n):
    return lambda x: x + n           # each remembers its OWN n
add5 = make_adder(5)
print(add5(10))  # 15
    1 2 10 2 15

    Step-by-step. make_counter() runs: frame created, count = 0 bound, inc defined (capturing the cell for count, not a copy), frame "returns" — but the cell survives, referenced by the closure. Each c1() call: reads/writes the cell via nonlocal. c2 = make_counter(10) runs the factory again → fresh frame → fresh cell → fully independent counter. Closures are stateful functions — objects with one method, no class needed (Chapter 18 shows when to upgrade to classes).

    ⚠️ Closure trapsForgetting nonlocal on write → UnboundLocalError (read-before-local-assign). Loop-variable capture (§11.5's [2,2,2]) — defaults freeze values. Over-closure: capturing large objects keeps them alive as long as the closure lives (memory leak shape — capture only what you need, or use weakrefs).

    11.8 Higher-order functions + decorators from first principles

    What. Functions are objects (§3.1): passable as arguments, returnable, storable in lists/dicts. A higher-order function takes or returns functions (map, filter, sorted(key=…), and every decorator). A decorator is a higher-order function that takes a function and returns an enhanced wrapper — with @name as the rebinding syntax: @d def f(…) is exactly f = d(f) executed at definition time.

    decorator from zero: no magic, just closures + rebinding 
    import functools

def once(fn):
    """Allow fn to run once; later calls get a message."""
    done = False                          # closure memory (per-decorated-function!)
    @functools.wraps(fn)                  # preserve __name__/__doc__ (ALWAYS!)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):         # accept ANY signature
        nonlocal done
        if done:
            return "(already ran)"
        done = True
        return fn(*args, **kwargs)        # call THROUGH + return the result!
    return wrapper

@once                # greet = once(greet) — rebinds the NAME to the WRAPPER
def greet(name):
    """Say hello."""
    return f"hello, {name}"

print(greet("Ali"))   # hello, Ali (wrapper ran fn)
print(greet("Zoe"))   # (already ran) (wrapper short-circuited)
print(greet.__name__, "|", greet.__doc__)  # greet | Say hello. (wraps preserved!)
    hello, Ali (already ran) greet | Say hello.

    Step-by-step. At def time: greet (original) is passed to once, which builds wrapper (closure over fn + done) and returns it; the name greet is retied to the wrapper (original reachable only via greet.__wrapped__). At call time: your arguments hit wrapper → checks done → first call runs fn(*a, **k) and returns its result; later calls short-circuit. functools.wraps copies metadata so the wrapper impersonates the original (without it: __name__ == "wrapper", doc lost, debuggers confused, stacked decorators degraded).

    Three decorator rules (preview of Chapter 21's full treatment). ① Always @functools.wraps(fn). ② Wrapper signature (*args, **kwargs) + return fn(*args, **kwargs) (forgetting return turns every decorated function into a None-machine!). ③ Decorators with arguments (@retry(times=3)) are factories returning decorators — one more nesting level. Builtins you already use that are decorators: @property, @staticmethod, @classmethod (Ch. 18), @lru_cache (§11.6), @contextmanager (Ch. 21).

    11.9 Designing good functions (the craft rules)

    • One job. If the name needs "and" (load_and_clean_and_plot), split it. Test: describe it in one sentence without "and".
    • Small. ~5–20 lines; fits on one screen. Long function = hidden sub-functions begging for names.
    • Few parameters. ≤ 3–4; bundle the rest into a dataclass/config/dict. Boolean params are a smell (the function does two things — split or keyword-only it).
    • Pure when possible. Same inputs → same output, no mutation, no I/O. Pure functions are trivially testable, composable, cacheable, parallelizable. Push impurity (I/O, randomness, time) to the edges.
    • Return, don't print (§11.1). Validate at boundaries, assert inside (user input → if/raise; internal invariants → assert; Ch. 14/22).
    • Name as contract: verb + object (parse_date, total_revenue), honest about effects (get_or_create, ensure_dir), no abbreviations the team must decode.

    11.10 Interview questions

    Q1: Explain *args and **kwargs — both directions — with the wrapper use case.
    In definitions: *args gathers extra positionals (tuple), **kwargs extra keywords (dict); params after * are keyword-only. In calls: */** unpack sequences/dicts into arguments. Canonical use: transparent wrappers — def wrapper(*a, **k): log(); return fn(*a, **k) — forwarding any signature (decorators, proxies, API evolution).
    Q2: What happens with no return statement? Why is print not a substitute for return?
    The function returns None. print emits pixels for humans; return hands a value to the program for computation, testing (assert f(x) == …), and composition (g(f(x))). Print-instead-of-return yields untestable, unchainable code — return from cores, print at edges.
    Q3: The mutable-default-argument trap: demo, root cause, fix.
    Demo: def f(xs=[]): xs.append(1); return xs → [1] then [1,1]. Cause: defaults evaluate once at def time; the list is shared (fn.__defaults__ proves it). Fix: xs=None + build inside. Generalize: immutable defaults only.
    Q4: What is a closure? What is nonlocal for? Give a use.
    A function bundled with its enclosing scope's referenced variables (cells surviving the outer return — fn.__closure__). nonlocal declares write-access to an enclosing (non-global) binding — without it, assignment creates a shadowing local (UnboundLocalError on read-first). Uses: counters, factories (make_adder), decorators (wrapper state like done/cache).
    Q5: What does @decorator do, exactly? Why functools.wraps?
    @d def f ≡ f = d(f) at definition time: decorator receives the function object, returns (usually) a closure wrapper. functools.wraps copies __name__/__doc__/__module__ (+ __wrapped__) so introspection, docs, tracebacks, and stacking keep working.

    11.11 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. area(w, h=1): call it 5 ways (positional, keyword, mixed, defaults, unpacked *[3,4]). Show the two illegal call shapes and their errors.
    2. Demonstrate the mutable-default bug, inspect __defaults__, fix with None, and re-verify independence across 3 calls.
    3. Annotate every line of §11.8's @once: where's the closure? the factory? what does each nonlocal/wraps/return do? Remove wraps and show what degrades.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. clamp(x, lo=0, hi=1) with docstring + hints + keyword-only bounds + 8 assert-tests (edges: lo==hi, x exactly on bounds, NaN behavior documented).
    2. fib recursive vs iterative vs lru_cache: time all three at n=30, count calls (counter closure!), explain the exponential→linear collapse.
    3. make_averager() closure (running mean without storing all values — hint: keep total+count via nonlocal). Prove two instances independent.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. @timer + @retry(times, delay) (factory!) + @memoize (hand-rolled dict cache): implement all three from scratch with wraps, stack them, and argue the correct stacking order with a probe experiment.
    2. Implement deep_map(fn, nested) applying fn to every leaf of arbitrarily nested lists/tuples/dicts (recursion + closure counter of leaves visited). Handle cycles gracefully (seen-set of id()s — argue why).

    11.12 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build func_lab.py. A single file demonstrating, with printed proof: args/kwargs both directions, keyword-only + positional-only enforcement (show the TypeErrors caught), mutable-default bug + fix, closure counter + adder factory, late-binding trap + fix, recursion trace of fib(5) (indented like §11.6), and a stacked @timer @once function.
