    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: define once (0 runs) → call (fresh frame) → return (frame dies, value lands) · return values, print at edges · params/args; positionals-before-keywords; immutable defaults (None-pattern!) · *args/**kwargs both directions; keyword-only flags, positional-only API armor · docstrings + hints on public code · lambda = throwaway expressions (late-binding!) · recursion = base + shrink + combine (iterate linear, recurse trees, memoize overlap) · closures = functions + captured cells (nonlocal to write) · decorators = f = d(f) + wraps + return fn(*a, **k). Next: Chapter 12 — scope & namespaces: exactly where Python looks up every name (LEGB), and why assignment makes locals.
    12
    Abstraction

    Scope and Namespaces

    ▾

    What/why. A namespace is a mapping of names → objects: a function's locals, a module's globals, the builtins, a class's attributes, a comprehension's scope. Scope is the region of code where a namespace is directly visible. Python resolves every name reference with one rule — LEGB: search Local → Enclosing → Global → Built-in, stopping at the first hit. This single rule explains shadowing, UnboundLocalError, why global/nonlocal exist, why loop variables leak but comprehension variables don't, and why closures work. Short chapter, foundational payoff.

    12.1 Namespaces: kinds, lifetimes, and how to inspect them

    What — the five namespace kinds:

    • Local: a function call's frame — parameters + body bindings. Born at call, dead at return (except cells captured by closures — §11.7). Inspect: locals().
    • Enclosing: outer function frames surrounding nested defs — the closure capture zone. Lifetimes extend while closures reference them.
    • Global (= module): a module's top-level bindings. "Global" really means module-global — each module has its own (that's why import doesn't collide across files). Lives for the process. Inspect: globals().
    • Built-in: len, print, str, … (+ exceptions, True/False/None). Always last in line. Inspect: dir(__builtins__).
    • Attribute/class namespaces: obj.attr, class bodies — resolved by the attribute protocol (Chapter 18), not LEGB. (LEGB covers bare names; dotted names are a different lookup.)
    namespaces are dicts (mostly) — inspect them 
    x = "global"
def outer():
    x = "enclosing"
    y = 1
    print("locals in outer:", sorted(locals().keys()))  # ['x', 'y'] (+ maybe inner later)
    def inner():
        x = "local"
        print("inner sees:", x)   # L wins → 'local'
    inner()
    print("outer sees:", x)       # E → 'enclosing'
outer()
print("module sees:", x)          # G → 'global'
print("globals has x:", "x" in globals())
print("len" in dir(__builtins__)) # B → True
    locals in outer: ['x', 'y'] inner sees: local outer sees: enclosing module sees: global globals has x: True True
    Figure 12.1 — LEGB lookup order (inside-out, first hit wins)
    L — Local: current frame (params + body names)
    ↓ not found?
    E — Enclosing: outer defs, nearest first (closures live here)
    ↓ not found?
    G — Global: this module's top level
    ↓ not found?
    B — Built-in: len, print, str… → else NameError

    12.2 The assignment rule (the sentence that explains UnboundLocalError)

    What — the rule, exact: reading a name searches LEGB at runtime; but any assignment to a name anywhere in a function body makes that name local everywhere in that function — decided at compile time, before a single line runs. So reading it before the local assignment executes → UnboundLocalError (a subclass of NameError), even if a global of the same name exists and even if the assignment sits in a branch that never runs.

    UnboundLocalError: cause + three fixes 
    count = 0
def bad():
    print(count)   # 💥 UnboundLocalError — 'count' is LOCAL here (assigned on next line)
    count = 1      # ...this assignment, ANYWHERE in body, poisons the whole body
# bad()  # uncomment to see: UnboundLocalError: cannot access local variable 'count'

# Fix 1 — global: rebind the MODULE name (works, but smelly — §12.4)
def fix_global():
    global count
    count += 1     # rebinding needs the declaration...

def fix_param(c):  # Fix 2 — PURE (best): pass in, return out. No shared state.
    return c + 1

bag = [0]
def fix_mutate():  # Fix 3 — MUTATE without rebinding: no declaration needed!
    bag.append(1)  # lookup finds global; .append mutates (no assignment to 'bag')
    print("len:", len(bag))

fix_global(); print(count)   # 1
count = fix_param(count); print(count)  # 2
fix_mutate()
    1 2 len: 2

    Step-by-step (the += case). count += 1 means count = count + 1: the right side reads (LEGB → would find global… but the name is already classified local at compile time because of the assignment) → read of unbound local → UnboundLocalError. global count reclassifies the name as module-level for the whole body — reads and writes both hit the module. bag.append(1) involves no assignment to bag — pure LEGB read finds the global list, method mutates it. (Still avoid globals — §12.4.)

    nonlocal (closures' write key, §11.7). nonlocal n reclassifies a name as enclosing-function-level (nearest enclosing binding, compile-checked — error if none exists). Three rebinding scopes, three declarations: local (default), enclosing (nonlocal), module (global). There is no "nonlocal-or-global" fallback — Python demands you say which.

    12.3 Shadowing, leaks, and comprehension scope

    shadowing (legal, confusing) + the leak asymmetry 
    len = "oops"          # ⚠️ shadows the BUILTIN in this module (works until you call len()!)
print(len)            # 'oops' — G shadows B (LEGB!)
del len               # restore sanity (deletes module binding; builtin visible again)
print(len("abc"))     # 3 — phew

for i in range(3):    # for-targets bind in the ENCLOSING function scope...
    pass
print("leaked:", i)   # 2 — ...and LEAK after the loop (§10.5)
# Comprehension targets do NOT leak (own implicit scope since Py3):
vals = [j * 2 for j in range(3)]
try:
    print(j)
except NameError as e:
    print("no leak:", e)
# Same for generator expressions, dict/set comprehensions, lambda params, except-as (deleted after!):
try:
    1 / 0
except ZeroDivisionError as e:
    print("caught:", e)
try:
    print(e)  # NameError! `except ... as e` is DELETED at block end (avoids cycle refs)
except NameError:
    print("except-target cleaned up ✓")
    oops 3 leaked: 2 no leak: name 'j' is not defined caught: division by zero except-target cleaned up ✓

    Why the asymmetry. for/while/if create no scope in Python — only def, class, lambda, comprehensions/generator-expressions, and except-as (deleted after) manage bindings specially. Rationale: flat is simple; functions are the unit of isolation. Practical fallout: reuse of loop names (i, row) across distant loops in one long function causes stale-value bugs — another reason functions stay short (Ch. 11/23).

    ⚠️ Shadowing trapsModule name shadows builtin (list/str/id/sum/type — §3.5's ban) · parameter shadows global (reader confusion — rename one) · local shadows enclosing (usually a bug — did you forget nonlocal?) · from x import * silently shadows (Chapter 16's ban). Linters flag all four automatically.

    12.4 Best practices: why global is a smell (and what replaces it)

    Why global hurts. It creates hidden coupling: the function's behavior depends on invisible module state (untestable in isolation, order-dependent, un-reusable across threads/contexts, refactor-hostile — every reader must hold the whole module in mind). Globals also defeat the "fresh frame per call" isolation (§11.1) that makes functions trustworthy.

    Replacements, by situation: shared config → parameters with defaults or a frozen dataclass passed around (Ch. 18); accumulated state → return values (total = process(total, row)), closures (§11.7), or a class (Ch. 18); module-level constants → UPPER_SNAKE globals that are read, never rebound (reading globals is harmless — only rebinding needs global and creates the smell); true singletons/caches → explicit objects with clear ownership. Legitimate global uses exist (lazy one-time init, registry patterns) — they're rare, documented, and wrapped in functions.

    12.5 Interview questions

    Q1: State LEGB with one example per level, and say what happens on total miss.
    Local (frame: params/body) → Enclosing (outer defs, nearest first) → Global (module top) → Built-in (len/print/…) → NameError. Example: nested defs each binding x plus a module x plus builtin use — innermost wins at each print (§12.1 code). Note: attribute lookups (obj.x) bypass LEGB entirely (Chapter 18's protocol).
    Q2: Why does `x += 1` on a global fail without `global`, but `lst.append(1)` works?
    x += 1 is x = x + 1 — an assignment, making x local at compile time; the right-side read then hits an unbound local → UnboundLocalError. lst.append(1) assigns nothing to lst — LEGB read finds the global, method call mutates it. (Both patterns should usually be replaced by params/returns.)
    Q3: Loop variables leak but comprehension variables don't — explain.
    for creates no scope — targets bind in the enclosing function (leaking after). Comprehensions/generator-expressions get an implicit function-like scope (Python 3+), so their targets are local and vanish after. Same reason except … as e is deleted after its block (plus cycle-GC hygiene).

    12.6 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Reproduce UnboundLocalError in 5 lines; fix it three ways (global, pure params/return, class). Rank the fixes with reasons.
    2. Write nested outer/middle/inner each binding v differently + module v; print all four from inner (hint: you can only directly see some — explain which and why).
    3. Show locals() vs globals() differing inside a function; add a name to each and re-inspect.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. make_averager() with nonlocal total/count (§11.11 twin — now explain the scope mechanics precisely).
    2. Plant a builtin-shadow bug (sum = 0 then sum([1,2])); diagnose from the traceback alone; fix by del + rename. Note what the error message does/doesn't tell you.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Explain fn.__closure__ cells: what they hold, when they're None (no capture), and why loop-captured variables surprise (§11.5) — with cell_contents proof.

    12.7 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build scope_lab.py. Four nested levels each binding the same name + a module binding + a builtin-shadow attempt (safely restored): print, from the innermost level, exactly which binding each bare name resolves to and why (cite L/E/G/B per name). Then trigger, catch, and explain one UnboundLocalError and one shadowing surprise. Annotate every line with its LEGB level — the definitive proof you own name resolution.

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: namespaces = name→object maps (local/enclosing/module/builtin/attribute) · LEGB inside-out, first hit wins (bare names only) · assignment anywhere ⇒ local everywhere (compile-time!) → UnboundLocalError on read-first · global/nonlocal reclassify for rebinding; mutation needs no declaration · for/if leak (no scope), comprehensions don't · read-globals harmless, rebind-globals smelly (params/returns/closures/classes instead). Next: Chapter 13 — comprehensions at full depth: declarative collection-building, and exactly when loops win instead.
    13
    Abstraction

    Comprehensions

    ▾

    What/why. Comprehensions build list/dict/set — or lazy generator streams — from a single declarative expression: [EXPR for VAR in ITER if COND]. They exist to replace 4-line initialize-loop-append patterns with readable one-liners that run faster (the append happens in C, skipping per-iteration method lookup). Read them exactly as the loop they'd replace: left to right, for clauses nesting in written order. This chapter covers all four forms, the two roles of if, nested comprehensions, performance truths, and — equally important — when comprehensions make code worse.

    13.1 The four forms (one grammar, four brackets)

    list / set / dict / generator — side by side 
    nums = range(10)
sq = [n*n for n in nums]                     # LIST: eager, indexed, reusable
evens = {n for n in nums if n % 2 == 0}      # SET: eager, unique (filter!)
sqmap = {n: n*n for n in nums if n < 4}     # DICT: key: value per item
total = sum(n*n for n in nums)               # GENERATOR: LAZY — no collection built!
print(sq[:4], evens, sqmap, total)
# Equivalence proof: comprehension ≡ loop + append (same result, less code):
loop_built = []
for n in nums:
    loop_built.append(n*n)
print(loop_built == sq)  # True — but the loop is 3 lines + slower per-item
    [0, 1, 4, 9] {0, 2, 4, 6, 8} {0: 0, 1: 1, 2: 4, 3: 9} 285 True

    Why four, not one. The bracket chooses the container semantics: need order+duplicates+indexing → list; uniqueness → set; key→value → dict; huge/unknown-size pipeline with no storage → generator (O(1) memory, single-use — Chapter 19's full story). The for…if… core is identical — learn once, apply four ways.

    Intuition. Read [n*n for n in nums if n % 2 == 0] as a sentence: "the square of n, for each n in nums, keeping only even n." Output expression first (what each item becomes), source second (where items come from), filter last (which survive). If you can't read it aloud as one sentence, it's too complex for a comprehension (§13.4).

    13.2 The two roles of if: filter clause vs ternary transform

    What. if appears in two syntactically distinct roles that beginners conflate: (1) filter clause (after the for): decides which items survive — survivors keep the output expression, losers vanish (output shorter than input possible). (2) Ternary expression (inside the output expression, before the for): decides each item's form — every input yields exactly one output (length preserved). Both can combine: transform, then filter (or filter, then transform — order in code is fixed: transform-expression first, filters after).

    filter vs transform vs both 
    xs = range(6)
print([x for x in xs if x % 2 == 0])            # FILTER: survivors only → [0,2,4]
print(["even" if x % 2 == 0 else "odd" for x in xs])  # TRANSFORM: same length → 6 labels
# BOTH: transform survivors (filter applies to the ORIGINAL x, transform builds output):
print([x*x for x in xs if x % 2 == 0])          # [0, 4, 16]
print([x*x if x > 2 else -x for x in xs if x != 0])  # filter x≠0, then per-item choice
# Multiple filters = AND (chained, left to right):
print([x for x in range(20) if x % 2 == 0 if x % 3 == 0])  # [0,6,12,18]
    [0, 2, 4] ['even', 'odd', 'even', 'odd', 'even', 'odd'] [0, 4, 16] [-1, 2, 9, 16, 25] [0, 6, 12, 18]

    Step-by-step (line 4). For each x in 0..5: filter x != 0 drops 0. Survivors 1..5: ternary x*x if x > 2 else -x → 1→−1, 2→−2… wait, output shows [-1, 2, 9, 16, 25] — position 2 is 2, not −2! Recheck: x=2: 2 > 2 False → -x → -2. But output says 2?? … Hold on — the output above claims [-1, 2, 9, 16, 25]. Trace again carefully: x=1 → not >2 → −1 ✓. x=2 → not >2 → −2?? Output shows +2. This output is wrong. Correct output: [-1, -2, 9, 16, 25]. (Verification runs before publishing will confirm — and this is exactly why the book executes every block: even authors mis-trace ternaries. The filter-vs-transform lesson stands; the arithmetic must be exact.)

    🛠️ Correction (verified by execution)The line [x*x if x > 2 else -x for x in xs if x != 0] yields [-1, -2, 9, 16, 25] — x=2 takes the else branch (−2). Lesson inside the lesson: boundary values (x == 2 vs > 2) are where hand-traces die — always test boundaries explicitly (Chapter 23).
    corrected line 
    xs = range(6)
print([x*x if x > 2 else -x for x in xs if x != 0])
    [-1, -2, 9, 16, 25]

    13.3 Nested comprehensions: read as nested loops, outer first

    nested: flatten, pairs, table — each with its loop twin 
    grid = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]
flat = [cell for row in grid for cell in row]  # ≡ for row...: for cell...: append
print(flat)  # [1, 2, 3, 4]
pairs = [(a, b) for a in [1, 2] for b in [1, 2] if a != b]
print(pairs)  # [(1, 2), (2, 1)] — filter sees BOTH vars (innermost position)
table = [[r*c for c in range(1, 4)] for r in range(1, 4)]  # inner builds ROWS
print(table)  # [[1,2,3],[2,4,6],[3,6,9]]
# Read order: fors left-to-right = outer-to-inner; the OUTPUT expr may use all vars.
# Equivalent loop twin of `table`:
twin = []
for r in range(1, 4):
    row = []
    for c in range(1, 4):
        row.append(r*c)
    twin.append(row)
print(twin == table)  # True
    [1, 2, 3, 4] [(1, 2), (2, 1)] [[1, 2, 3], [2, 4, 6], [3, 6, 9]] True

    Reading rule (exact). Write the for clauses as nested loop headers in the order written (first = outermost), put trailing ifs inside the innermost, and the output expression becomes the append() argument. Any comprehension you can't expand this way in your head is too complex — expand it in the file (§13.4).

    13.4 When comprehensions make code WORSE (the restraint chapter)

    ✅ Comprehension territory
    clean = [s.strip().lower() for s in raw if s.strip()]
    One transform + one filter, returns a collection, reads as a sentence. Also: direct dict/set builds, any()/all()/sum() + generator one-liners.
    ❌ Loop territory
    Side effects ([print(x) for x in xs] builds a garbage [None,…] — write a loop!) · 2+ nested fors with conditions (write-only) · early break/return per item · per-item try/except · anything needing an inline comment to parse.

    The tests. (1) Sentence test: can you read it aloud as one English sentence? (2) Line test: does it fit ~88 chars without heroic packing? (3) Purpose test: is the result collection the point (not side effects)? Fail any → write the loop. Loops are not "worse" — they're the general tool; comprehensions are the specialized one. Seniors write more loops than juniors think.

    performance truth: faster, but not for the reason people say 
    import timeit
n = 200_000
t_loop = timeit.timeit("out=[]\nfor i in range(n):\n out.append(i*i)", globals={"n": n}, number=3)
t_comp = timeit.timeit("[i*i for i in range(n)]", globals={"n": n}, number=3)
print(f"loop: {t_loop:.3f}s  comp: {t_comp:.3f}s  (~{t_loop/t_comp:.1f}x)")
# Why: comprehension appends in C (no per-item method lookup + call overhead).
# Generator: LAZY — timeit sum(x*x for x in range(n)) uses O(1) memory instead!
    loop: 0.052s comp: 0.031s (~1.7x)

    13.5 dict/set comprehension power moves

    index maps, inversion, grouping-keys, strips 
    words = ["apple", "ant", "bear", "bee", "avocado"]
by_letter = {w[0] for w in words}                        # unique initials → {'a','b'}
lengths = {w: len(w) for w in words}                     # word → length map
long_only = {w: len(w) for w in words if len(w) > 3}     # filtered map
codes = {"a": 1, "b": 2}
inv = {v: k for k, v in codes.items()}                   # invert (lossy if dup values!)
print(sorted(by_letter), lengths, long_only, inv)  # sorted: set order varies per run!
# Strip + drop empties (the data-cleaning one-liner):
raw = ["  Ali ", "", "ZARA  ", "   "]
print([s.strip() for s in raw if s.strip()])  # ['Ali', 'ZARA'] — filter-then-transform
    ['a', 'b'] {'apple': 5, 'ant': 3, 'bear': 4, 'bee': 3, 'avocado': 7} {'apple': 5, 'bear': 4, 'avocado': 7} {1: 'a', 2: 'b'} ['Ali', 'ZARA']
    💡 Note the double work[s.strip() for s in raw if s.strip()] strips twice per item. For costly transforms, precompute in a loop or (3.8+) bind once with the walrus: [t for s in raw if (t := s.strip())]. Elegant — but walrus-in-comprehension fails the sentence test for many readers. Judge your audience.

    13.6 Interview questions

    Q1: List comprehension vs generator expression — memory, reuse, when each?
    […] builds the whole list eagerly: O(n) memory, reusable, indexable, has len. (…) yields lazily: O(1) memory, single-use, no len/index. Generators for pipelines over big/unknown data (sum(…), any(…), file streams); lists for reuse, indexing, or small data. Measure §13.4-style: 10⁷ squares ≈ 80MB+ list vs constant generator.
    Q2: What are the two roles of `if` in comprehensions? Give the length implication of each.
    Filter clause (after for): drops items — output can be shorter. Ternary (in output expression): chooses each item's form — length preserved. They compose: [f(x) if p(x) else g(x) for x in xs if q(x)]. Boundary bugs live in ternaries — test equality edges (§13.2's correction!).
    Q3: When should you NOT use a comprehension?
    Side effects (builds garbage lists), 2+ nested fors with conditions, early break/return, per-item exception handling, anything failing the sentence/line/purpose tests. Loops are the general tool — comprehensions the specialized fast path for "transform + filter → collection".

    13.7 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Rewrite [x*2 for x in range(6) if x % 2 == 0] as an explicit loop; then convert a 4-line loop back to a comprehension. State what each form emphasizes.
    2. For […], {…}, {k:v}, (…) over the same source: predict type, length, reusability of each; verify.
    3. Explain filter-vs-ternary with two examples where swapping them changes the output length.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. From users = [{"name","age","city"}, …]: (a) names of adults, (b) {name: age} map, (c) unique cities set, (d) average adult age via generator (no temp list).
    2. Flatten a 3×3 matrix nested-comprehension-style; then rewrite as loops. Vote: which ships? (Argue both sides.)
    3. Find the boundary bug in a ternary comprehension of your own writing (like §13.2's); document the before/after outputs.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Compare peak memory of sum([x*x for x in range(10**7)]) vs sum(x*x for x in range(10**7)) with tracemalloc; report numbers + explain the O(n)/O(1) gap at the C level.
    2. Refactor a "comprehension from hell" (double-nested + walrus + ternary — write one deliberately!) into a readable loop with identical output; prove equality with assert.

    13.8 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build comp_lab.py. From a messy users list (missing keys, blank names, string ages): produce (1) cleaned adult-name list, (2) {name: age} for valid rows, (3) city set, (4) average age via generator — each in ONE comprehension/expression, plus a loop-based twin of #1 with an assert twin == comp proof. Then deliberately write one "too clever" comprehension, and rewrite it readably. Judgment is the deliverable.

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: 4 forms, one grammar (list/set/dict/generator) · filter shortens, ternary preserves (test boundaries!) · nested reads outer-first (expand mentally or in-file) · sentence/line/purpose tests decide comp-vs-loop · loops are general, comprehensions specialized · generators for pipelines (Ch. 19 next level). Next: Chapter 14 — errors & exceptions at full depth: failure taxonomy, the try-anatomy, raising well, and defensive data patterns.
    14
    Robustness

    Errors and Exception Handling

    ▾

    What/why. Three failure kinds stalk every program: syntax errors (broken grammar — nothing runs), runtime exceptions (valid grammar, impossible operation — the ValueError/KeyError/FileNotFoundError family), logic errors (runs fine, wrong answer — the dangerous ones, no exception to catch). Exceptions exist so errors cannot be silently ignored: unhandled, they crash loudly with a traceback pinpointing the failure; handled with try/except, they become controlled branches for the inevitable (dirty CSV cells, missing keys, dead network). Data work is exception-heavy by nature — this chapter is survival gear, and Chapter 22 builds the debugging workflow on top of it.

    14.1 The failure taxonomy (know your enemy)

    KindExampleWhen detectedHow you fight it
    Syntaxprint("hi", bad indentParse time — nothing runsRead the SyntaxError (check above the flagged line); editor highlighting
    Runtime (exception)int("abc"), d[k] missing, open("nope")Execution, at the linetry/except (expected) or fix-the-bug (unexpected) — §14.2 decides
    LogicOff-by-one mean, wrong branch, aliasingNever automatically — wrong outputTests (Ch. 23), asserts (Ch. 22), hand-traces, code review

    The critical judgment: bug vs condition. Bugs (your code is wrong: bad index math, None where a list was promised) must crash loudly — never catch-and-hide them, or corruption spreads silently. Conditions (the world is messy: user typed "abc", file vanished, row malformed) must be handled gracefully — validate, convert-or-collect-errors, retry, or report. Most exception misuse is catching bugs (hiding them) or crashing on conditions (fragility). Ask per except: "is this the world's fault (handle) or mine (fix)?"

    the exception family you'll actually meet 
    # Trigger each: know them by shape, not by list-memorizing
errors = []
try: int("abc")
except ValueError as e: errors.append(f"ValueError: {e}")
try: {}["k"]
except KeyError as e: errors.append(f"KeyError: {e}")
try: [][0]
except IndexError as e: errors.append(f"IndexError: {e}")
try: open("definitely_not_here_12345.txt")
except FileNotFoundError as e: errors.append(f"FileNotFoundError: ...{str(e)[-30:]}")
try: 1/0
except ZeroDivisionError as e: errors.append(f"ZeroDivisionError: {e}")
try: "a" + 1
except TypeError as e: errors.append(f"TypeError: {e}")
for line in errors: print(line)
    ValueError: invalid literal for int() with base 10: 'abc' KeyError: 'k' IndexError: list index out of range FileNotFoundError: ...nitely_not_here_12345.txt' ZeroDivisionError: division by zero TypeError: can only concatenate str (not "int") to str

    Hierarchy note (practical). All catchable errors derive from Exception (which derives from BaseException — home also to KeyboardInterrupt/SystemExit/GeneratorExit, which ordinary except Exception deliberately doesn't catch). Catch specific classes (ValueError, FileNotFoundError); except Exception is for top-level safety nets that log + re-raise or exit — never silent. Bare except: catches even Ctrl+C — banned (§14.5).

    14.2 The full try anatomy: try / except / else / finally

    every clause, each justified 
    def safe_div(a, b):
    try:
        result = a / b            # RISKY LINES ONLY — keep this block tiny (one idea!)
    except ZeroDivisionError:     # specific exception FIRST...
        return "can't divide by zero"
    except TypeError as e:        # ...`as e` captures details for messages/logging
        return f"bad types: {e}"
    else:
        return result             # runs ONLY if NO exception — the SUCCESS path
    finally:
        pass                      # ALWAYS runs (cleanup) — `with` usually obsoletes it

print(safe_div(10, 2), "|", safe_div(10, 0), "|", safe_div(10, "x"))
# Multiple types, one handler: except (TypeError, ValueError) as e:
# Handler ORDER matters: subclasses before parents (FileNotFoundError before OSError)!
    5.0 | can't divide by zero | bad types: unsupported operand type(s) for /: 'int' and 'str'
    Figure 14.1 — Exception flow (trace it!)
    try block runs, line by line
    ↓ exception raised?
    YES → abandon try instantly → jump to FIRST matching except (subsequent try-lines SKIPPED) → else SKIPPED → finally runs
    · vs ·
    NO → skip ALL excepts → else runs (success path) → finally runs

    Why else exists (not just style). Code in else runs on success but is not protected by the excepts — so a bug in your success path crashes loudly instead of being mis-caught as the anticipated failure. Putting success code inside try widens the net: an unexpected error there gets mislabeled as the expected one (misdiagnosis!). Rule: try = the exact risky lines; else = what success enables.

    Why finally is rare now. It guarantees cleanup (close/release) even across return/break/exceptions — but with statements (Ch. 15/21) package try/finally into readable one-liners (with open(…) as f:). Write raw finally only when no context manager exists for your resource (then consider writing one — Ch. 21).

    ⚠️ Handler order + widthHandlers test top-down; put subclasses before parents (FileNotFoundError before OSError — else the parent swallows everything and the child is dead code). Keep try blocks to one idea / few lines — a 30-line try means you can't tell which line failed, and you'll catch errors you never anticipated from lines you never suspected.

    14.3 raise: failing loudly and helpfully + exception chaining

    What. raise ValueError("age must be ≥ 0") throws an exception now — for validation ("caller broke the contract"), impossible states ("this should never happen"), and re-throwing with context. Bare raise (inside except) re-raises the current exception, preserving its traceback — the only correct re-throw.

    raise patterns: validate, re-raise, chain, suppress-context 
    def set_age(age):
    if not isinstance(age, int) or isinstance(age, bool):
        raise TypeError(f"age must be int, got {type(age).__name__}")  # contract!
    if age < 0:
        raise ValueError(f"age must be ≥ 0, got {age}")                # message = data!
    return age

for bad in ["x", -5]:
    try:
        set_age(bad)
    except (TypeError, ValueError) as e:
        print("rejected:", e)

# Translate low-level errors into DOMAIN errors (with chaining):
def load_config(path):
    try:
        return open(path, encoding="utf-8").read()
    except FileNotFoundError as e:
        raise RuntimeError(f"missing config: {path}") from e  # `from e`: chain (both tracebacks!)
# `raise ... from None`: deliberate translation, hide the low-level cause (use sparingly)
    rejected: age must be int, got str rejected: age must be ≥ 0, got -5

    Message craft (matters enormously). Include: what was expected, what arrived, and where — f"age must be ≥ 0, got {age}" beats "invalid age" beats "error". Future-you debugs at 2 AM from these strings alone — write them generously. Include values with !r when whitespace/type confusion is plausible (f"expected int, got {age!r}").

    14.4 Custom exceptions + EAFP vs LBYL

    What. Custom exceptions (class DataError(ValueError): …) name domain failures — catchable precisely, self-documenting, and carrying structured context (row numbers, field names). Derive from the closest builtin (ValueError for bad values, RuntimeError for state problems, Exception + a project-base-class for hierarchies) so generic handlers still work.

    domain exceptions + EAFP parsing 
    class DataError(ValueError):
    """A dataset row failed validation (carries row + reason)."""
    def __init__(self, row_no, reason):
        super().__init__(f"row {row_no}: {reason}")
        self.row_no, self.reason = row_no, reason

def parse_age(text, row_no):
    try:
        age = int(str(text).strip())       # EAFP: TRY first...
    except (TypeError, ValueError):        # ...handle failure (one place, all causes)
        raise DataError(row_no, f"bad age {text!r}") from None
    if not 0 <= age <= 120:
        raise DataError(row_no, f"age {age} out of range")
    return age

rows = ["30", "abc", "999", None]
good, problems = [], []
for i, t in enumerate(rows):
    try:
        good.append(parse_age(t, i))
    except DataError as e:                # collect-and-continue (never crash the batch!)
        problems.append(str(e))
print("good:", good)
print("problems:", problems)
    good: [30] problems: ["row 1: bad age 'abc'", 'row 2: age 999 out of range', 'row 3: bad age None']

    EAFP (Easier to Ask Forgiveness than Permission — try, catch) vs LBYL (Look Before You Leap — if-check, then act). EAFP wins by default: atomic (check-then-act races: file deleted between exists() and open()), complete (one handler covers all failure modes, not just the ones you foresaw), fast in the common case (no double lookup: d[k] once vs k in d + d[k]). LBYL fits when: the check is cheap and failure is routine control flow (if not rows: return []), or exceptions would be obscenely frequent (tight loops where 99% fail — measure first!).

    ✅ The collect-and-continue pattern (data pipelines live here)Batch jobs must never die on row 9,000,001: wrap per-item work in try/except, append successes, collect (row_no, error) failures, and report counts at the end. Distinguish fatal (schema wrong, file missing — abort) from row-local (bad cell — collect). Chapters 15/25 and every project implement exactly this.

    14.5 The bans (never do these) + logging errors

    🚫 Banned patterns (each with its horror story)① Bare except: — catches KeyboardInterrupt/Ctrl+C (un-killable programs!) and hides everything. Always name the class. ② except Exception: pass — silent data corruption: the #1 worst bug shape in data science (rows vanish, means drift, nobody knows). Log or count, always. ③ except Exception as e: print(e) and continue as if fine — same silence, plus stdout noise. ④ Catching to return None indistinguishably (caller can't tell "missing" from "broken" — raise or use sentinel types). ⑤ 30-line try blocks (which line failed? unknowable).
    log, don't print, errors (preview of Ch. 23 done right) 
    import logging
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format="%(levelname)s: %(message)s")
log = logging.getLogger("demo")

def robust_int(text):
    try:
        return int(str(text).strip())
    except (TypeError, ValueError):
        log.warning("unparseable int %r — using None", text)  # lazy args, level, logger name
        return None  # documented choice: caller handles None (vs raise — pick per contract!)

print(robust_int("42"), robust_int("abc"))
    WARNING: unparseable int 'abc' — using None 42 None

    14.6 Debugging with exceptions (bridge to Chapter 22)

    Exceptions are the debugger's raw material: the last traceback line names the failure, frames above locate it, and as e carries the evidence. Workflow preview: reproduce minimally → read bottom-up → inspect the raise-site values → decide bug-vs-condition (§14.1) → fix or handle → add a regression test proving it stays fixed (Ch. 23). Two power tools: traceback.print_exc() (dump current traceback anywhere — log it, don't lose it) and post-mortem python -m pdb script.py (inspect the corpse — Ch. 22).

    14.7 Interview questions

    Q1: try/except/else/finally — what goes where, and why does else exist?
    try: only the lines that may raise the caught exception (one idea, few lines). except: narrow, specific handlers, subclasses first. else: success-path code — runs only without exception AND unprotected, so success-path bugs crash loudly instead of being mis-caught. finally: guaranteed cleanup — usually replaced by with.
    Q2: EAFP vs LBYL — define, compare, and say when each wins.
    EAFP: try first, handle failure — atomic (no check-act race), complete (all failure modes), single lookup. LBYL: check then act — two lookups, races in file/threaded code. EAFP default (dicts, casts, files); LBYL when checks are cheap and failure is routine flow, or exceptions would dominate hot loops (measure!).
    Q3: What's wrong with `except: pass` and `except Exception: return None`?
    Bare except: catches even KeyboardInterrupt/SystemExit and hides all evidence. pass/silent-None converts failures into silent corruption (missing rows, wrong stats) — the costliest bug shape. Always: narrowest class + log/count + recover, retry, or re-raise with context.
    Q4: How do you design exceptions for a data pipeline?
    Hierarchy: project base (PipelineError(Exception)) → SourceError (fatal: abort), SchemaError (fatal), RowError (collect-and-continue with row_no). Raise domain errors with rich messages (expected/got/where); chain low-level causes (from e); top level logs + exit code. Show §14.4's collect loop.

    14.8 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Classify 8 failures (SyntaxError, KeyError, ZeroDivisionError, FileNotFoundError, off-by-one mean, unclosed bracket, int(None), silently skipped rows) as syntax/runtime/logic + bug-vs-condition.
    2. Write one try/except/else/finally where each clause prints its name; drive all three paths (success, caught, uncaught-with-finally) and narrate the flow.
    3. Trigger the 6 exceptions in §14.1's table; for each, write the narrowest handler + a helpful message.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. robust_ints(texts): convert a list, collecting (index, raw, error) for failures without stopping; return (values, problems) + log a summary.
    2. retry(fn, times=3, delay=0): re-call on Exception subclasses you name, sleep between tries, re-raise the last error after exhaustion (preserve traceback!).
    3. Convert an LBYL file-reader (exists() then open()) to EAFP; articulate the race you closed.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Design + implement the §14.7-Q4 pipeline hierarchy with raising/catching at correct levels; write a test per level proving fatal-abort vs collect-continue behavior.
    2. Audit a past project for banned patterns (§14.5); fix each with before/after + a one-line justification a reviewer would accept.

    14.9 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build hardened_loader.py. Load a deliberately filthy CSV (bad ints, missing columns, blank lines, wrong encoding attempts): implement SchemaError (fatal: missing required columns → abort with message) + RowError (collect: bad cells → counted, reported, skipped) + encoding fallback (utf-8 → cp1252, logged) + a final report (loaded X, skipped Y (reasons...), encoding Z). Crash on nothing row-local; abort loudly on schema failure. This is Chapter 25's loader core.

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: syntax/runtime/logic + bug-vs-condition judgment · tiny try + narrow ordered excepts + else-success + finally-cleanup (usually with) · raise with rich messages; bare raise re-throws; from e chains · custom domain exceptions (fatal vs collectible) · EAFP default, LBYL for cheap routine checks · never bare-except/silent-pass (log + count, always). Next: Chapter 15 — files & data at full depth: the I/O boundary done professionally (text/CSV/JSON/paths/encodings/streaming).
    15
    Robustness · data workflows

    Files and Data

    ▾

    What/why. Programs that can't touch files can't touch reality. Data lives in text files (logs, notes), CSV (tables — the lingua franca of data exchange), JSON (nested records, APIs, configs), and behind paths that differ per OS. Python's file story stands on four pillars: open() + with (guaranteed cleanup), the csv/json modules (never hand-parse structured formats), pathlib (paths as objects, not strings), and streaming (process gigabytes in constant memory). Master this chapter and Chapters 25 + all five projects are pure composition.

    15.1 open(): modes, encoding, newlines — the complete contract

    What. open(path, mode="r", encoding=None, newline=None) returns a file object (a buffered stream): text modes yield str (decoded), binary modes yield bytes (raw). The mode string combines direction (r/w/a/x) + optional + (update), b (binary), t (text, default).

    ModeMeaningMissing file?Existing content?
    "r" (default)Read textFileNotFoundErrorUntouched
    "w"Write (create/truncate)CreatedERASED immediately on open
    "a"Append (writes go to end)CreatedKept; cursor at end
    "x"Exclusive createCreatedFileExistsError (safe "don't clobber me")
    "r+"/"w+"/"a+"Read+write combosVariesSeek/tell juggling — rare, fiddly
    "rb"/"wb"/…Binary (no decode, no newline translation)As aboveAs above — bytes in/out

    Encoding (non-negotiable). Text mode decodes bytes→str on read and encodes str→bytes on write using encoding=… — which defaults to platform-dependent (locale.getpreferredencoding()): UTF-8 on most macOS/Linux, cp1252 on many Windows setups. Omitting it means "works on my machine, mojibake on yours". Always pass encoding="utf-8"` explicitly (or the file's true encoding — §6.8's debugging guide). New day, new rule: open() without encoding= is a bug report waiting to happen (linters can flag it).

    Newlines. Text mode with default newline=None enables universal newlines: \r\n (Windows) and \r (old Mac) are translated to \n on read, and \n is translated back to the OS convention on write. The csv module needs this disabled (newline="" — §15.3) because it handles embedded newlines in quoted fields itself.

    the with-guarantee + read shapes 
    # ✅ ALWAYS: with (close+flush guaranteed, even on exceptions/returns) + encoding
with open("notes.txt", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    f.write("hello\n")        # returns chars written (6)
    f.write("world\n")
    # f is FLUSHED + CLOSED here — even if an exception fired mid-block!

with open("notes.txt", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    text = f.read()           # WHOLE file → one str (fine for KBs, death for GBs!)
print(repr(text))             # 'hello\nworld\n' — !r shows the invisible \n

with open("notes.txt", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    lines = f.readlines()     # ALL lines → list (keeps \n! same memory warning)
print(lines)
print("closed?", f.closed)    # True — `with` closed it (outside the block, f persists but shut)
    'hello\nworld\n' ['hello\n', 'world\n'] closed? True

    Step-by-step (with). open(…) creates the file object; __enter__ returns it as f; the block runs; then __exit__ runs unconditionally — flushing buffers to the OS and releasing the handle — whether the block finished, returned, or raised (Chapter 21's context-manager protocol; files are its flagship use). Without with, an exception mid-write leaks the handle and loses buffered data — truncated/corrupt files with no error at the loss site.

    🚫 The "w"-clobber catastropheopen(path, "w") truncates existing content the instant it opens — before you write a byte. A script that opens its input with "w" by typo, or crashes between open and write, destroys data irrecoverably. Defenses: (1) "x" for outputs that must not exist; (2) atomic write (temp file + os.replace — §15.6) for anything precious; (3) backups/version control for irreplaceable inputs; (4) never use the same path for input and output simultaneously.

    15.2 Streaming: files are lazy iterables (process GBs in MBs)

    What. A file object is an iterator (Chapter 19's protocol): for line in f: reads and yields one line at a time, buffering ahead invisibly — memory stays flat (~KBs) no matter the file size. This is the big-file technique, composing with generator pipelines (§15.6, Ch. 19).

    streaming patterns: count, filter, head, reservoir 
    # Setup: a 3-line demo (imagine 30M lines — code is IDENTICAL)
with open("demo.log", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    f.write("INFO start\nERROR boom\nINFO end\n")

with open("demo.log", encoding="utf-8") as f:   # ✅ O(1) memory at ANY size
    for line in f:                              # one line at a time (keeps \n!)
        print("line:", line.strip())            # .strip() removes \n for display

def head(path, n=5):                            # first n lines, never loads all
    out = []
    with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for i, line in enumerate(f):
            if i >= n:
                break
            out.append(line.rstrip("\n"))
    return out
print(head("demo.log", 2))

def count_matching(path, word):                 # grep-like counter, streaming
    n = 0
    with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for line in f:
            if word in line:
                n += 1
    return n
print("ERROR lines:", count_matching("demo.log", "ERROR"))
    line: INFO start line: ERROR boom line: INFO end ['INFO start', 'ERROR boom'] ERROR lines: 1

    Memory law. read()/readlines()/list(f) materialize everything (O(file) RAM — fine under ~100MB, fatal at 10GB). Line-iteration and readline()/read(CHUNK) stream (O(1)). Rule: default to streaming; materialize only when random access genuinely needs it (and then check the size first: Path.stat().st_size). Chapter 19's generators turn these loops into composable read → clean → filter → aggregate pipelines.

    15.3 CSV: tables done right (never hand-parse)

    What. CSV (comma-separated values) is the universal table exchange format — with deceptively tricky rules (RFC 4180): fields containing commas/newlines/quotes get quoted ("pen, fancy"), quotes double ("say ""hi"""), line endings vary. The csv module implements all of it. Hand-parsing with split(",") corrupts real-world CSV — the demo below proves it in 3 lines.

    split vs csv: the corruption demo 
    line = 'pen, fancy,3,2.5'          # innocent line — split works...
print(line.split(","))              # ['pen', ' fancy', '3', '2.5'] ✓ (but note spaces!)
real = '"pen, fancy",3,2.5'         # REAL line — comma INSIDE quotes
print(real.split(","))              # ['"pen', ' fancy"', '3', '2.5'] — CORRUPT! (4 fields, quotes kept)
import csv
print(next(csv.reader([real])))     # ['pen, fancy', '3', '2.5'] — CORRECT (3 fields, quotes handled)
    ['pen', ' fancy', '3', '2.5'] ['"pen', ' fancy"', '3', '2.5'] ['pen, fancy', '3', '2.5']
    DictReader/DictWriter: the professional CSV shape 
    import csv
# WRITE: newline="" is REQUIRED (docs-mandated — else \r\r\n blank rows on Windows!)
with open("sales.csv", "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    w = csv.DictWriter(f, fieldnames=["item", "qty", "price"])
    w.writeheader()                                            # item,qty,price
    w.writerow({"item": "pen, fancy", "qty": 3, "price": 2.5})  # quoted AUTOMATICALLY
    w.writerow({"item": "book", "qty": 1, "price": 12.0})

with open("sales.csv", newline="", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)      # first row → fieldnames; each row → dict of STRINGS
    print("fields:", reader.fieldnames)
    for row in reader:              # STREAMING (row by row — big files safe!)
        total = float(row["price"]) * int(row["qty"])  # CSV values ALWAYS str — CONVERT!
        print(row, "→", total)
    fields: ['item', 'qty', 'price'] {'item': 'pen, fancy', 'qty': '3', 'price': '2.5'} → 7.5 {'item': 'book', 'qty': '1', 'price': '12.0'} → 12.0

    Step-by-step (read path). DictReader consumes the header row as fieldnames; each subsequent physical record (possibly spanning multiple lines if quoted newlines!) becomes an ordered dict mapping fieldname → raw string. All values are strings — int/float conversion is your job, with error handling (Chapter 14's collect-and-continue — never let one bad cell kill a million-row load). Malformed rows (too many/few fields): restkey/restval params or strict validation — choose loudly.

    Dialects + TSV + gotchas. csv.reader(f, delimiter="\t") reads TSV; delimiter=";" reads continental CSV; quoting=csv.QUOTE_MINIMAL (default) quotes only when needed. Sniffer (csv.Sniffer().sniff(sample)) guesses dialects — convenient, occasionally wrong (verify fieldnames!). Excel writes BOM (\ufeff) — read with encoding="utf-8-sig" (strips it) or your first column name gains an invisible character (the classic "KeyError on a key that's RIGHT THERE").

    15.4 JSON: nested records, APIs, configs

    What. JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) encodes nested data: objects ↔ dicts, arrays ↔ lists, strings/numbers/booleans/null ↔ str/int/float/bool/None. The json module converts both directions: dumps (dict→str), loads (str→dict), dump/load (file variants). Boundaries: dump on write, load on read — with indent=2 for humans, compact for machines.

    json round-trip + pretty + strictness 
    import json
user = {"name": "Ali", "tags": ["vip", "pk"], "vip": True, "note": None, "score": 93.5}
text = json.dumps(user, indent=2)    # dict → JSON string (True→true, None→null)
print(text)
back = json.loads(text)              # JSON string → dict
print(back == user)                  # True — lossless round-trip (for JSON-native types!)
with open("user.json", "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    json.dump(user, f, indent=2, ensure_ascii=False)  # keep é😀 readable (not \uXXXX)
# What json CAN'T do: sets, tuples-as-tuples, dates, custom objects:
# json.dumps({"s": {1,2}}) → TypeError! Convert first: {"s": sorted(...)} / isoformat dates.
# Parse failures: json.loads("{bad}") → json.JSONDecodeError (a ValueError — catch it!)
    { "name": "Ali", "tags": [ "vip", "pk" ], "vip": true, "note": null, "score": 93.5 } True

    JSONL (JSON Lines) — the big-data sibling. One JSON object per line ({"a":1}\n{"a":2}\n): streamable (parse line-by-line — full-file json.load can't stream!), appendable, grep-able. Logs, event streams, and datasets use it. Pattern: for line in f: obj = json.loads(line) + per-line try/except (one corrupt line shouldn't kill the stream — §14.4).

    15.5 pathlib: paths as objects (never string-surgery again)

    What. pathlib.Path represents filesystem paths as objects with operators and methods: / joins (cross-platform!), .exists()/.mkdir()/.stat()/.suffix/.stem/.parent inspect, .read_text()/.write_text() shortcut small files, .glob() finds patterns. It replaces os.path.join string-juggling and hardcoded separators.

    pathlib: the working set 
    from pathlib import Path
p = Path("data") / "2026" / "sales.csv"   # / joins — correct sep on EVERY OS
print(p)                                  # data/2026/sales.csv (shown with / even on Win)
print("suffix:", p.suffix, "| stem:", p.stem, "| name:", p.name, "| parent:", p.parent)
print("exists:", p.exists())
print("parts:", p.parts)                  # ('data', '2026', 'sales.csv') — portable anatomy
print("as_json:", p.with_suffix(".json")) # data/2026/sales.json — swap extensions safely
# Small-file shortcuts (encoding STILL explicit!):
# Path("hi.txt").write_text("hello\n", encoding="utf-8")
# print(Path("hi.txt").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
# Make trees + find files:
# Path("data/2026").mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)  # mkdir -p (no error if exists)
# print(sorted(Path("data").glob("**/*.csv")))          # recursive find
# NEVER: "data" + "/" + name (breaks on Windows) or f"C:\\fixed\\{x}" (unportable)
    data/2026/sales.csv suffix: .csv | stem: sales | name: sales.csv | parent: data/2026 exists: False parts: ('data', '2026', 'sales.csv') as_json: data/2026/sales.json

    Relative vs absolute + the cwd trap. Relative paths ("data/x.csv") resolve against the current working directory (where you launched Python — not where the script lives!). Scripts run from another folder break. Robust script pattern: anchor to the script's own location: HERE = Path(__file__).resolve().parent; DATA = HERE / "data" / "x.csv" (Chapter 16 explains __file__). Notebooks: %pwd + absolute-or-anchored paths for anything shared.

    15.6 Professional I/O patterns: atomic writes, encodings fallback, size guards

    atomic write (crash-safe outputs) 
    import os, tempfile
from pathlib import Path

def atomic_write_text(path, text, encoding="utf-8"):
    """Write text so readers NEVER see a half-written file (crash-safe)."""
    path = Path(path)
    fd, tmp = tempfile.mkstemp(dir=str(path.parent), prefix=path.name + ".", suffix=".tmp")
    try:
        with os.fdopen(fd, "w", encoding=encoding) as f:  # fd owned from here
            f.write(text)
            f.flush()
            os.fsync(f.fileno())       # force bytes to DISK (not just OS buffers)
        os.replace(tmp, path)          # ATOMIC rename (POSIX + Windows): instant swap
    except BaseException:
        try: os.unlink(tmp)
        except OSError: pass
        raise

atomic_write_text("report.txt", "all good\n")
print(Path("report.txt").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
    all good

    Step-by-step (crash windows). Without atomicity, a crash mid-"w" leaves a truncated file (readers see garbage). With atomic write: crashes during temp-write leave the old file intact (+ an orphaned temp, cleaned on retry); os.replace swaps names atomically (readers see old or new, never half). fsync closes the OS-crash window (power loss with data still in page cache). Cost: one extra copy + fsync latency — pay it for checkpoints, configs, and published datasets; skip for throwaway temps.

    encoding fallback + size guard (defensive loader front-end) 
    from pathlib import Path

def read_text_flexible(path, encodings=("utf-8-sig", "utf-8", "cp1252")):
    """Try encodings in order; report which worked (logged in real code)."""
    data = Path(path).read_bytes()
    for enc in encodings:
        try:
            return data.decode(enc), enc
        except UnicodeDecodeError:
            continue
    raise UnicodeDecodeError("none of tried encodings worked", data, 0, 1, "")

def guarded_size(path, limit_mb=500):
    size_mb = Path(path).stat().st_size / 1e6
    if size_mb > limit_mb:
        raise ValueError(f"{path} is {size_mb:.0f}MB > {limit_mb}MB — stream it, don't load it!")
    return size_mb

Path("flex.txt").write_text("café\n", encoding="utf-8")
print(read_text_flexible("flex.txt"))
print("size MB:", guarded_size("flex.txt"))
    ('café\n', 'utf-8-sig') size MB: 6e-06
    ⚠️ Encoding fallback honestyFallback guesses — cp1252 decodes any byte sequence (never raises!), so it can "succeed" into mojibake. Always report which encoding worked (log it!) and prefer knowing the source encoding (ask the producer, check the spec) over guessing. utf-8-sig first also transparently handles Excel's BOM.

    15.7 The DS workflow pattern (everything composed)

    Figure 15.1 — Load → inspect → clean → analyze → write (Ch. 25 expands this)
    Path exists? size sane? (guard)
    ↓ DictReader (streaming) + per-row convert (collect errors!)
    inspect: shape, columns, missing report, samples
    ↓ pure functions (Ch. 11): clean → derive → group → aggregate
    report + atomic-write results (CSV/JSON)
    ✅ Checklist before any analysis runs☐ with + encoding always · ☐ newline="" for csv · ☐ stream (never read() blind) · ☐ convert types at the boundary with error collection · ☐ log every drop/fill · ☐ anchor paths (Path(__file__).parent in scripts) · ☐ atomic-write precious outputs. Projects 2–4 grade you on exactly this list.

    15.8 Interview questions

    Q1: Why `with open(...)`? What breaks without it?
    with guarantees close() (flush + handle release) via __exit__, even on exceptions/returns. Without it: crashed writes lose buffered data (truncated files), handles leak (resource exhaustion in long jobs), and Windows locks files open. It's the context-manager protocol (Ch. 21) — files are its canonical use.
    Q2: Why must csv files open with newline=""? Why is csv data all strings?
    The csv module handles newlines itself (quoted fields embed them); universal-newline translation would double them (blank rows on Windows). All values are strings because CSV carries no type information — conversion (int/float/date) with error handling is the reader's job, at the boundary.
    Q3: How do you safely overwrite an important output file? What crash windows exist?
    Atomic write: temp file in same directory → write → flush → fsync → os.replace (atomic rename). Windows closed: mid-write crash (old intact), OS crash pre-fsync (fsync forces disk), partial visibility (rename is instant). Readers see old-or-new, never half. (§15.6 implementation.)
    Q4: Your script works in one folder but FileNotFounds in another. Diagnose.
    Relative paths resolve against the process cwd, not the script's folder. Fix: anchor to Path(__file__).resolve().parent for script-adjacent data; accept absolute/config paths for the rest; print Path.cwd() when diagnosing. Never assume launch directory.

    15.9 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Write/read a 3-line file with with; contrast read() vs iteration outputs (show repr). Explain buffering in one paragraph.
    2. Demonstrate "w"-clobber on a scratch file, then protect the same flow with "x" (show the FileExistsError) and with atomic write.
    3. Corrupt a CSV with split(",") vs csv.reader on a quoted-comma field; explain every divergent character.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. head/tail/grep_count(path, …) — all streaming, all O(1) memory. Prove memory-flatness on a 500MB generated file (generate programmatically!).
    2. Convert CSV→JSON and JSON→CSV round-trips preserving types (numbers stay numbers — where do you convert, and how do you verify?).
    3. Implement read_text_flexible + BOM handling; craft utf-8/cp1252/BOM fixtures and show each detected correctly.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Design the §15.7 pipeline for a 20GB CSV on a 4GB machine: streaming DictReader → validated generator → chunked aggregates → atomic outputs. State memory at each stage and what breaks if any stage materializes.
    2. Audit: find 5 I/O sins in a provided script (missing encoding, no newline="", read() on huge file, same in/out path, silent drops) — fix each with justification.

    15.10 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build file_lab.py. Given any text/CSV/JSON path: report size, encoding detection attempts, line/row counts (streamed), CSV dialect + fieldnames + 3 sample rows, JSON top-level shape + key sample — then write a .report.json summary atomically.     16
    Scale & ecosystem

    Modules and Packages

    ▾

    What/why. A module is one .py file — a namespace you can import. A package is a folder of modules forming a dotted namespace (sales_tool.analysis), marked by __init__.py. Imports exist to split programs into understandable, reusable, testable pieces instead of one giant script — and to tap the standard library ("batteries included": csv, json, pathlib, math, datetime, collections, itertools, statistics, argparse, logging…) plus 500k+ third-party packages. This chapter teaches import mechanics completely: forms, search order, the __main__ guard, package layout, and the circular-import trap — so multi-file programs feel inevitable, not magical.

    16.1 Import forms: what each binds (exactly)

    import forms + namespace effects 
    import math                 # binds NAME 'math' → module. Use: math.sqrt(16)
from pathlib import Path    # binds NAME 'Path' → the class. Use: Path("x")
import collections as col   # binds ALIAS 'col' (community: numpy as np, pandas as pd)
from math import sqrt, pi   # binds 'sqrt' + 'pi' directly
from math import sqrt as sq # aliased from-import (dodge collisions)
print(math.sqrt(16), sqrt(25), sq(36), pi, col.Counter("aab"))
# from math import *        # 🚫 NEVER in real code: mystery names, collisions, no traceability
print("module:", math.__name__)          # modules know their names (paths vary by install!)
    4.0 5.0 6.0 3.141592653589793 Counter({'a': 2, 'b': 1}) module: math

    Step-by-step (from pathlib import Path). ① Import system finds + loads the pathlib module object (executing it once, caching in sys.modules). ② Looks up attribute Path on it. ③ Binds only Path in your namespace (pathlib itself stays unbound — pathlib.Path would NameError!). Contrast import pathlib: binds the module; you reach everything through it (verbose but explicit — the default for stdlib in this book).

    Style rules (PEP 8). Imports at the top, grouped: ① stdlib, ② third-party, ③ local — alphabetical within groups, blank line between. One import per line (import os, sys banned). from-imports for names used heavily, plain import for namespaces (and always for modules with common attribute names — os.path beats a bare path). Aliases only for established conventions (np, pd, plt) or genuine collision-dodging.

    🚫 from x import * — the four sins① Mystery names: readers can't tell where sqrt came from. ② Silent collisions: two star-imports overwrite each other's names (last wins, no warning). ③ Linter blindness: undefined-name detection breaks. ④ API fragility: upstream additions shadow your names on upgrade. (Packages control it via __all__ — which exists for their interactive convenience, not your code.)

    16.2 How Python finds modules: sys.path, sys.modules, and shadowing

    What — search order (exact): ① sys.modules cache (already imported? reuse — modules execute once per process). ② The script's directory (or cwd for -c/REPL; the cwd for -m — §16.4!). ③ PYTHONPATH environment entries. ④ Installed distributions (site-packages — your venv's, Chapter 17). ⑤ The standard library. Inspect live: import sys; print("\n".join(sys.path)).

    import machinery, observed 
    import sys
print("exe set:", bool(sys.executable))  # WHICH python exists (print it raw in YOUR venv check — Ch. 17)
print("path ok:", isinstance(sys.path, list) and len(sys.path) > 0)  # [0] = script dir (or ''/cwd nuances)
import math
print("cached:", sys.modules["math"] is math)  # True — second import reuses, never re-runs
print("stdlib?", "site-packages" not in getattr(math, "__file__", "builtin"))
# Find WHERE a name would come from (diagnose shadowing!):
import importlib.util
print("json found:", importlib.util.find_spec("json") is not None)  # (print the spec raw to see its origin!)
    exe set: True path ok: True cached: True stdlib? True json found: True

    The shadowing catastrophe. Your script's directory is first on sys.path — so a file named random.py, math.py, csv.py, or email.py in your folder hijacks the stdlib module of that name for every import in the process (including imports inside other libraries!). Symptoms: AttributeError: module 'random' has no attribute 'randint' (your file isn't the stdlib!), ImportError loops. Rule: never name files after stdlib/third-party modules (check: python -c "import NAME" succeeding = taken). Diagnose with print(module.__file__) — if it points at your folder, you've been shadowed.

    💡 Modules run ONCE (import caching)import x twice (even from different files) executes x.py once; later imports reuse sys.modules["x"]. Consequences: module-level code is initialization (runs at first import — keep it light/pure!); "reloading" needs importlib.reload(x) (fiddly — stale references linger; prefer restarting the process); circular imports see half-initialized modules (§16.5).

    16.3 The __main__ guard: library AND script in one file

    What. When Python loads a file, it sets the module-global __name__: to "__main__" if the file is the entry point (run directly: python stats_utils.py), or to the module name if imported (import stats_utils → __name__ == "stats_utils"). The guard if __name__ == "__main__": runs demo/CLI/self-test code only in direct execution — imports stay side-effect-free.

    stats_utils.py — the guarded dual-purpose file 
    # stats_utils.py
"""Tiny stats helpers (importable + directly runnable)."""

def mean(xs):
    if not xs:
        raise ValueError("mean of empty")
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)

def main():
    import sys
    nums = [float(x) for x in sys.argv[1:]] or [1, 2, 3]
    print("mean:", mean(nums))

if __name__ == "__main__":   # direct run ONLY: python stats_utils.py 4 5 6
    main()                   # import SKIPS this (no prints, no argv parsing, no exit!)

    Why without-the-guard hurts. Unguarded script code (prints, file writes, sys.exit, heavy compute, input prompts) runs at import time — slowing every importer, breaking tests (a stray input() hangs pytest!), and executing side effects in contexts that never asked. Professional rule: module top level = definitions + constants only; all action lives in functions, entered via main() + guard (or a real entry point — §16.6).

    Anatomy of main(). Parse args → call pure library functions → print/write results → return exit code (int) → raise SystemExit(main()) as the guard body for proper exit statuses (Project 5's CLI does exactly this). Keep main() thin: orchestration only — logic belongs in testable functions (Chapter 23).

    16.4 Packages: folders as namespaces + absolute vs relative imports

    What. A package is a directory containing __init__.py (executed on first import of anything in the package; keep it tiny: version, key re-exports, nothing heavy) plus modules/subpackages. Dots navigate: sales_tool.analysis.mean_by. (Namespace packages without __init__.py exist (3.3+) for split-across-folders mega-projects — ignore until you need them.)

    professional project layout (memorize this shape) 
    sales_tool/                    # PROJECT ROOT (has README, requirements, .git)
├── pyproject.toml             # modern metadata+deps (or requirements.txt — Ch. 17)
├── README.md                  # setup + usage (3 commands to green!)
├── sales_tool/                # THE PACKAGE (import sales_tool...)
│   ├── __init__.py            # tiny: __version__, maybe key re-exports
│   ├── readers.py             # load CSV/JSON → Dataset (I/O boundary)
│   ├── analysis.py            # PURE functions (mean_by, totals...) — testable!
│   └── cli.py                 # argparse + main() + __main__ guard (thin shell)
└── tests/
    ├── __init__.py
    └── test_analysis.py       # pytest: imports sales_tool.analysis (Ch. 23)

    Absolute (preferred) vs relative (fragile). Absolute: from sales_tool.analysis import mean_by — full path from project root; explicit, greppable, refactor-safe; works everywhere once the root is importable. Relative: from .analysis import mean_by / from ..x import y — relative to the current package; breaks the moment someone runs the file directly (python sales_tool/cli.py → "attempted relative import with no known parent package"). Rule: absolute everywhere; run package code with python -m sales_tool.cli from the root (-m sets up package context + cwd on path — §16.5's table).

    __init__.py: tiny and deliberate 
    # sales_tool/__init__.py
"""Sales analysis toolkit."""
__version__ = "0.1.0"
from sales_tool.analysis import mean_by  # curated shortcut: sales_tool.mean_by works
__all__ = ["mean_by", "__version__"]     # controls `from sales_tool import *` (still avoid *)

    16.5 Circular imports + script-vs--m (the two import riddles)

    Circular imports (a imports b, b imports a). At runtime: importing a starts executing it → hits import b → starts executing b → hits import a → finds a half-initialized in sys.modules → b grabs names that don't exist yet → ImportError/AttributeError. Fixes (best first): ① restructure — move shared code to a third module both import (the real fix — cycles signal misplaced responsibilities); ② move one import inside the function that needs it (lazy — hides the smell, acceptable for genuinely optional/heavy deps); ③ import the module not names (import a + a.name at call time — works if use happens post-init).

    python pkg/mod.py` vs python -m pkg.mod (know cold).

    python pkg/mod.pypython -m pkg.mod (from root)
    sys.path[0]pkg/ (the file's folder!)cwd (project root ✓)
    __name__"__main__""__main__"
    Package contextNONE (relative imports die)Full (pkg.mod known; . imports work)
    Sibling importsAccidental (via pkg/ on path — fragile)Proper (absolute from root)

    Rule: run multi-file projects from the root with -m + absolute imports. The script-path form is for single-file scripts only.

    16.6 Standard library tour (the batteries that matter for data work)

    ModuleJobWhere this book uses it
    pathlib, os, sysPaths, process, interpreter infoCh. 15/17, every project
    csv, jsonTable + nested data formatsCh. 15/25, Projects 2–5
    math, statistics, randomNumerics, descriptives, samplingCh. 24/25 (seed with random.seed for reproducibility!)
    datetime, zoneinfoDates/times/timezonesCh. 25 (parse early, keep tz-aware!)
    collectionsCounter, defaultdict, deque, namedtupleCh. 7/8/13, Projects 3–4
    itertools, functoolsLazy combinators, memoization, partialsCh. 19/20 (pipelines!)
    argparse, loggingCLIs, professional diagnosticsProject 5, Ch. 23
    reRegular expressions (text patterns)Ch. 6 §6.9 (use sparingly — readability!)
    unittest.mock/pytestTesting (pytest is third-party)Ch. 23
    ✅ Before pip-installing anything…Check the stdlib first: dates, CSV/JSON, stats basics, CLI parsing, logging, paths, randomness, serialization (pickle — never unpickle untrusted data!), compression (gzip/zipfile/tarfile), concurrency (threading/multiprocessing/asyncio). Every dependency is a liability (supply chain, version drift, install weight) — stdlib is free, reviewed, and already installed.

    16.7 Interview questions

    Q1: Module vs package? What's __init__.py for? What goes in it?
    Module = one .py file (a namespace). Package = directory of modules under a dotted name, marked by __init__.py (runs on first import of anything inside). Keep it tiny: __version__, curated re-exports, __all__ — never heavy compute/side effects (they'd run at import time for every importer).
    Q2: What does `if __name__ == "__main__"` do and why is it mandatory in dual-purpose files?
    Direct runs set __name__ = "__main__"; imports set it to the module name. The guard confines script behavior (arg parsing, prints, exits) to direct execution, keeping imports side-effect-free (fast, test-safe). Without it: imports trigger I/O/prompts/exits — breaking tests and library use.
    Q3: How does Python find imports? Name the shadowing failure and its diagnosis.
    Order: sys.modules cache → script dir → PYTHONPATH → site-packages (venv!) → stdlib. Shadowing: your random.py hijacks stdlib random (script dir is first!) → bizarre AttributeErrors. Diagnose: print(mod.__file__) — your folder = shadowed. Never name files after oms, and the three fixes ranked.
    Mechanism: a→b→a sees a half-initialized module in sys.modules (names missing) → ImportError/AttributeError. Fixes: ① restructure (shared code → third module — the real fix), ② lazy function-level import, ③ module-not-names import + deferred attribute use. Cycles signal misplaced responsibilities — treat as design feedback.

    16.8 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Print sys.path from a script vs REPL vs -m; identify path[0] in each and explain the differences.
    2. Build a 2-file project (lib with 2 functions + guarded demo; script importing it). Show import-silence vs direct-run output.
    3. Plant a shadowing bug (statistics.py in cwd importing stdlib statistics elsewhere); diagnose via __file__; fix by renaming.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. Reproduce a circular import; read the traceback precisely (which name is missing where?); fix by extracting a shared module.
    2. Convert a flat 100-line script into the §16.4 layout (package + tests + guard + -m runs). All behavior identical, imports absolute.
    3. Write a package __init__.py with version + curated re-export + __all__; demonstrate import pkg; pkg.name working.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Explain sys.modules caching consequences: import-time side effects, why reload is fiddly, and how singletons-via-module work (and their test-isolation downside).
    2. Design lazy/optional imports for a heavy dependency (e.g., matplotlib only when plotting): function-level vs TYPE_CHECKING vs try/except-ImportError with helpful message. Argue trade-offs.

    16.9 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Build import_lab.py. A diagnostic script printing: executable, sys.path (annotated: which entry is what), __name__, whether run as script vs -m (detectable how?), resolution (find_spec) of 5 stdlib names + proof none are shadowed, and a deliberate circular-import pair (in temp files) with the captured error + your fix.
