    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: @d = f = d(f) at def-time (closure wrapper + wraps + return fn(*a, **k)) · factories add a level (config → decorator → wrapper) · stacking = outside-in per call (order is behavior!) · with = enter → body → exit-always (exit sees the outcome; truthy suppresses) · generator CMs = one yield in try/finally · ExitStack for dynamic N · register-pattern for plugins. Next: Chapter 22 — debugging at full depth: the professional workflow, tracebacks, pdb, and strategy tables.
    22
    Power patterns · craft

    Python Debugging

    ▾

    What/why. Debugging is not guessing, tweaking, or hoping — it is the scientific method applied to code: observe precisely, hypothesize explicitly, predict observably, test minimally, fix at the cause, verify + lock with a regression test. Seniors debug faster than juniors not by knowing more answers but by following a workflow instead of thrashing (random changes destroy evidence and breed new bugs). Memorize this chapter's loop; it scales from typos to distributed outages — only the instruments change.

    Figure 22.1 — The professional debugging workflow (follow literally)
    1 · UNDERSTAND the error (read it FULLY — type + message + which line)
    ↓
    2 · READ traceback bottom-up (WHAT last line → WHERE frames above)
    ↓
    3 · LOCATE failure (which line? which value? print suspects!)
    ↓
    4 · REPRODUCE minimally (smallest snippet that still breaks — bisect!)
    ↓
    5 · INSPECT state (print/assert/pdb — assumptions vs reality)
    ↓
    6 · TEST one hypothesis (single change → PREDICT → run → confirm/refute)
    ↓
    7 · FIX at the cause (not the symptom!) → 8 · RETEST + REGRESSION TEST (lock it!)

    22.1 Traceback mastery: anatomy + bottom-up protocol + chaining

    traceback anatomy, fully annotated 
    def mean(xs):
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)     # line 2: the crash site (division)

mean([])                         # line 4: the trigger (empty input)
# Traceback (most recent call last):                 ← ① header: frames follow, oldest FIRST
#   File "prog.py", line 4, in <module>               ← ② outer frame (read UP from bottom)
#     mean([])                                       ← ③ its source line (the CALL)
#   File "prog.py", line 2, in mean                   ← ④ inner frame (most recent = crash site)
#     return sum(xs) / len(xs)                       ← ⑤ offending line (0/0!)
# ZeroDivisionError: division by zero                ← ⑥ WHAT: read FIRST — type + reason

    Bottom-up protocol (exact). ① Read the last line fully: exception type (which rule broke?) + message (which values?). ② Move up to the innermost frame (last File… block): file + line number + function + echoed source. ③ Walk outward (up the frames): who called whom with what? — the story of how execution reached the crash. ④ Distinguish crash site (where it died — often innocent!) from fault site (where badness originated — empty list passed at line 4!). Beginners fix the crash site (wrap line 2 in try!) — professionals fix the fault (validate at line 4 / in mean's contract — §22.5).

    Chained tracebacks ("During handling… caused by…"). raise New from old (§14.3) prints both chains: read the bottom chain's last line (the final error), then the upper chain (the root cause). During handling of X, another occurred (implicit, no from) = error-inside-handler — the upper X is context, the lower is what killed you. Long chains in frameworks (10+ frames of library internals): scan for YOUR files (the outermost YOUR-frame = your trigger; innermost YOUR-frame = your last touch before library land).

    22.2 Reproduce minimally: bisection + the minimal snippet

    What. Bisection: repeatedly halve the suspect (code, data, or time) while preserving the failure — 1M rows → 500k → … → 3 rows that still break. Minimal repro: the smallest self-contained snippet exhibiting the bug (ideally <10 lines, no files, no network). Why: tiny repros isolate variables (only one suspect left), run in milliseconds (fast hypothesis cycles), and are shareable (teammates/Stack Overflow/forums demand them — "minimal reproducible example").

    bisection in action (data + code) 
    # Symptom: pipeline dies on full data. Bisect the DATA:
#   rows[:500_000] ✓ works → bug in second half → [:750_000] 💥 → [:625_000] ✓ ...
#   → 3 guilty rows found in ~20 runs (log₂(1M) ≈ 20 — bisection is LOGARITHMIC!)
# Then bisect the CODE: comment halves (§2.3's comment-out!) until 5 guilty lines remain.
# Then FREEZE it as regression material (Ch. 23):
def mean(xs):
    if not xs:                                   # ← the FIX (fault site: contract!)
        raise ValueError("mean of empty sequence")
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)
# Minimal repro preserved as a TEST (fails pre-fix, passes post-fix):
assert mean([2, 4]) == 3.0
try:
    mean([])
    raise AssertionError("should have raised!")
except ValueError:
    print("regression locked: empty → ValueError ✓")
    regression locked: empty → ValueError ✓

    Bisection discipline. Change one variable per run (halve data OR disable code — never both, or evidence confounds). Keep notes (run # : change → result). Stop at understanding, not just green ("it works now, dunno why" = bug relocated, not fixed — it will return). The minimal repro becomes the regression test (§22.5) — debugging output is a test asset, not trash.

    22.3 Instruments I: print-probes + f-string debugging + asserts

    probe craft: labels, !r, =, and strategic placement 
    def pct(part, whole):
    print(f"[pct] in: {part=!r}, {whole=!r}")   # ={ } self-documents; !r exposes whitespace/type!
    result = 100 * part / whole
    print(f"[pct] out: {result=}")
    return result

print(pct(3, 4))
# Strategic placements: function ENTRY (args?) / BRANCH points (which path?) /
# LOOP (first/last iterations? use `if i < 3 or i > n-3`) / BEFORE crash line (suspects!) /
# INVARIANTS (assert, below — executable assumptions!)

def withdraw(balance, amount):
    assert balance >= 0, f"balance {balance} already negative (caller bug!)"
    assert amount > 0, f"amount {amount} not positive"
    assert amount <= balance, f"overdraft: {amount} > {balance}"
    return balance - amount
print(withdraw(100, 30))
# withdraw(100, 500) → AssertionError: overdraft: 500 > 100 (fault PINPOINTED, not downstream!)
# RULE: assert INTERNAL invariants (your logic's promises); validate EXTERNAL input with if/raise (Ch. 14)!
# (python -O STRIPS asserts — never rely on them for user-facing validation!)
    [pct] in: part=3, whole=4 [pct] out: result=75.0 75.0 70

    Probe hygiene. Label every probe ([fn] prefix — grep-able!), print !r (hidden chars visible) + type() when types suspect, throttle loop probes (first/last N — million-line logs help nobody), and remove or log-level them after (stray probes pollute real output — Ch. 23's logging replaces them permanently).

    22.4 Instruments II: pdb + breakpoint() + IDE debugging + post-mortem

    pdb: the 10 commands that cover 95% 
    # Insert EXACTLY where confused (Python 3.7+):
#   breakpoint()          # drops into pdb HERE (honors PYTHONBREAKPOINT=0 to disable globally!)
# At the (Pdb) prompt:
#   l / ll      list code around / LONG-list whole function (know where you are!)
#   n           NEXT line (step OVER calls)
#   s           STEP INTO the call (descend!)
#   c           CONTINUE to next breakpoint / end
#   r           RETURN from current function (run to its exit)
#   p expr      PRINT any expression (full Python! call functions, slice, anything)
#   pp expr     PRETTY-print (nested structures readable!)
#   w           WHERE: stack trace (which frames? how deep?)
#   u / d       UP / DOWN frames (inspect CALLERS' variables!)
#   b line      BREAKpoint at line (b func, b file:line, conditional: b 12, x>5)
#   q           QUIT (abandon — careful: kills program!)
# Post-mortem (inspect the CORPSE after a crash — no repro needed!):
#   python -m pdb script.py  →  c  →  (crash)  →  w, u, p suspects...
# IDE equivalent (VS Code/PyCharm): gutter-click breakpoint → F5 → hover vars,
# WATCH panel (live exprs), CALL STACK pane (click frames!), STEP OVER/INTO buttons.

    When pdb beats probes. Deep stacks (inspect 5 frames up — probes can't), loops (conditional breakpoints beat 10k prints), unknown unknowns ("WHAT is this variable?!" — p type(x), x answers instantly), heisenbugs probes perturb (timing-sensitive — though logging perturbs less than prints). When probes beat pdb: quick single-value checks, production-attached processes (can't interrupt), async/multi-threaded flows (pdb serializes confusingly — log instead). Professionals use both daily.

    💡 Rubber-duck debugging (the zero-tech instrument)Explain your code line by line, aloud (to a duck, a teammate, or the air) — stating what each line actually does (not what you intended). Half of all bugs confess mid-sentence ("…and then i increments — wait, NO, continue SKIPS the increment!"). Do this before escalating to tools or teammates — it's the highest-ROI minute in debugging.

    22.5 Fix at the cause + the strategy table + regression habit

    Cause vs symptom (the senior distinction). Symptom fix: wrap the crash line in try/except (crash gone, corruption continues — the empty-mean now silently returns None and poisons downstream stats!). Cause fix: enforce the contract at the fault site (mean raises on empty; caller validates input upstream). Tests: would the fix survive different triggers of the same fault? does it make the bug impossible (validation/types) or merely silent? Symptom fixes multiply; cause fixes divide.

    SymptomFirst moveUsual cause → fix site
    Exception + tracebackBottom-up read (§22.1); minimal reproBad value/type at named line → validate at source
    Wrong result, no errorAssert intermediates; bisect probesLogic/off-by-one/aliasing → the wrong line, + test
    Works once, fails laterDiff states (mutation? globals? exhausted gen?)Shared mutable / single-use iterator → copy/factory
    Only on big dataShrink (bisect rows); profile memoryO(n²)/materialization/leak → stream (Ch. 19/24)
    Only on their machineCompare exe + versions (§17.5)Wrong venv / drifted pins → freeze + recreate
    Flaky / intermittentSeed randomness; log orderingsSet/dict-order assumptions, races, time — make deterministic
    Slow (not wrong)Profile (Ch. 24!) — never guessHotspot ≠ hunch 90% of the time → measure first
    ✅ The regression habit (debugging's deliverable)Every fixed bug earns a regression test (Ch. 23) that FAILS on old code and PASSES on new — run it against both to prove it. Bugs recur (refactors resurrect them); tests remember. A fix without a test is a rumor. File the minimal repro AS the test — §22.2's pattern, every time.

    22.6 Interview questions

    Q1: Walk me through your debugging workflow on a production traceback.
    Recite §22.0's loop with a real example: read last line (type+message) → innermost frame (crash site) → outward frames (trigger story) → separate crash site from fault site → minimal repro (bisect data/code) → probes/pdb single-hypothesis tests → fix at cause (contract/validation, not symptom-suppression) → retest + regression test. Mention rubber-ducking and one-change-per-run discipline.
    Q2: pdb vs print-debugging — when each? Name the key pdb commands.
    pdb: deep stacks (u/d frames), loops (conditional b), unknowns (p anything), post-mortem (python -m pdb). Probes: quick checks, uninterruptible processes, async flows (log instead). Commands: l(ist), n(ext), s(tep), c(ontinue), p/p(p), w(here), u/d, b(reak), q(uit). IDE: same power visual (watch/call-stack/hover).
    Q3: Cause vs symptom fix — with an example of each for the same bug.
    Bug: mean([]) ZeroDivisionError. Symptom fix: try/except around the division returning None — crash gone, but silent None corrupts downstream stats (and every caller must None-check). Cause fix: mean raises ValueError on empty (contract!) + caller validates upstream — impossible states unrepresentable, fault localized, regression-tested. Test: does the fix survive other triggers of the same fault?

    22.7 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Take 3 old tracebacks; annotate every frame (file/line/fn) + the bottom line in plain words; mark crash site vs fault site for each.
    2. Add 5 asserts to a past function (entry/branch/exit invariants); trigger 2 deliberately; note how assertion messages beat raw tracebacks.
    3. Solve one bug using ONLY breakpoint() + n/p/w/u (no probes). Journal each command + what it revealed.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. Debug blind (full workflow + journal): avg(xs) = sum/len called with [], ["3"], a generator, None. Deliver: repros, fault analysis, cause-fix, regression tests.
    2. Bisect a 10k-row CSV with one poison row to the exact row in ≤14 runs (log₂); automate the bisection loop.
    3. Post-mortem drill: crash a 3-level script under python -m pdb; navigate to the outermost YOUR-frame; inspect + explain without re-running.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. "Passes on 1k rows, OOMs on 10M": full diagnosis plan (which stage grows? list vs gen? tracemalloc evidence?) + streaming fix + before/after peak-RSS numbers.
    2. "Flaky test, fails 1 in 20": determinism audit (seeds? set-order? time? tmp collisions?) → reproduce reliably → fix → prove 200 green runs.

    22.8 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Run bug_hunt.py. Take (or write) a 60-line data script with FIVE planted bugs (one per strategy-table row: exception, logic, stateful, scale-only-via-size-param, order-assumption). Debug each with a written journal (workflow step → evidence → hypothesis → test → fix → regression test). Deliverable: the journal + 5 tests. Debugging is a written discipline — the journal is the skill.

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: 8-step workflow (understand → bottom-up → locate → repro-minimal → inspect → one-hypothesis → cause-fix → regression-test) · crash site ≠ fault site (fix the fault!) · bisect data+code (logarithmic!) · probes labeled + !r/=; asserts for internals, raises for boundaries · pdb (breakpoint, n/s/c/p/w/u/b, post-mortem) + rubber-duck first · strategy table per symptom · every fix ships a regression test. Next: Chapter 23 — writing professional Python at full depth: style, naming, logging, config, and testing.
    23
    Craft

    Writing Professional Python

    ▾

    What/why. Code is read 10× more than written — by future-you (who forgot everything), teammates, reviewers, and interviewers. "Working" is the minimum; professional means readable (PEP 8, names, small functions — readable code gets fixed faster and breaks less), reliable (tested, logged, configured — failures are caught early and diagnosed fast), and reusable (DRY, modular, documented — the second use costs ~zero). This chapter turns working scripts into maintainable software: style automation, naming, function craft, logging, configuration, type-checking, and pytest testing — with the economics of why each practice pays.

    23.1 PEP 8 + automation: style without arguments

    What. PEP 8 is Python's style guide (naming, spacing, line length, idioms). The professional move isn't memorizing it — it's automating it: formatters rewrite code (Black/Ruff-format: canonical layout, zero decisions) and linters flag problems (Ruff/Flake8: unused imports, shadowing, banned patterns, complexity). Run both on every save + in CI: style debates end forever, diffs stay minimal, reviews discuss logic.

    Rule (essence)DoDon'tEnforced by
    Namestotal_sales, mean_score(), MAX_N, DataLoaderx2, Tmp, doStuff()Linter (N818 etc.) + review
    Lines ≤ 88–100Break with parens (formatter does it)200-char monsters, \ continuationsFormatter
    Spacingf(a, b), x = 1, 2 blank lines around top defsf(a,b), x=1, walls of codeFormatter
    Idiomsif x is None, if items:, with open== None, len(x)>0, bare openLinter (E711/E501…) + this book!
    ImportsTop, grouped stdlib→3rd→local, one per lineMid-file, import os,sys, *Linter (isort rules)
    ComplexitySmall fns, guards, ≤ ~10 branches/fn50-line nested pyramidsLinter (C901) + review
    tooling: install once, run always 
    # Install (dev tools live in the venv too!):
#   pip install ruff pytest
# Run (root of project):
#   ruff check .          # lint: errors, smells, banned patterns
#   ruff format .         # format: canonical layout (like Black, faster)
#   ruff check --fix .    # auto-fix what's safe (unused imports, etc.)
# Editor: format-on-save + lint-on-type (VS Code Ruff extension — 2 minutes setup)
# CI gate: same commands must pass before merge (style enforced by robots, not humans)
    ❌ Script smell (works, rots)
    def f(d):
      x=[]
      for r in d:
        if r[2]>0:
          x.append((r[0],r[2]*1.2))
      return x
    Magic index 2, magic 1.2, meaningless names — every reader reverse-engineers it.
    ✅ Professional (same logic, maintainable)
    TAX_MULT = 1.2
    def taxed_items(rows):
      """Return (name, taxed_price) for rows with positive price."""
      return [(r["name"], r["price"] * TAX_MULT)
        for r in rows if r["price"] > 0]
    Named constant, named fields, docstring, one idea — readable in seconds.

    23.2 Naming + function craft (the readability core)

    Naming economics. Names are read hundreds of times per write — every extra second of name-decoding multiplies across readers × readings. Rules: full words (count not cnt — except universal i/n/df/ax), units in names (timeout_s, size_mb, price_cents — prevents Mars-orbiter-class bugs!), booleans as predicates (is_valid, has_rows, needs_retry), functions as verb+object (parse_date, total_revenue), collections plural (rows, users), no type-encoding (name_str — hints do this!), no lies (get_ must not mutate; list must be a list).

    Function craft (from §11.9, enforced). One job (no "and") · ~5–20 lines · ≤3–4 params (bundle config!) · pure core, I/O shell · return values, print at edges · guards over nesting · no magic literals (name them!) · docstring + hints on anything shared. Plus: DRY with judgment — duplicate twice, abstract on the third (premature abstraction couples things that merely look alike — "rule of three"). And YAGNI (You Aren't Gonna Need It): no speculative generality — extension points earn their existence.

    magic → named, nested → guarded (before/after) 
    # BEFORE: magic + nesting (works; unreadable; untestable pieces)
def proc(r):
    if r:
        if len(r) < 10000:
            t = 0
            for x in r:
                if x > 0:
                    t += x * 1.2
            return t / len(r)
    return 0

# AFTER: named + guarded + decomposed (same math, professional)
TAX_MULT = 1.2
MAX_ROWS = 10_000

def mean_taxed(values):
    """Mean of positive values scaled by TAX_MULT (empty → 0.0)."""
    if not values:
        return 0.0
    if len(values) >= MAX_ROWS:
        raise ValueError(f"{len(values)} rows exceeds {MAX_ROWS}")
    taxed = [v * TAX_MULT for v in values if v > 0]
    return sum(taxed) / len(values)

print(proc([10, -5, 20]), mean_taxed([10, -5, 20]))  # 12.0 12.0 — same answer, different futures
    12.0 12.0

    23.3 Logging: diagnostics that scale (never print in libraries)

    What/why. logging replaces prints with leveled, named, routable, lazy diagnostics: levels filter noise (DEBUG<INFO<WARNING<ERROR<CRITICAL), logger names locate sources (sales.readers), handlers route (console/file/syslog…), lazy %s-args skip formatting when disabled, and exception() attaches tracebacks in one call. Prints have none of this — and pollute stdout (which may be piped data!).

    logging: setup once, log everywhere 
    import logging
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format="%(asctime)s %(levelname)s %(name)s: %(message)s")
log = logging.getLogger("sales")          # module logger (use __name__ in real modules!)

rows_loaded = 1200
log.debug("raw sample: %r", [1, 2])       # hidden at INFO (zero format cost — lazy %s!)
log.info("loaded %d rows", rows_loaded)   # normal operations (no f-string — lazy!)
log.warning("dropping %d bad rows", 37)   # concerning but handled
try:
    1 / 0
except ZeroDivisionError:
    log.exception("math failed")          # ERROR + FULL TRACEBACK, one call
# Levels: DEBUG (diagnose) < INFO (normal) < WARNING (handled-bad) < ERROR (failed op) < CRITICAL (dying)
# Libraries: NEVER basicConfig (app owns config!) — just getLogger(__name__) + NullHandler default.
    ... INFO sales: loaded 1200 rows ... WARNING sales: dropping 37 bad rows ... ERROR sales: math failed Traceback (most recent call last): ... ZeroDivisionError: division by zero

    Log craft. Log decisions and boundaries (loaded N, dropped M because…, retrying, using fallback X) — not every loop iteration (volume!). Include counts + reasons + samples (dropping 37 rows (bad age); e.g. row 8812). Errors log once, at handling time (with traceback) — re-logging up the chain spams. Secrets never (passwords/tokens/keys — neither values nor full URLs-with-credentials).

    23.4 Configuration: no magic in code (args/env/files)

    What/why. Paths, thresholds, URLs, batch sizes, model names — anything that varies by run/machine/secret — must live outside code: CLI args (argparse — per-run knobs), environment variables (per-machine/secrets: os.environ["API_KEY"] — never in git!), config files (TOML/JSON/YAML — structured, reviewable, versioned). Hardcoding forces code edits for ops changes (risky), leaks secrets into git history (forever!), and blocks reuse across environments.

    config layering: defaults < file < env < CLI (precedence!) 
    import argparse, os

def build_config(argv=None):
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Analyze sales.")
    p.add_argument("--input", default=os.environ.get("SALES_INPUT", "sales.csv"))
    p.add_argument("--min-age", type=int, default=18)     # type-checked + documented free!
    p.add_argument("--verbose", action="store_true")
    args = p.parse_args(argv)
    if args.min_age < 0:                                # validate EARLY (fail fast, clear msg)
        p.error("--min-age must be ≥ 0")
    return args

cfg = build_config(["--input", "q3.csv"])
print(cfg.input, cfg.min_age, cfg.verbose)  # q3.csv 18 False
# Secrets pattern: token = os.environ["API_KEY"]  (KeyError = loud missing-secret — GOOD!)
# Never: API_KEY = "sk-..." in code (git history remembers FOREVER — rotate + purge if leaked!)
    q3.csv 18 False

    23.5 Type hints + mypy: static safety for dynamic code

    hints that pay rent (3.9+ builtin generics) 
    from __future__ import annotations  # (3.7–3.9 nicety; 3.10+ fine without for most)

def mean(xs: list[float]) -> float: ...
def get_user(uid: int) -> dict[str, object] | None: ...   # 3.10+ X | None (or Optional[X])

# mypy (install: pip install mypy; run: mypy src/): finds WRONG-TYPE calls WITHOUT running:
#   get_user("abc")  →  error: Argument 1 has incompatible type "str"; expected "int"
# Adopt gradually: hint NEW code fully → `mypy --strict` on new modules → expand.
# Hints are DOCS the IDE enforces (autocomplete, inline errors) + the reviewer reads.

    Hint economics. Cost: ~5% more keystrokes on signatures. Payoff: IDE catches wrong-arg bugs while typing, mypy catches cross-file type bugs pre-commit, signatures become executable documentation (types > prose for contracts). Skip hints only for throwaway 5-liners. (Runtime enforcement (pydantic, beartype) exists for boundaries that must verify — APIs, configs, plugins.)

    23.6 Testing with pytest: prove it works (the reliability core)

    What/why. Tests are executable specifications: they prove behavior today and guard it forever (refactors/bugfixes verified in seconds, regressions caught at commit-time not customer-time). pytest: write plain test_*.py functions with assert — no boilerplate, great failure output, fixtures/parametrize for scale. What to test: pure functions exhaustively (happy + empty + edge + error paths — §11.1's testability argument!), I/O boundaries with tmp fixtures (tmp_path), regressions per fixed bug (§22.5). Coverage target: critical paths 100%, overall high — but meaningful asserts over percentage theater.

    tests/test_analysis.py — the shape of good tests 
    # tests/test_analysis.py  →  run: pytest -q  (add -x: stop at first fail, -v: verbose, -k: filter)
import pytest

def mean(xs):
    if not xs:
        raise ValueError("mean of empty")
    return sum(xs) / len(xs)

def test_mean_basic():                        # happy path
    assert mean([1, 2, 3]) == 2.0

@pytest.mark.parametrize("xs,expected", [    # MANY cases, one function (table-driven!)
    ([5], 5.0), ([0, 0], 0.0), ([-2, 2], 0.0), ([0.1, 0.2], pytest.approx(0.15)),
])
def test_mean_cases(xs, expected):
    assert mean(xs) == expected                # approx for FLOATS (§4.2 — never ==)!

def test_mean_empty_raises():                 # error path (contract!)
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="empty"):
        mean([])

def test_mean_does_not_mutate():              # purity/contract property
    xs = [3, 1, 2]
    mean(xs)
    assert xs == [3, 1, 2]                    # input untouched (aliasing guard — Ch. 3!)

    Test craft. Name as specifications (test_empty_raises_value_error). One behavior per test (failures pinpoint!). Arrange-Act-Assert structure. Parametrize tables over copy-paste. approx for floats. tmp_path fixture for files (auto-cleaned). monkeypatch for env/time/randomness (deterministic!). Run on every save (watch) + every commit (CI gate) — unrun tests are fiction.

    23.7 READMEs + review checklist (the professional surface)

    README skeleton (3 commands to green!) 
    # sales-tool — one-line WHAT + WHY
# Setup:  python -m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate && pip install -r requirements.txt
# Usage:  python -m sales_tool.cli profile data/sales.csv --min-age 18
# Tests:  pytest -q  |  Lint: ruff check . && ruff format --check .
# Layout: readers/ (I/O) → analysis/ (pure) → cli (shell)  +  decisions (WHY, not what)

    Self-review checklist (before every commit/PR). ☐ Formatted + linted? ☐ Names honest? ☐ Functions small/pure/guarded? ☐ No magic (constants/config)? ☐ No prints (logging)? ☐ Errors narrow + messaged? ☐ Hints on new signatures? ☐ Tests for new behavior (+ regressions)? ☐ README/decisions updated? ☐ No secrets? Run it literally for a month — then it's instinct.

    23.8 Interview questions

    Q1: logging vs print — the full technical case?
    Levels (filterable verbosity), logger names (source location), handlers (console/file/syslog routing), lazy %-args (zero-cost-when-disabled), exception(traceback) in one call, thread-safety, and config-without-code-edits. Prints lack all six and pollute stdout (piped data!). Libraries must never configure logging (app owns it) — only emit via module loggers.
    Q2: What makes code "clean" — beyond working? (Give the review checklist.)
    Readable (names/units/verbs, small fns, guards, no magic, formatted), reliable (tested incl. edges+errors, logged, configured, hinted), reusable (DRY-by-three, modular, documented decisions). Cite §23.7's checklist + economics (read-10×-written; tests pay at refactor-time; names multiply across readers).
    Q3: How do you test data-analysis code? (pure vs I/O vs floats vs determinism)
    Pure cores exhaustively (happy/empty/edge/error, parametrized tables, approx for floats, purity asserts). I/O via tmp_path fixtures + golden files. Randomness/time/env via monkeypatch seeds (deterministic!). Regressions per bug (fail-old/pass-new proven). Run on save + CI gate. Untestable shape (I/O-mixed giants) → refactor to pure-core/imperative-shell first.

    23.9 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. Install ruff; run check + format on 3 old scripts; accept everything; journal each change class (what rule? why better?).
    2. Convert 8 prints to leveled logs (right level each + lazy args); add one exception(); show level-filtering (INFO hides DEBUG).
    3. Rename-a-thon: take a cryptic 40-liner; rename every name per §23.2 (units! predicates! verbs!); diff readability.
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. Refactor a 60-line script: extract 3+ pure functions + constants + config (argparse) + main() + guard + 8 pytest tests (incl. 2 error-paths). Before/after line counts + test run.
    2. Hint a module fully; run mypy; fix every error (no ignores); note which errors were REAL bugs vs annotation gaps.
    3. Write the §23.7 README for a project; hand it to someone cold; fix every step they stumble on.
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. Review a peer's PR-diff (or your old code) with §23.7's checklist: file every finding as rule + evidence + fix (review-shaped, not complaint-shaped).
    2. Design logging strategy for a nightly pipeline (levels per event class, rotation, alerting thresholds, secret-safety); defend each choice against "just print".

    23.10 Mini-challenge 🏆

    Challenge

    Professionalize end-to-end. Take your messiest working script: ruff-clean → rename → decompose (pure core + shell) → constants + argparse config → logging → hints + mypy-clean → pytest suite (≥10 tests, all paths) → README + checklist sign-off. Deliver before/after + test output + a 5-line "decisions" note. This is the exact bar Projects and the Capstone grade against.

    🏁 Chapter summary → nextRemember: automate style (ruff format+lint, on-save + CI) · name economics (units! predicates! verbs!) · craft functions (one job, small, pure, guarded, no magic) · logging over print (levels/names/lazy/exception) · config out of code (args > env > file; secrets NEVER in git) · hints + mypy (docs that check) · pytest everything (parametrize, approx, tmp_path, regressions) · README + self-review checklist per commit. Next: Chapter 24 — performance at full depth: measure-first, Big-O, structures, vectorization, and memory.
    24
    Craft

    Performance with Python

    ▾

    What/why. Slow code has causes, not vibes — and the first law is MEASURE FIRST: hunches about hotspots are wrong ~90% of the time (profilers exist because intuition fails). This chapter's ladder: ① Algorithm/structure (O(n²)→O(n), list→set — 1000× wins), ② Vectorize/push loops to C (NumPy/pandas/C-builtins — 100×), ③ Lazy/stream (O(n)→O(1) memory — enables scale), ④ Cache/memoize (recompute→lookup), ⑤ Parallelize (GIL-aware: threads for I/O, processes for CPU) — and ⑥ don't (readable code that meets its budget is done — premature optimization is the root of evil). Targets set the game: is this a 1-second script or a 24/7 service?

    24.1 Measure first: timeit + perf_counter + cProfile (instruments, exact)

    the three instruments + their jobs 
    import time, timeit, cProfile, pstats, io

# ① timeit: MICRO-benchmark snippets fairly (repeats + disables GC + best-of):
t_join = timeit.timeit("''.join(str(i) for i in range(1000))", number=200)
t_plus = timeit.timeit("s=''\nfor i in range(1000): s += str(i)", number=200)
print(f"join: {t_join:.3f}s  plus-loop: {t_plus:.3f}s  → join wins ~{t_plus/t_join:.0f}×")

# ② perf_counter: MACRO-time blocks in real code (monotonic, ns resolution):
start = time.perf_counter()
total = sum(range(1_000_000))
print(f"sum(1M): {time.perf_counter()-start:.4f}s → {total}")

# ③ cProfile: FIND the hotspot (function-level stats — NEVER guess where time goes!):
def work():
    return {i: i*i for i in range(5000)}
buf = io.StringIO()
cProfile.run("work()", sort="cumulative", stream=buf) if False else None  # (see note)
# Real usage:  python -m cProfile -s cumulative script.py  →  per-function table
print("profile rule: optimize the TOP cumulative row (excl. builtins you can't change)")
    join: 0.042s plus-loop: 0.310s → join wins ~7× (varies — join always wins) sum(1M): 0.0120s → 499999500000 profile rule: optimize the TOP cumulative row (excl. builtins you can't change)

    Instrument discipline. timeit for comparing snippets (fair repeats; report best-of, not mean — noise only inflates). perf_counter for timing stages in real runs (log per-stage timings in pipelines — free production telemetry!). cProfile for finding hotspots (sort cumulative; ignore {built-in} noise; drill into YOUR top rows). Re-profile after every change (fixes relocate bottlenecks — yesterday's #2 is today's #1). Never micro-tune unmeasured code — that's astrology, not engineering.

    ⚠️ Benchmarking trapsOne run (noise! repeat) · tiny N (constant factors dominate — test realistic sizes) · caching between runs (first-call effects — warm up) · time.time() (wall-clock jumps — use perf_counter) · optimizing what profiles say is 1% (Amdahl: 100× on 1% = 1% total!).

    24.2 Big-O for practitioners: the growth table + the usual crimes

    Growthn=10³n=10⁶Name + example
    O(1)11Constant: dict/set lookup, append, index
    O(log n)~10~20Log: bisect, balanced-tree ops
    O(n)10³10⁶Linear: single pass, sum, max, map
    O(n log n)~10⁴~2×10⁷Linearithmic: sorted() — the price of order
    O(n²)10⁶10¹² ✖Quadratic: nested loops over same data (THE crime)
    THE crime (O(n²) membership) → THE fix (O(n) set) 
    import time
allowed = list(range(3000))
probes = list(range(0, 6000, 2))
t0 = time.perf_counter(); slow = [p for p in probes if p in allowed]; t1 = time.perf_counter()
allowed_set = set(allowed)
t2 = time.perf_counter(); fast = [p for p in probes if p in allowed_set]; t3 = time.perf_counter()
print(f"list-membership: {t1-t0:.3f}s  set-membership: {t3-t2:.4f}s  same={slow == fast}")
# 9M ==-checks vs 3000 hashes — ~1000× at n=3000, INFINITE gap as n grows (O(n²)→O(n)).
    list-membership: 0.086s set-membership: 0.0001s same=True (varies — 100–1000× typical)

    Crime blotter (recognize on sight). x in list inside a loop (→ set!) · dedupe-by-scan (→ dict/set) · list.insert(0, x) / pop(0) in loops (→ deque) · string += in loops (→ join) · df.append in loops (→ collect + one concat — pandas Part 3!) · re-sorting per query (→ sort once, bisect per query) · nested loops over the same collection (→ index one side into dict/set first). Each is a structure fix — one line, orders of magnitude.

    24.3 Push loops to C: builtins + comprehensions + vectorization preview

    the speed ladder (same sum-of-squares) 
    import timeit
N = 2000
t_loop = timeit.timeit("s=0\nfor i in range(N): s += i*i", globals={"N": N}, number=50)
t_comp = timeit.timeit("sum(i*i for i in range(N))", globals={"N": N}, number=50)
t_map  = timeit.timeit("sum(map(lambda i: i*i, range(N)))", globals={"N": N}, number=50)
print(f"loop: {t_loop:.3f}s  genexpr: {t_comp:.3f}s  map+lambda: {t_map:.3f}s")
print("ladder: explicit-loop (slowest, bytecode/step) < genexpr/builtin (C-accelerated sum) < numpy (C loop, no per-item boxing — Part 3!)")
print("(map+lambda ≈ loop: lambda re-adds per-item Python cost — operator (§20.4) or genexpr instead!)")
    loop: ...s genexpr: ...s map+lambda: ...s (ratios vary; genexpr beats loop ~1.5–2×; numpy would beat both ~50×)

    Why C-wins exist. Every Python-level operation pays interpreter overhead (bytecode dispatch, object boxing, refcounting — §3.2) per item. Builtins/comprehensions run their inner machinery in C (tight loops, no boxing): sum(genexpr) boxes items but accumulates in C. NumPy (Part 3) removes boxing too (typed arrays, vectorized C loops — 50–100× over Python loops). The ladder rule: fewer Python-steps per item = faster — but readability gates every climb (a clear loop beating its budget stays a loop!).

    24.4 Memory: generators + streaming + __slots__ (O(n) → O(1))

    list vs generator footprint (the enabling win) 
    import sys
N = 200_000
lst = [i*i for i in range(N)]
gen = (i*i for i in range(N))
print(f"list: {sys.getsizeof(lst)/1024:.0f} KiB  generator: {sys.getsizeof(gen)} bytes")
print("math: list pays ~8B pointer + int-object (~28B) per item (~7MB here); generator pays a FRAME (~100B) total.")
print("rule: STREAM whenever you consume once (§19.5 pipeline!); MATERIALIZE only to reuse/sort/len/index.")
# tracemalloc (find the LEAK, not just the size):
#   import tracemalloc; tracemalloc.start() → run → tracemalloc.get_traced_memory() → snapshot.compare_to()
# __slots__: many small objects (millions of rows-as-objects) — drop per-instance __dict__:
class Point:
    __slots__ = ("x", "y")     # fixed fields, no __dict__ (~40-60% smaller per instance!)
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x, self.y = x, y
    list: ... KiB generator: ... bytes (list ~1000× the generator — exact bytes vary)

    Memory discipline. Stream single-pass flows (Ch. 15/19 pipelines scale to ∞ rows at constant RAM). Release big refs promptly (del/scope-exit — CPython refcounts free immediately, §3.2). Prefer iterators across stage boundaries (map/filter/islice — §20.3). Suspect a leak? tracemalloc snapshots diffed across iterations name the growing allocation site (usually: appending cache with no bound, or listeners/handlers never removed).

    24.5 Caching + parallelism: reuse work, then multiply workers (GIL-aware!)

    Cache (Ch. 20's lru_cache, strategic). Recompute→lookup for pure, repeated, hashable-arg calls (parsed configs, compiled regexes — re caches internally!, fib-style recursion — §11.6/20.4). Bound it (maxsize) on wild inputs; never cache IO/time/random. Parallelism (GIL map). CPython's GIL serializes Python bytecode across threads — so: threads for I/O-bound (network/disk waits release the GIL — concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor, 10–100× throughput on fetches) vs processes for CPU-bound (separate interpreters, separate GILs — ProcessPoolExecutor, ~N× on N cores; pays pickling + startup). NumPy/pandas ops release the GIL internally (vectorized code parallelizes differently — Part 3). Async (asyncio) = cooperative I/O concurrency (huge fan-out, one thread — beyond Part 1's scope; know the name).

    executor map (same interface, different engines) 
    from concurrent import futures

def fetch(url):
    return f"data-of-{url}"          # stand-in for requests.get (I/O-bound → THREADS)

urls = ["a", "b", "c", "d"]
with futures.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as pool:
    print(list(pool.map(fetch, urls)))     # 4 concurrent fetches (GIL released while waiting!)

def heavy(n):
    return sum(i*i for i in range(n))      # CPU-bound → PROCESSES (shown single-call here)
print(heavy(100000))
# with futures.ProcessPoolExecutor() as pool: list(pool.map(heavy, [...]))  # needs __main__ guard (Ch. 16!) + picklable fns
    ['data-of-a', 'data-of-b', 'data-of-c', 'data-of-d'] 333328333350000

    24.6 Interview questions

    Q1: "My Python is slow" — your exact process?
    Measure-first: reproduce at realistic N → profile (cProfile cumulative) → top row is the suspect → classify (algorithm/structure? Python-loop? memory? recompute?) → fix at that level (set/dedupe·builtin/genexpr·stream·cache) → re-profile (bottlenecks relocate!) → stop at budget. Never micro-tune unmeasured; Amdahl governs (100× on 1% ≈ nothing). Cite a real crime→fix (membership list→set).
    Q2: GIL: what, and how does it shape threads vs processes vs async vs NumPy?
    GIL serializes Python-bytecode execution per interpreter → threads don't parallelize CPU work (fine for I/O — waits release it; ThreadPool for fetches). CPU parallelism: processes (separate GILs; pickling cost) or C-extensions releasing the GIL (NumPy vectorized ops). Async: single-threaded cooperative I/O fan-out (not parallelism — concurrency). Choose by bound: I/O→threads/async, CPU→processes/vectorize.
    Q3: List vs generator trade-offs — when must you materialize?
    Generators: O(1) memory, lazy, single-pass (no len/index/reuse/sort) — for pipelines consumed once (§19.5). Materialize (list) to: iterate repeatedly, index/slice, len(), sort, or debug-inspect. Size math: pointer+object per item vs fixed frame (~1000× at scale). Default streaming at boundaries; materialize deliberately with a comment (WHY reuse needs it).

    24.7 Exercises

    Level 1 · Understand
    1. timeit 5 idiom pairs (join vs +=, set vs list membership, genexpr vs loop, in dict vs in list, sort-once+bisect vs re-sort); report ×-factors.
    2. Profile a 3-stage script with cProfile; identify the top row; verify "hunch vs profile" differ (write both down first!).
    3. getsizeof ladder: int/str/tuple/list/dict/set/generator of N items; explain per-item costs (§3.2 boxing).
    Level 2 · Apply
    1. Take an O(n²) script (nested membership/dedupe); fix via set/dict-index; show timings at 3 sizes proving the growth change (plot optional).
    2. Convert a list-pipeline to a generator pipeline; prove O(1) memory (tracemalloc peak) at 10× rows; keep outputs identical (assert).
    3. ThreadPool a 20-URL fetch (simulated latency); show wall-time ≈ slowest-single (not sum); then explain why CPU work wouldn't scale the same (GIL demo).
    Level 3 · Interview
    1. "1B-row log, 8GB RAM, 5-minute budget": architecture memo (streaming stages? chunking? which structure per stage? where processes? what telemetry?) with Big-O + memory math per stage.
    2. Find a REAL slowdown in your code via profiling (not guessing); fix; present before/after profile excerpts + the one-line lesson (crime→fix pattern name).

    25
    Bridge

    Python for Data Science

    ▾

    What/why. This chapter is the bridge: 24 chapters of language become the profession. Python won data science for concrete reasons (readable glue + C-speed libraries + notebooks + ecosystem), and every chapter maps to daily DS work: files→ingestion, dicts→records, exceptions→dirty data, functions→pipelines, OOP→components, performance→scale. No NumPy/pandas/ML teaching here (Parts 2–6!) — instead: why Python, the workflow map, chapter-to-job links, pure-Python DS patterns (profiling, cleaning, splitting, seeding, timing), and the series roadmap. Read this, and Part 1's purpose snaps into one picture.

    25.1 Why Python won data science (structural reasons, not hype)

    ReasonMechanismEvidence
    Readable glueSimple syntax + batteries-included → analysts AND engineers share one languageNotebook-to-production path exists (prototype Friday, pipeline Monday)
    C-speed librariesNumPy/pandas/scikit-learn: Python API over C/Fortran kernels (Ch. 24's ladder!)Python UX at ~C speed for array/table/model ops
    Notebook loopJupyter: code + output + narrative in cells (REPL thinking at scale — §2.6)Exploration, teaching, reporting share one artifact
    Ecosystem gravityEvery dataset/API/model ships Python-first (pip-installable — Ch. 17)New papers ship .py; Kaggle/Colab default Python
    General-purposeSame language scrapes, cleans, serves, automates (web/APIs/DBs/DevOps)No handoffs between "analysis" and "engineering" languages

    The honest limits (know them!). Raw Python loops are slow (→ vectorize, Part 3); the GIL shapes parallelism (→ processes/C-extensions, §24.5); dynamic typing needs discipline (→ hints/tests, Ch. 23); R/Julia/SQL/Spark win niches (stats DSLs, HPC, queries, clusters — polyglot teams are normal). Python's moat is breadth × readability × libraries, not any single benchmark.

    25.2 The data-science workflow (where Part 1 lives in it)

    Figure 25.1 — Workflow stages and their Part-1 foundations
    ASK (question → measurable target; config it — Ch. 23)
    ↓
    GET (files/APIs/DBs → ingestion: open/csv/json/pathlib — Ch. 15, requests later)
    ↓
    CLEAN (missing/dupes/types/outliers → pure functions + exceptions — Ch. 11/14)
    ↓
    EXPLORE (profile/distributions/relations → Counter/stats/loops — Ch. 7/8/10)
    ↓

    ↓
    COMMUNICATE (plots/reports/notebooks → f-strings/files — Ch. 6/15) → DEPLOY (venv/requirements/logging — Ch. 17/23)

    Part 1's share. Roughly half of professional DS time is Python-fundamentals work (ingestion, cleaning, validation, plumbing, automation) — done with exactly this book's tools. Parts 2–6 add the specialized layers (SQL/stats → NumPy/pandas → visualization → ML → DL/NLP/LLMs) — each assumes fluency here (a pandas bug is usually a Python bug: aliasing, mutation, types, scope!). Projects 1–5 rehearse the workflow's Python core end-to-end.

    25.4 Pure-Python DS patterns (profiling · cleaning · splits · seeding)

    profile + clean + split + seed (stdlib only!) 
    import csv, json, random
from collections import Counter

random.seed(42)                       # REPRODUCIBILITY: seed ONCE at program start (log it!)
print("seed: 42 (log seeds with results — unseeded runs are unrepeatable!)")

rows = [
    {"id": "1", "age": "30", "city": "Pune"},
    {"id": "2", "age": "", "city": "  pune "},     # missing age, messy city
    {"id": "3", "age": "abc", "city": "Mumbai"},   # bad age
    {"id": "1", "age": "30", "city": "Pune"},      # DUPLICATE
]
# CLEAN: pure per-row (None = reject-with-reason; §14.5 quarantine idea):
def clean(row):
    age = row["age"].strip()
    if not age:
        return None, "missing age"
    try:
        age_i = int(age)
    except ValueError:
        return None, f"bad age {age!r}"
    if not 0 <= age_i <= 120:
        return None, f"age {age_i} out of range"
    return {"id": row["id"], "age": age_i, "city": row["city"].strip().title()}, None

cleaned, rejects = [], []
for i, r in enumerate(rows):
    good, why = clean(r)
    (cleaned if good else rejects).append(good or {"row": i, "why": why})
seen, deduped = set(), []             # DEDUPE by key (set-membership — §24.2!)
for r in cleaned:
    key = (r["id"], r["age"], r["city"])
    if key not in seen:
        seen.add(key)
        deduped.append(r)
# PROFILE: shape + missing + distributions (Counter — §8.3!):
print("clean:", len(deduped), "| rejected:", len(rejects), "| reasons:", Counter(x["why"] for x in rejects))
print("cities:", Counter(r["city"] for r in deduped))
# SPLIT: seeded shuffle → train/test (deterministic!):
random.shuffle(deduped)
cut = int(0.7 * len(deduped)) or 1
train, test = deduped[:cut], deduped[cut:]
print("train/test:", len(train), len(test))
    seed: 42 (log seeds with results — unseeded runs are unrepeatable!) clean: 1 | rejected: 2 | reasons: Counter({'missing age': 1, "bad age 'abc'": 1}) cities: Counter({'Pune': 1}) train/test: 1 0

    Pattern notes. Seeding: one random.seed(N) (logged!) makes shuffles/splits repeatable — papers/pipelines record seeds as first-class config (NumPy/sklearn: random_state=42 — same idea, later parts). Cleaning: pure per-row + reject-with-reason (never silent drops — counts + reasons logged!). Dedupe: key-tuples + set (O(n), §24.2). Splits: shuffle-then-cut at minimum (stratified/grouped splits in ML parts). Tiny here — the shapes scale to millions (stream §19.5 + validate §14.5 + log §23.3).

    How to carry Part 1 forward. Keep the workflows (debug loop §22, review checklist §23.7, pure-core/shell §20.5/23, streaming §19.5) — they're part-independent.     25.6 Interview questions

    Q1: Why is Python dominant in data science — structurally?
    Five structural causes (§25.1): readable glue shared by analysts+engineers (notebook-to-production path), C-speed libraries (Python UX at kernel speed), notebook iteration loop, ecosystem gravity (datasets/APIs/models ship Python-first), general-purpose coverage (scraping→serving in one language). Plus honest limits (loop speed→vectorize, GIL→processes, typing discipline) — seniors name trade-offs, not just wins.
    Q2: Map the DS workflow to Python skills — where does "basic Python" end and DS begin?
    It doesn't — ~half of DS time IS fundamentals (ingestion/cleaning/validation/plumbing per §25.2–25.3). Walk the workflow: ask(config)→get(files/APIs)→clean(pure+exceptions)→explore(collections/stats)→model(classes/seeds)→communicate(strings/files)→deploy(venvs/logging) — each stage's Python core from this book, each later Part adding its specialized layer.
    Q3: Seeding, splits, and reject-with-reason — why do these three matter?
    Seeding: repeatability (logged seed → identical shuffles/splits; unseeded = unrepeatable science). Splits: honest evaluation (test unseen; shuffle-then-cut minimum; stratified later). Reject-with-reason: data integrity (counts+reasons logged, quarantine inspectable — silent drops corrupt silently). Demo §25.4's pattern from memory.

    P1
    Project · Chapters 1–10

    Project 1: Smart Calculator & Unit Lab

    ▾

    Brief. A REPL calculator that evaluates arithmetic safely (no eval on raw input — parse it!), tracks history, and converts units (temperature, length, data-size). Why: proves Chapters 1–10 fluency (I/O, types, operators, strings, lists, dicts, branching, loops) in one shippable tool with real parsing and real edge cases.

    Requirements. ① REPL loop (quit/history/clear commands; Ctrl-C exits cleanly). ② Safe evaluator: tokenize → parse → compute supporting + - * / // % **, parentheses, unary minus, floats (recursive-descent or shunting-yard — YOUR parser, not eval!). ③ Unit lab: 100C to F, 5km to m, 2048MB to GB (normalize units, validate domains — no −300°C!). ④ History: last N with recall (!3 re-runs entry 3). ⑤ Errors that TEACH (3 + → "expected a number after '+'" with position carets, not tracebacks).

    Milestones. M1: REPL + + - * / (no parens) + history list. M2: precedence + parens + unary + // % **. M3: unit conversions + validations. M4: !n recall + error carets + help text. M5: polish (docstrings, hints, README, 15+ asserts-as-tests in a test_calc.py).

    Acceptance. 2 + 3 * 4 → 14 · (2+3)*4 → 20 · 2**3**2 → 512 (right-assoc!) · 7/0 → friendly error · 100C to F → 212 · -40C to F → −40 (the crossover!) · garbage → caret error, REPL survives · !2 replays · 15 tests green.

    Stretch. Variables (x = 5) · functions (sqrt(16)) · --expr "2+2" CLI mode · timing per eval (§21.1).

    P2
    Project · Chapters 6–10, 13, 15

    Project 2: Text Analyzer Studio

    ▾

    Brief. A CLI that profiles any text file: counts, top words (stopword-aware), reading level, n-grams, concordance (keyword-in-context), and a comparison mode (two files' vocab overlap). Why: strings + collections + files + comprehensions at realistic scale — the exact wrangling core of NLP (Part 6 preview, stdlib-only!).

    Requirements. ① analyze book.txt: chars/words/sentences/paragraphs, unique words, type-token ratio, avg word/sentence length. ② Top-N words with stopword filtering (ship a stopwords.txt`; flag: --no-stop). ③ Bigrams/trigrams top-N (zip + Counter). ④ concordance word --width 40: every occurrence with context (generators — don't load 100MB!). ⑤ compare a.txt b.txt: shared vocab %, distinctive words per file (Counter subtraction!). ⑥ Encodings: --encoding flag + errors policy (§15.6); BOM-safe (§15.6's utf-8-sig!).

    Milestones. M1: counts + top words on one file. M2: stopwords + n-grams. M3: concordance (streaming). M4: compare mode. M5: encoding flags + tests + README with sample outputs.

    Acceptance. Self-analyzes its own README correctly (hand-verify top-5!) · 50MB file processes in O(1) memory (prove: tracemalloc peak!) · BOM file reads clean · comparison %s sum sanely · all flags documented in --help.

    Stretch. Reading-ease score (Flesch) · hapax legomena (words used once) · JSON report output · --top configurable everywhere.

    P3
    Project · Chapters 11–17

    Project 3: Expense Tracker Pro

    ▾

    Brief. A personal finance CLI: add/list/summarize expenses stored in CSV, with monthly reports, category budgets + alerts, and JSON export. Why: the full application shape — pure-core logic + I/O shell, validation, persistence, CLI config, venv+requirements, tests — a portfolio piece proving job-ready craft.

    Requirements. ① add DATE AMOUNT CATEGORY [--note] (validate: ISO date, positive amount, known-or-new category with confirm). ② list [--month YYYY-MM] [--category] (sorted, formatted table). ③ report [--month]: totals, per-category, daily average, top-5 notes. ④ budget set CATEGORY AMOUNT + overspend warnings on add (budgets in budgets.json). ⑤ Storage: expenses.csv via csv-module + atomic writes (§15.8!) — kill the process mid-write without corruption (TEST it!). ⑥ export --json out.json (round-trip: re-import equals original).

    Milestones. M1: add+list over CSV (manual testing). M2: report math (pure functions + tests!). M3: budgets + warnings. M4: atomic writes + export/import round-trip. M5: venv + requirements + pytest suite (≥20 tests) + README (setup/usage/examples).

    Acceptance. Fresh clone → README's 3 commands → working app · invalid inputs rejected with guidance (never tracebacks) · mid-write kill loses ≤ the in-flight row (prove!) · 20 tests incl. error paths + round-trip · ruff + mypy clean.

    Stretch. Receipt-note search · --currency config · recurring expenses · lru_cache on monthly aggregates (with invalidation on add!).

    P4
    Project · Chapters 14–21

    Project 4: Robust Data Pipeline

    ▾

    Brief. A production-shaped ETL: stream a messy 100k-row CSV → validate + clean (pure stages) → quarantine rejects with reasons → load clean rows to JSONL + summary report, with retries, timing, logging, and idempotent re-runs. Why: THE data-engineering miniature — every pattern (generators, exceptions, CMs, decorators, logging, atomic writes, ExitStack) composed under dirty-data fire. Interview gold.

    Requirements. ① Generator pipeline (§19.5): read → parse → validate → normalize → write — O(1) memory at 100k rows (prove via tracemalloc!). ② Row contract: typed schema (dataclass!), per-field validators, RowError(row_no, field, reason, raw) — rejects to quarantine.csv WITH reasons (never silent!). ③ Resilience: @retry on the (simulated-flaky) source reader + @timer per stage + counts logged per 10k rows. ④ Outputs: clean.jsonl + report.txt (rows in/out/rejected, per-reason counts, per-stage timings, seed) + run.log — ALL atomic (§15.8). ⑤ Idempotent: re-run overwrites deterministically (same input+seed → byte-identical outputs — assert in tests!). ⑥ --dry-run (validate only, no writes) + --limit N + --seed flags.

    Milestones. M1: streaming read→write skeleton + row counting. M2: schema + validators + quarantine. M3: retries/timers/logging + report. M4: atomic outputs + idempotence proof + flags. M5: dirty-data torture suite (10 corruption types!) + README + architecture note.

    Acceptance. 100k messy rows → correct clean/quarantine split (hand-verify 20 rejects' reasons!) · O(1) memory (peak logged!) · kill mid-run → no partial outputs · re-run → byte-identical · torture suite (bad encodings! ragged rows! null bytes! dup headers!) all handled-or-quarantined, zero crashes.

    Stretch. --workers (ProcessPool chunk fan-out — §24.5!) · schema-as-TOML config · sqlite3 load target · progress bar (hand-rolled, no deps!).

    P5
    Project · Chapters 18–25 · capstone rehearsal

    Project 5: Mini-ML Toolkit (from scratch!)

    ▾

    Brief. Machine learning with zero ML libraries (stdlib only!): a Dataset class (load/profile/clean/split), k-NN + majority-baseline classifiers with sklearn-shaped fit/predict/score, metrics (accuracy/confusion-matrix), and a seeded experiment runner comparing models. Why: proves the book's thesis — Part-1 Python alone builds real ML tooling (and you'll understand sklearn forever, because you've been sklearn).

    Requirements. ① Dataset (dataclass rows!): from_csv (streaming + quarantine, reuse P4's patterns!), profile(), train_test_split(test_size, seed) (seeded shuffle §25.4; assert disjoint + exhaustive!). ② MajorityClassifier (fit=majority label; the baseline every model must beat!) + KNNClassifier(k) (fit=memorize; predict=k-nearest vote, ties→smallest label, document!). ③ Metrics: accuracy, confusion_matrix (nested dict!), per-class precision/recall (pure functions + tests with hand-computed tables!). ④ experiment.py: seeds × k-values grid → results table (stdout + results.csv) → winner + margin-over-baseline verdict. ⑤ OOP discipline:estimators share a base class (documented contract!), dunders where earned (__len__/__repr__), properties for fitted-state guards (predict-before-fit → clear error!).

    Milestones. M1: Dataset load+split (+ split-invariant tests!). M2: Majority + accuracy (first experiment runs!). M3: k-NN + confusion matrix (hand-verified!). M4: experiment grid + precision/recall. M5: polish (hints/mypy, 25+ tests, README with results table + "what I'd try in Part 5" section).

    Acceptance. Bundled 300-row dataset (generate it deterministically!) → full grid runs in seconds · k-NN beats baseline by a logged margin · hand-verified confusion matrix on a 12-row fixture · split invariants hold across 10 seeds · predict-before-fit errors clearly · README's table reproducible via one command.

    Stretch. Min-max scaler (fit-on-train-ONLY discipline!) · stratified split · cross-validation · timing/memory report per k (Ch. 24!).

    🏁 Projects summary → nextRemember: P1 (parse+REPL) → P2 (text at scale) → P3 (shippable app) → P4 (robust pipeline) → P5 (ML from scratch) — each bigger, each reusing prior patterns (that's the compounding!). Ship all five on GitHub with READMEs (your portfolio's foundation!). Next: Interview prep — turning this book into offers.
    🎯
    Career · synthesis

    Python Interview Prep

    ▾

    What/why. Interviews test fluency under pressure: can you explain precisely (theory), code correctly (live), reason about trade-offs (design), and debug systematically (practical)? This section compresses the book into interview-shaped preparation: the guaranteed-asked canon (with chapter pointers — never memorize, always understand + demo), live-coding strategy, DS-role specifics, red flags to avoid, and a 2-week prep plan. Every answer here should be reproducible on a whiteboard with a marker — that's the bar.

    I.1 The canon: 15 guaranteed questions (answer + demo each!)

    #QuestionAnswer spine (expand live!)Ch
    1Mutable vs immutable? Consequences?Identity vs value semantics: aliasing, == vs is, hashability (dict keys!), default-arg trap. DEMO: a=b=[]; a.append(1) + tuple-with-list.3–4
    2List vs tuple vs set vs dict — when each?Ordered/mutable (list) · ordered/fixed/hashable (tuple) · unique/fast-membership (set) · keyed lookup (dict). DEMO: dedupe + band-count + record shapes.7–8
    3List comprehension vs generator?Eager list (reuse/index/len, O(n) RAM) vs lazy iterator (single-pass, O(1)). DEMO: getsizeof + pipeline; sum(x for …).13/19
    4Decorators: what + write one?@d def f ≡ f=d(f) at def-time; closure wrapper; wraps; factory=3 levels; stacking order. DEMO: timer from memory (§21.1).21
    5Context managers: how? Write one?enter→body→exit-always (outcome-visible); class vs @contextmanager+try/finally; suppression. DEMO: temp_flag or cd (§21.6).21
    6*args/**kwargs? Forwarding?Pack/unpack; transparent wrappers f(*a, **k); keyword-only *; forwarding chains. DEMO: traced wrapper (§11.3).11
    7LEGB + global/nonlocal?Lookup chain; assignment binds local; global rebinding (avoid!); nonlocal for closures (§11.8 counter). DEMO: counter both ways.12
    8Shallow vs deep copy?New-container-shared-items vs recursive clone; copy() slicing vs deepcopy; nested-mutation surprise. DEMO: [[]]*3 + fix.3/7
    9Exception handling done right?Narrow excepts, else/finally, chaining (from), custom hierarchies, per-row quarantine. DEMO: parse-float pipeline (§14.5).14
    10OOP: self/inheritance/super/dunder?self=instance binding; inheritance=IS-A+override; super()=MRO coop; dunders=protocols (__len__/__eq__). DEMO: dataclass + one dunder.18
    11GIL? Threads vs processes?GIL serializes bytecode → threads for I/O (released on wait), processes for CPU (own GILs), async for fan-out. DEMO: executor map (§24.5).24
    12How do you debug?8-step workflow: bottom-up traceback → repro-minimal → one-hypothesis → cause-fix → regression test. DEMO: narrate a real bug story.22
    13Big-O of your solution? (always asked!)State time+space with reasoning (nested? hashing? sort?); offer the faster structure. DEMO: membership list→set live (§24.2).24
    14Handle a 10GB file on 4GB RAM?Stream (generator pipeline, O(1)), chunk outputs, never materialize; profile with tracemalloc. DEMO: §19.5 sketch from memory.15/19
    15Reproducible Python project?venv + frozen requirements + seeds logged + README 3-commands + tests. DEMO: §17.6 flow + seed discipline (§25.4).17/23/25

    I.2 Live-coding strategy (the performance, not just the code)

    ✅ Do (scores points)
    Repeat the problem + examples back (alignment!) · ask edge-cases (empty? None? dupes? types?) BEFORE coding · narrate approach + Big-O FIRST (verbal design!) · brute-force then optimize (working > clever!) · test with 3 cases aloud (happy/edge/error) · name well + guard + comment WHY.
    ❌ Don't (red flags!)
    Silent coding (they can't score thoughts!) · jumping to code with no plan · ignoring edge cases (until prompted twice) · clever one-liners needing explanation · "it works, dunno why" · blaming the language · never testing (not even mentally!).

    The 5 classic live problems (drill until fluent). ① FizzBuzz-with-twist (guards + test table). ② Word-frequency top-N (Counter + stopwords + ties!). ③ Parse CSV → per-group means (csv + defaultdict + float-safe). ④ Flatten nested lists (recursion + base cases + depth limit!). ⑤ Retry-with-backoff decorator (factory + wraps + re-raise — §21.2 verbatim!). Each: state Big-O, handle 2+ edges, write 3 asserts.

    Portfolio > promises. Five GitHub projects with READMEs + tests + green runs beat any certificate. Walk interviewers through P4's quarantine design or P5's k-NN-from-scratch — decisions and trade-offs, not just code. Commit history showing iteration (not one giant dump!) signals real process.

    🔑
    Verify · selected solutions

    Exercise Answers (Selected)

    ▾

    How to use. Attempt exercises first (struggle = learning!), then verify here. L1: full answers (foundations must be exact). L2: solution sketches + key results (fill details yourself). L3: approach pointers (interview depth is earned). Mini-challenges: acceptance criteria to self-grade. If an answer surprises you, re-read its section — the gap is the lesson.

    A.1 Chapters 1–5 (setup → operators)

    Ch1 L1: install proof + REPL vs script + versions
    python --version → 3.10+ (3.8 EOL'd — upgrade!). REPL: instant feedback, exploratory, stateful-but-throwaway (§2.6); scripts: saved, rerunnable, shareable, testable. Versions differ in syntax available (match 3.10, X|None 3.10, tomllib 3.11, venv default 3.3+) — always know yours (sys.version).
    Ch2 L2: comment-out debugging + help() discovery
    Bisect-by-comment: halve the file, run, keep the failing half (~log₂(n) steps — §22.2's origin!). help(print) reveals sep/end/file/flush — the file/flush pair previews Ch. 15/23 (redirect + unbuffered logs).
    Ch3 L1/L2: aliasing, == vs is, default-arg trap
    L1: a = b = [] → ONE list, two names; a.append(1) shows in b (aliasing!). == compares values ([1]==[1] True, different objects!); is compares identity (True only same object). L2: default-arg trap — def f(x=[]) shares ONE list across calls (defaults evaluate once!); fix: def f(x=None): x = x or []. [[]]*3: three refs to ONE inner list; fix: [[] for _ in range(3)].
    Ch4 L1/L2: float equality, True==1, conversion table
    0.1+0.2 == 0.3 → False (0.30000000000000004 — binary fractions!); fix: math.isclose / pytest.approx / Decimal-for-money. True == 1 → True (bool subclasses int! — True+True == 2). Conversions: int("3")✓ int("3.5")✗ int(3.9)→3 (truncate-toward-zero, NOT floor for negatives: int(-3.9)→-3!).
    Ch5 L1/L2: precedence, and/or values, banker bugs
    Precedence: 2+3*4**2 = 2+3*16 = 50 (** first, right-assoc: 2**3**2=512!). and/or return OPERANDS (0 or [] or "x" → "x"; "a" and 0 and "b" → 0) — not booleans! Chained a<b<c = a<b and b<c with single evaluation (use for ranges!). not binds loosest of the three: not a == b = not (a==b).

    A.2 Chapters 6–10 (strings → loops)

    Ch6 L1/L2: email pipeline, f-string mastery, split traps
    Pipeline " Ali.Raza@Mail.Com " → strip → lower → split("@") → "ali.raza@mail.com" (§6 verified output!). f-specs: f"{x:.2f}" (2dp), f"{n:,}" (thousands), f"{p:.1%}" (percent), f"{v=!r}" (debug). split() (any-whitespace, strips empties) vs split(" ") (exact-space, keeps empties — "a b".split(" ") → ['a','','b']!). Emails: validate with parts-count + no-spaces (regex in later parts).
    Ch7 L1/L2: methods, aliasing, top-N
    In-place (sort/reverse/append/extend → None!) vs new (sorted/reversed/slice/concat). nums=[5,7,9] → even-search prints "no even number" (verified §10 loop!). Top-N: sorted(scores, reverse=True)[:3] (or heapq.nlargest). Slice assignment a[1:3] = [x] resizes (length can change!).
    Ch8 L1/L2: tuple swap, Counter bands, set ops
    Swap: a, b = b, a (RHS tuple built first — atomic!). Unpacking: first, *rest = xs; _ for throwaways. Counter: Counter(ages) + most_common(3); banding via dict-comp over thresholds. Sets: a | b (union), a & b (intersection), a - b (difference), a ^ b (symmetric) — dedupe + membership at O(1).
    Ch9 L1/L2: guards, banding, truthiness
    Guard order: cheapest/most-discriminating first; validate-then-compute. Grade banding: if score >= 90: "A" elif >= 80… (boundaries tested at 89.999/90!). Ternary for VALUES ("pass" if s>=40 else "fail"), never for actions. Truthiness: empty/None/0/False → False; non-empty/nonzero → True — if items: over len>0.
    Ch10 L1/L2: enumerate/zip, break/else, search pattern
    enumerate(xs, start=1) for numbered; zip stops at shortest (document or zip_longest!). for/else: else runs iff NO break (search-miss handling — the nums=[5,7,9] "no even" demo!). while: condition + progress-guarantee (else infinite — always identify the shrink!). Replacement ladder: loop → comprehension → builtin → (later) vectorize.

    A.3 Chapters 11–15 (functions → files)

    Ch11 L1/L2: signatures, closures, decorators-preview
    Arg order: positional → *args → keyword-only → **kwargs; defaults immutable-or-None. Closure: inner fn + captured cell (__closure__ proof); nonlocal to rebind (counter!). Recursion: base + shrink + trust (trace factorial(3): 3*2*1). Decorator = f = d(f) + wrapper + wraps (full Ch. 21!).
    Ch12 L1/L2: LEGB traces, global/nonlocal, loop-var leakage
    LEGB: Local → Enclosing → Global → Builtin (first hit wins). Assignment binds LOCAL (read-then-assign → UnboundLocalError!). global: rebind module var (avoid — return/params instead). Loop/comprehension vars leak in for (i survives!) but NOT genexps (own scope). Fix shadowing: rename, never reuse builtins (list, id, sum!).
    Ch13 L1/L2: filter-vs-ternary, boundary trace, nesting
    Filter [x for x in xs if p] EXCLUDES; ternary [a if p else b for x in xs] TRANSFORMS (same length!). Boundary: [x*x if x>2 else -x for x in range(6) if x!=0] = [-1,-2,9,16,25] (filter drops 0 FIRST, then ternary maps — verified!). Nesting: at most 2 levels inline; name complex predicates. Genexps: sum(… for …) — O(1).
    Ch14 L1/L2: hierarchy, else/finally, quarantine
    Order excepts specific→broad (ValueError before Exception!). else = try-succeeded path; finally = always (cleanup!). raise New from old preserves cause (traceback shows BOTH). Quarantine: per-row try/except → (good, rejects-with-reason) — counts + reasons, never silent. Custom: subclass Exception (never BaseException!), one per failure mode.
    Ch15 L1/L2: with-guarantee, encodings, atomic writes
    with open: close+flush guaranteed across exceptions/returns (vs leak-prone bare open!). Encoding: ALWAYS encoding="utf-8" (or utf-8-sig for BOM!); errors="replace" for hostile data (logged!). Newline: newline="" for csv (no phantom blanks!). Atomic: write-temp + os.replace (readers see old-or-new, never half!). JSON: json.dump + UTF-8 + round-trip test.

    A.4 Chapters 16–20 (modules → functional)

    Ch16 L1/L2: imports, __main__ guard, circular
    Guard: if __name__ == "__main__": main() (import-safe!). Absolute imports preferred; sys.path[0] = script dir (run as python -m pkg.mod from root!). Circular: A↔B top-level imports → ImportError/partial-module — fix by extracting shared module or deferring import. __all__ declares public API.
    Ch17 L1/L2: venv proof, freeze discipline, triage
    Proof: which python → .venv/… + pip list minimal. Freeze: pip freeze > requirements.txt AFTER verifying (pins transitive too!); recreate: venv → install → run tests. Triage: wrong-exe (which!) → not-installed-here (pip show) → drift (diff freeze) → cache/corrupt (recreate). Never sudo-pip / never system-python projects.
    Ch18 L1/L2: self, properties, dataclasses, dunders
    self: instance the method was called on (binding, not keyword!). Property: computed/validated attribute access (@prop.setter validates!). Dataclass: @dataclass → init/repr/eq free; field(default_factory=list) for mutables; frozen=True for hashable values. Dunders: __len__/__getitem__/__eq__/__repr__ = protocols (implement → builtins work!). super(): cooperative MRO chain (call it!).
    Ch19 L1/L2: iterator protocol, yield-freeze, pipelines
    Protocol: __iter__ + __next__ + StopIteration (for-loop drives it!). yield freezes frame (locals preserved; resumes after!). Single-use: generators exhaust (re-call factory for fresh!). Pipeline: read→parse→filter→aggregate, O(1) (prove: getsizeof + tracemalloc!). yield from delegates (+ returns subgenerator's return!). Infinite: count/repeat/cycle + islice to take.
    Ch20 L1/L2: twins rule, any/all, groupby, lru_cache
    Twins: named-fn → map/filter OK; lambda → comprehension wins; reduction → builtin first (sum/any/prod). any stops at first-True; all([]) True (vacuous!). groupby groups CONSECUTIVE (sort first or defaultdict!). lru_cache: pure + hashable + bounded (fib(100) instant; cache_info proves!). operator.itemgetter beats lambdas for keys.

    A.5 Chapters 21–25 (patterns → bridge)

    Ch21 L1/L2: decorator mechanics, factories, CMs
    @timer def f ≡ f = timer(f) at def-time; wrapper: *a/**k + return fn(*a,**k) + wraps (all three, always!). Factory: config→decorator→wrapper (retry demo). Stacking: bottom-first-applied, top-first-run (order is behavior — argue from call path!). CM: enter→body→exit-always (args = outcome!); gen-CM = one yield in try/finally; truthy exit suppresses (rare!). ExitStack for dynamic N.
    Ch22 L1/L2: workflow, tracebacks, pdb, cause-fixes
    Loop: understand → bottom-up (last line FIRST!) → locate → repro-minimal (bisect!) → inspect → one-hypothesis → cause-fix → regression-test. Crash site ≠ fault site (fix the ORIGIN!). pdb: breakpoint() + n/s/c/p/w/u/b (+ post-mortem -m pdb). Symptom-fix (try around crash → silent corruption!) vs cause-fix (contract at fault + validation upstream). Every fix ships a test.
    Ch23 L1/L2: style, naming, logging, config, tests
    Ruff: check + format (on-save + CI). Names: full words, units (timeout_s!), predicates (is_valid), verb-fns, plural-collections. Logging: levels/names/lazy-%s/exception() (never print in libs!). Config: args>env>file; secrets in ENV, never git. pytest: parametrize, approx floats, tmp_path files, error-paths, purity asserts. README: 3-commands-to-green + checklist sign-off.
    Ch24 L1/L2: measure-first, Big-O crimes, GIL, memory
    Instruments: timeit (snippets), perf_counter (stages), cProfile (hotspots — top cumulative row!). Crimes: in list→set, insert(0)→deque, +=→join, re-sort→bisect, nested-same-data→index-first. GIL: threads=I/O, processes=CPU, async=fan-out, NumPy=releases-GIL. Memory: stream (O(1)), materialize-to-reuse, tracemalloc diffs leaks, slots for millions. Stop at budget!
    Ch25 L1/L2: why-Python, workflow, seeding/splits
    Five reasons: readable glue, C-libs, notebooks, ecosystem, general-purpose (+honest limits!). Workflow: ask→get→clean→explore→model→communicate→deploy (Part-1 core each stage!). Pipeline: pure clean + reject-reasons + set-dedupe + Counter-profile + seeded shuffle-split (seed LOGGED!). Series: SQL/stats → NumPy/pandas → viz → ML → DL/NLP/LLMs (each assumes THIS book!).

    A.6 Projects, interview, L3 guidance

    Project self-grading (all five)
    Grade against each project's Acceptance lines literally (P1: 512 + −40-crossover + caret errors; P2: O(1) on 50MB + BOM-clean; P3: kill-proof writes + 20 tests; P4: hand-verified rejects + byte-identical re-runs; P5: beats-baseline + verified matrix + split invariants). Bar: fresh-clone README run works; ruff+mypy clean; no tracebacks on bad input; decisions documented. Portfolio-ready = you'd show it in an interview (because you will!).
    L3 + mini-challenge approach pointers
    L3s are interview-shaped: whiteboard the design first (contracts → edges → Big-O), implement, then test-table it (3+ cases incl. error paths). Mini-challenges: build the named file to its spec, then write the required note/guideline (the WRITING is half the grade — judgment documented). Stuck >45 min? Re-read the chapter's interview Qs (they're the compressed lesson), then ask for help WITH your repro + journal (§22 discipline applies to asking too!).
    🏁 Answers summary → nextRemember: verify-then-move-on (gaps found here are cheap; in interviews they're expensive!) · L1 exactness is non-negotiable (foundations!) · L2 sketches demand your completion (doing > reading!) · L3/challenges are portfolio-adjacent (ship them!).     🏁
    Prove · capstone

    Mastery Checkpoint

    ▾

    What/why. Reading ≠ knowing. This checkpoint certifies Part-1 mastery through three gates: a knowledge exam (answer cold), a practical exam (build cold), and the capstone (ship something real). Pass all three and you don't just "know Python" — you operate in it: read, write, debug, test, and explain at a professional level. Rules: closed-book for Gate 1 (docs allowed for Gates 2–3 — professionals read docs!); no AI code-writing (AI questions OK — "why does X behave Y?" teaches; "write X" doesn't); log your hours (mastery is measured in reps!).

    C.1 Gate 1: knowledge exam (cold, 90 minutes)

    Answer all 15 canon questions (§I.1) aloud, with whiteboard demos, plus: trace [x*x if x>2 else -x for x in range(6) if x!=0] exactly (→ [-1,-2,9,16,25]); expand @retry(n=3) to its three levels; read a 3-frame traceback bottom-up (mark crash vs fault sites); state Big-O + fix for 3 planted crimes (membership/dedupe/concat). Pass: 13/15 fluent (demos run!), trace exact, traceback correct, 3/3 crimes fixed. Remediation: miss → re-read that chapter → re-take in 3 days.

    C.2 Gate 2: practical exam (cold, 3 hours, docs allowed)

    Build checkpoint_lab.py from a blank file: streaming CSV profiler (counts, Counter top-N, missing-%, reject-quarantine with reasons) + --top/--encoding/--limit flags + atomic report.txt + seeded sample-split + 10 pytest tests + ruff-clean + timed stages logged. Pass: runs on a messy 10k-row fixture (provided-by-you, 5+ corruption types!) with correct outputs, O(1) memory (tracemalloc evidence!), all tests green, zero tracebacks on hostile rows. This is P2+P4 compressed — your floor, not your ceiling.

    C.3 Gate 3: capstone (1–2 weeks, portfolio-grade)

    Ship one: (a) a novel project combining ≥3 project-patterns (e.g., log-studio: P2-text + P4-pipeline + P5-baseline-classifier on log severities!); or (b) a deep extension of P4/P5 (workers + config-file + SQLite target + full torture suite). Must include: venv + frozen requirements · pytest suite (≥25 tests, error-paths + invariants + regression-per-bug-found!) · logging + argparse config · atomic outputs · README (setup/usage/architecture/decisions/results) · ruff + mypy clean · GitHub repo with meaningful commit history (≥10 commits showing iteration!). Pass: a peer (or mentor/AI-reviewer) clones → 3 commands → green tests + correct outputs + can explain your architecture from the README alone.

    C.4 Mastery checklist (sign every line — honestly!)

    #I can… (demonstrably, not aspirationally!)Proof
    1Set up a reproducible project (venv, pins, README, git) in 10 minutesAny project repo
    2Write pure functions with hints + docstrings + tests as reflexGate 2 file
    3Predict aliasing/mutation behavior before running (Ch. 3–8 traces)Gate 1 Q1/Q8
    4Handle dirty data (quarantine + reasons, never silent, never bare-except)Gate 2 + P4
    5Stream big files at O(1) (generators, no materialization)Gate 2 evidence
    6Debug systematically (workflow + journal + regression test per bug)Bug journal
    7Write decorators + context managers from memory (timer/retry/temp_flag)Gate 1 Q4/Q5
    8State Big-O of my code and fix O(n²) crimes on sightGate 1 crimes
    9Explain any of 15 canon answers with a running demoGate 1
    10Ship: tested, logged, linted, documented, review-checklisted codeCapstone repo

    C.5 After Part 1 (the road, in order)

    ① Celebrate (you earned it — this was a real textbook!). ② Rest 2–3 days (consolidation is neurological, not laziness). ③ Part 2: SQL + Statistics (query the data you've learned to wrangle!). ④ Keep the habits: venvs, seeds, tests, journals, READMEs — every Part, every project. ⑤ Teach someone Ch. 3 or Ch. 21 (teaching is the final mastery test — gaps you can hide from yourself, you can't hide from a student!). ⑥ Return to this book as a reference (that's why it's one searchable file — Part-1-first debugging, forever!).
